The methodology intentionally prioritized conservative, explainable analytical methods over aggressive

## 2.1 Timeline & Scope

Run on Less – Messy Middle was conducted over 18 days in September 2025. Thirteen fleets operating 14 Class
8 tractors representing four powertrain technologies — diesel/renewable diesel/biodiesel, CNG/RNG, BEV, and
FCEV — participated in the study. Most vehicles were production units, though several platforms — including
the Tesla Semi, Windrose Technologies sleeper, and Hyundai XCIENT — were in limited production or pre-production stages at the time of the Run. All trucks operated in active revenue freight service on real customer
routes, consistent with NACFE's principle that Run on Less documents real-world operational performance.

Collectively, the demonstration logged more than 73,000 validated miles across the four powertrain categories.
Fleet selection emphasized diversity in geography, payload characteristics, operational archetype, and
technology readiness to ensure findings remain broadly representative of North American freight operations
while documenting what leading-edge operators can achieve with current technology. See Figure 3.

> **Figure 3 — Scope of Run on Less – Messy Middle**

> Axes: none
> Series: none

> Notes: Logo "RUN ON LESS MESSY MIDDLE by NACFE", September 2025 • Production Vehicles • 10-Second Telemetry Intervals

## 2.2 Instrumentation & Data Sources

Trucks in the Run were instrumented with telematics devices, which were used to collect a variety of
information about how each vehicle was performing. Data also was collected from other sources in order to get

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 39

---

<!-- page: 40 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

a fuller picture of vehicle performance. NACFE’s goal was to ensure consistent data collection occurred across all
participating fleets.

### 2.2.1 Telematics Infrastructure

Each participating tractor was equipped with a Geotab telematics system providing continuous duty-cycle data.
Geotab's curve-based logging algorithm captures data at variable intervals — from seconds to minutes — based
on vehicle activity and parameter changes. For analytical consistency, this data was resampled to standardized
10-second intervals, producing approximately 155,520 normalized data points per vehicle over the
demonstration period. This approach enables granular analysis of operational patterns, efficiency variations, and
environmental factors that influence real-world performance. Where available, supplemental OEM data feeds
provided additional vehicle-specific parameters.

Key parameters recorded include the following.
* Distance traveled, driving time, and idle time
* Vehicle speed and operational status (driving, idle, charging/fueling)
* Energy or fuel consumption by type (diesel, CNG/RNG, electricity, hydrogen)
* GPS coordinates enabling route reconstruction and terrain analysis
* Elevation data from multiple sources (onboard sensors, digital elevation models)
* Battery state of charge (BEV) or fuel tank levels (diesel, CNG, hydrogen)
* Payload indicators where available from onboard systems or dispatch records
* Environmental conditions

### 2.2.2 GPS Elevation Integration

Terrain analysis is central to understanding powertrain performance, particularly for technologies sensitive to
grade — BEVs, in particular, show significant efficiency variation with elevation change. Run on Less – Messy
Middle methodology integrated GPS-based elevation data with digital elevation model (DEM) cross-references
to produce validated elevation profiles for each route. This integration addresses known limitations of GPS-only
elevation data while enabling grade-band analysis across all fleets.

### 2.2.3 Operational Data Sources

The Geotab system also captured comprehensive operational data, including charging events, fueling
transactions, fuel levels, and precise vehicle location throughout the study period. This automated capture
eliminated reliance on manual reporting while ensuring consistent data collection across all participating fleets.
Some fleets also provided weight and load information, enabling payload-adjusted efficiency analysis where
available.

## 2.3 Data Standardization

Cross-fleet comparison requires consistent data segmentation and unit normalization. The methodology used in
Run on Less – Messy Middle applied several standardization protocols as outlined below.
* **Time Segmentation:** All daily metrics use midnight Eastern Standard Time (EST) day bins for consistent
time segmentation across fleets operating in different time zones. This approach simplified aggregation
of results for cross-fleet analysis.

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 40

---

<!-- page: 41 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

* **Distance Calculation:** GPS-calculated distance served as the primary distance source for all fleets,
derived from validated coordinate data and speed-time integration. This approach provided consistent,
cross-validated measurements across all telematics systems. The exception was the second Saia truck
(Saia 2), where odometer-based distance was used as the primary source. GPS-calculated distance
typically aligned within 1% to 3% of odometer readings, providing confidence in cross-fleet
comparability while avoiding anomalies — jumps, resets, or implausible values — that can compromise
odometer-based metrics.
* **Energy Unit Normalization:** Energy consumption across all four powertrain types was normalized to
enable meaningful comparison. The primary normalization approach converted all energy inputs to
diesel gallon equivalent (DGE) based on energy content, with technology-specific metrics retained for
detailed analysis. Hydrogen consumption is reported both in kilograms (the standard industry unit) and
in miles per kilogram, with DGE equivalence calculated using standard energy content values (1 kg H₂ ≈
1.0 DGE based on lower heating value) [7]. For BEVs, net energy consumption accounts for charging
losses and regenerative braking recovery, providing a true measure of grid-to-wheel efficiency rather
than battery-to-wheel figures that understate actual energy requirements.

## 2.4 Validation & Data Quality

Following procedures refined in Run on Less Regional and subsequent demonstrations, each dataset underwent
a multi-stage validation process to ensure accuracy and completeness [3].

1. Automated screening for missing values, anomalies, timestamp gaps, and statistical outliers.
2. Cross-validation between telematics calculations and fleet-provided dashboard data, with discrepancies
investigated and resolved.
3. Route validation through GPS coordinate analysis, including path continuity and geographic plausibility
checks.
4. Energy reconciliation comparing calculated consumption against charging records (BEV), fueling records
(diesel, CNG), or hydrogen dispensing logs (FCEV).
5. Payload verification through vehicle-reported weight estimates (derived from powertrain controller
calculations), dispatch documentation, or validated average gross-vehicle-weight ranges from historical
fleet data.

The validation process achieved alignment within 1% to 5% between calculated metrics and fleet dashboard
baselines across most fleets and days. Where larger discrepancies persisted, the analytical approach that
matched validated reference data was adopted, ensuring that reported metrics reflect actual operational
performance rather than calculation artifacts.

### 2.4.1 Technology-Specific Validation Considerations

Different powertrain technologies present distinct data collection and validation challenges. Figure 4 illustrates
the technology-specific considerations shaped the analytical approach for each vehicle type.

> **Figure 4 — Data collection and validation process**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: DATA SOURCES: Telematics (10-Second Intervals), GPS & Elevation Data, Fleet Reports & Interviews; INTEGRATION: Data Integration & Screening; VALIDATION: Automated Anomaly Detection, Cross-Validation vs. Dashboard, Energy Reconciliaton; OUTPUT: Validated Dataset (1% to 5% alignment)
> Notes: Logo "RUN ON LESS MESSY MIDDLE by NACFE", Technology-specific validation: $H_2$ cumulative totals, BEV energy components, payload sensors

Figure 4: Data collection and validation process

* **Hydrogen fuel cell vehicles** required special handling because onboard energy fields represent
cumulative totals rather than incremental consumption. The analytical methodology applied differential
calculations with reset detection to convert cumulative values to trip-level consumption. Usable
hydrogen mass of 68.6 kg was based on Hyundai-provided tank capacity specifications rather than
nominal published figures.
* **Battery-electric vehicles** presented challenges in distinguishing driving energy, charging energy,
regenerative recovery, and idle consumption. The methodology tracked each energy component
separately, enabling analysis of driving efficiency (battery-to-wheel). Charging losses were not directly
measured, as this would require charger-side energy data not available from vehicle telematics.
Reported BEV efficiency metrics reflect battery-to-wheel performance.
* **Payload data** remains an area where data quality varies significantly across fleets. Vehicle-reported
weight estimates derived from powertrain controller calculations were available for some trucks;
otherwise, dispatch records or validated average weights were applied. These algorithm-based weight
estimates showed inconsistent accuracy and were used as supplementary references rather than
primary data sources.

## 2.5 Fleet Interviews & Operational Context

Structured interviews with drivers, fleet managers, and maintenance personnel provided essential context for
interpreting telematics data. Quantitative metrics alone cannot explain why a particular day showed exceptional

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

or poor performance. Operational context — a routing change, weather event, customer delay, or equipment
issue — often provides the explanation.

Discussions focused on:
* Route predictability and duty-cycle consistency
* Charging or fueling logistics and dwell-time impacts
* Vehicle reliability, maintenance intervals, and inspection programs
* Human-factor influences including driver training, incentive systems, and operational discipline
* Infrastructure experiences — charging availability, fueling logistics, and downtime impacts
* Technology-specific observations and lessons learned

These qualitative insights are integrated throughout the fleet-level analyses in Section 5, providing the
operational narrative that explains what the numbers mean in practice.

Core performance indicators were calculated consistently across all fleets and energy pathways. The metrics in

## 2.7 Analytical Framework

NACFE established an analytical framework that included a speed-band analysis, a grade-band analysis, PMR
methodology and a regression analysis.
* **Speed-Band Analysis:** Efficiency varies significantly with operating speed. This methodology segments
performance into standardized speed bands — typically 0 to 30 mph (urban/congestion), 30 to 50 mph
(arterial/suburban), 50 to 60 mph (moderate highway), and 60+ mph (high-speed highway). This enabled
comparison of how different powertrains respond to different operating regimes. This analysis reveals,
for example, that diesel efficiency remains remarkably stable across speed bands while BEV efficiency
shows much greater variation.
* **Grade-Band Analysis:** Terrain impact was captured through grade-band segmentation, categorizing road
segments by grade percentage (typically ≤−3%, −3% to −1%, −1% to +1%, +1% to +3%, and ≥+3%). This
structure enabled quantification of how different powertrains responded to climbing versus descending
grades, revealing technology-specific sensitivities. Grade-band analysis proved particularly important for
understanding BEV performance, where regenerative braking on descents partially offsets climbing
energy but with significant net efficiency variation between flat and mountainous terrain.
* **PMR Methodology:** Probable Maximum Range estimates provide a standardized approach to range
comparison across technologies. PMR is calculated from observed efficiency data rather than
manufacturer specifications, using the following formula.

$$\text{PMR} = \text{Usable Energy Capacity} \times \text{Observed Efficiency}$$

For each fleet, PMR is reported as a range reflecting best-day and worst-day efficiency, providing
realistic bounds for operational planning. This approach acknowledges that range varies significantly
with terrain, payload, speed, and conditions — a single "rated range" figure would misrepresent
operational reality.
* **Regression Analysis:** After validation, data were aggregated into normalized time and distance intervals
to compute average and peak performance values. Regression analyses were performed to isolate the
effects of payload, terrain, speed, and environmental conditions, extending NACFE's established
modeling framework. This analysis identified which operational factors have the greatest explanatory
power for efficiency variation, supporting fleet planning decisions.

## 2.8 Study Limitations & Scope

The objective of this demonstration was not to rank or compare fleets competitively but to quantify how
external and operational factors influence energy performance across the four major powertrain pathways.
Several limitations should be considered when interpreting results.

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 44

---

<!-- page: 45 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

* **Sample size:** Thirteen fleets operating 14 tractors over 18 days provide meaningful operational data but
do not constitute statistical industry representation. Results illustrate achievable performance under
current market conditions rather than industry averages.
* **Self-selection:** Fleet participation was voluntary and intentionally focused on operators actively
deploying advanced efficiency technologies. These fleets represent leading-edge practice rather than
typical operations.
* **Seasonal timing:** The September demonstration period reflects moderate weather conditions. Winter
performance — particularly for BEVs affected by battery thermal management — would likely differ.
* **Payload data limitations:** Direct weight measurements were not consistently available across all fleets.
Algorithm-based weight estimation from vehicle data remains problematic, and future studies would
benefit from improved weight data collection systems such as onboard trailer sensors or integration
with weigh-in-motion roadside systems.
* **Infrastructure variability:** Charging and hydrogen infrastructure availability varies significantly by region
and continues to evolve. Results reflect infrastructure conditions during the demonstration period.

Despite these limitations, the diversity of regions, payloads, and duty-cycle archetypes ensures findings remain
broadly representative of North American freight operations and provide actionable insights for fleet planning
decisions.

## 2.9 Methodology & Data Analytics Summary

The methodology applied in Run on Less – Messy Middle combines high-resolution telematics capture with
rigorous validation and qualitative operational context. By standardizing data collection, applying consistent
analytical frameworks, and validating results against fleet reference data, this approach produces operational
metrics that fleets, policymakers, and manufacturers can use with confidence.

## 3.1 4Gen Logistics

4Gen Logistics, established in 1943, operates approximately 83 trucks primarily serving Southern California port
drayage markets. The company has deployed Volvo VNR Electric trucks (Figure 8) in demanding two-shift
drayage operations.

## 3.15 Fleet Summary

The fleets participating in Run on Less – Messy Middle represent a wide cross-section of North American freight
operations, spanning long-haul truckload, regional grocery supply, LTL long-haul, drayage, bulk distribution, and
hydrogen delivery. Their combined profiles demonstrate the diversity of duty cycles, freight types, fleet

These organizational profiles establish the operational context for the performance results presented in Sections
5 and 6 and provide a baseline for understanding powertrain suitability across freight segments.

# 4 DUTY CYCLES & OPERATIONAL ARCHETYPES

Duty-cycle classification is the foundation of the analytical structure used in Run on Less – Messy Middle.
Understanding how fleets operate — daily miles, terrain, utilization, route structure, stop density, and fueling or
charging opportunities — is essential for interpreting powertrain performance across the 13 participating fleets.

This section defines the standardized duty-cycle categories and operational archetypes used in Sections 5 and 6,
drawing upon NACFE's prior Runs telematics-based efficiency research, and NACFE’s Executive Director Mike

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 61

---

<!-- page: 62 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

Roeth's industry segmentation work [2] [3] [4] [5]. The framework enables meaningful cross-fleet comparisons
and clarifies why different energy pathways fit different freight applications.

## 4.1 How Duty Cycle Shapes Powertrain Choice

Duty cycle has a direct influence on technology suitability. Energy consumption, uptime requirements, and
operational feasibility vary significantly across freight segments.
* **Long-haul operations** benefit from powertrains with high energy density and fast refueling capability.
These operations require 500 to 800+ mile daily productivity to fully utilize single-driver and team hours-of-service windows.
* **Return-to-base regional operations** align with daily charging or on-site fueling, enabling infrastructure
investment at controlled locations rather than depending on public networks.
* **Drayage and short-haul shuttle operations** are strongly compatible with BEVs due to short, repeatable
corridors, consistent return-to-base behavior, and manageable daily mileage within current battery
capacities.
* **Bulk and tanker operations** are sensitive to payload impacts, making vehicle weight an important factor.
Heavy-haul applications may require high-GCW capability that not all alternative powertrains currently
deliver.
* **LTL networks** contain multiple duty cycles (pickup and delivery plus linehaul), requiring flexibility across
operational modes and potentially supporting different technologies for different network segments.

## 4.2 Purpose of Duty-Cycle Classification

Duty-cycle classification enables consistent interpretation of fleet performance by identifying the operational
factors that most influence energy efficiency and daily productivity. The classification system used in this report
considers the following.
* **Daily mileage and utilization patterns:** From 100-mile urban routes to 800+ mile transcontinental
operations
* **Speed distribution patterns:** Elevation gain/loss per 100 miles, grade severity, and topographic
consistency
* **Stop density and dwell behavior:** Frequent delivery stops versus point-to-point linehaul
* **Return-to-base vs. over-the-road structure:** Predictable daily cycles versus multi-day routing
* **Fueling or charging opportunities:** Infrastructure availability along routes and at terminals
* **Payload variability:** Weight-sensitive versus volume-limited freight characteristics
* **Driver hours-of-service capacity:** Single-driver vs. team operations

Run on Less – Messy Middle data confirmed that terrain and duty-cycle structure are the strongest predictors of
efficiency variation, particularly for battery-electric vehicles where efficiency swings of 50% to 70% were
observed across different operating conditions.

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 62

---

<!-- page: 63 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

## 4.3 Core Duty-Cycle Definitions

The defining characteristic of over the road (OTR) versus return to base (RTB) is whether the driver and vehicle
return to a home terminal within a single shift or HOS cycle. RTB operations (including long RTB/linehaul) allow
depot-based infrastructure investment; OTR operations require corridor or public infrastructure independence.

### 4.3.1 Long-Haul OTR

Long-haul OTR features the following characteristics.
* Multi-day or continuous operations spanning multiple regions
* 500 to 800+ miles per day typical; validated maximum exceeded 1,100 miles
* Variable terrain and climate across operating territory
* Fueled at public corridors; infrastructure independence essential
* High sustained highway speeds (60+ mph dominant)

Albert Transport (diesel), MVT (diesel), and JoyRide (BEV long-haul) represented this duty cycle in Run on Less –
Messy Middle.

### 4.3.2 Regional Linehaul

Regional linehaul represents a form of "long return-to-base" operation as defined in NACFE's 2025 white paper,
*A Need to Redefine Class 8 Long-Haul Trucking* [9]. While daily mileage may approach traditional long-haul levels
(250 to 500 miles), the structured terminal-to-terminal nature and relay driver models enable depot-based
infrastructure strategies more typical of RTB operations.
* 250 to 500 miles per day in structured terminal-to-terminal operations
* Relay networks with predictable route structures
* High sustained speeds on Interstate corridors
* Compatible with depot-based fueling or charging strategies

Saia (BEV), UPS Salt Lake (CNG triples), and Schneider (diesel mountain corridors) represented this duty cycle.

### 4.3.3 Regional RTB

Regional RTB is characterized by the following conditions.
* Single-day cycles returning to home terminal each shift
* 100 to 350 miles/day depending on route density
* Predictable schedules enabling overnight fueling/charging
* Strong alignment with depot-based infrastructure investment

Wegmans (CNG), Frito-Lay (diesel), Penske (hydrogen RTB), Pilot (hydrogen regional), and Nevoya (BEV regional)
represented this duty cycle.

### 4.3.4 Urban/Metro P&D

Urban/Metro P&D is characterized by the following conditions.
* Stop-dense operations with frequent loading/unloading
* Low-speed routing through urban environments
* High idle percentage affecting energy efficiency
* Daily mileage typically 50 to 150 miles

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY

---

<!-- page: 64 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

Saia P&D operations (BEV) represented this duty cycle. Though the vehicles were not tracked for the scope of
this Run, UPS P&D (CNG) vehicles represented this duty cycle as well.

### 4.3.5 Drayage/Port Shuttles

Drayage/Port Shuttles is characterized by the following conditions.
* High utilization with repeatable short-haul corridors
* Strong RTB patterns enabling depot charging
* 100 to 250 miles per day typically
* Proven BEV application due to predictable routes and manageable daily range

4Gen (BEV) represented this duty cycle.

### 4.3.6 Load Type Qualifiers

An additional qualifier for each of these duty cycles is bulk load (BL), truckload (TL), or less-than-truckload (LTL),
which has ramifications for freight density carried and whether trucks weigh out or cube out in carrying freight.
* **Bulk Load (BL):** Weight-limited operations where payload mass constrains capacity. Vehicle tare weight
directly impacts freight economics. Kleysen heavy-haul tanker operations (CNG) represent this duty
cycle.
* **Truckload (TL):** Single-shipper loads that may be weight- or volume-limited depending on commodity.
Albert Transport (Diesel), MVT transcontinental freight (Diesel) represent this duty cycle.
* **Less-Than-Truckload (LTL):** Multi-stop consolidated freight typically volume-limited. Higher stop density
creates different efficiency patterns. Saia linehaul and P&D operations (BEV) represent this duty cycle.

## 4.4 Industry Segmentation Framework

NACFE's freight segmentation framework categorizes operations by payload sensitivity, providing additional
context for interpreting powertrain suitability.
* **Category A – High Payload Sensitivity (Weight-Limited):** Operations like Kleysen where vehicle tare
weight directly impacts revenue freight capacity. Every pound of vehicle weight reduces payload
capability. These applications are most sensitive to the weight penalties of large battery packs and may
favor hydrogen (lighter energy storage) or optimized diesel/CNG configurations.
* **Category B – Mixed Freight/General Truckload:** Operations like Albert Transport, MVT, Schneider, and
JoyRide carrying varied freight that may be weight- or volume-limited depending on load characteristics.
These applications have moderate payload sensitivity and can accommodate some tare weight increase
if operational benefits justify the trade-off.
* **Category C – Volume-Limited/RTB-Oriented Freight:** Operations like Wegmans, Frito-Lay, 4Gen, Nevoya
and Saia where freight density is low enough that trailer volume constrains capacity before weight limits
are reached. These applications can absorb battery weight penalties without sacrificing revenue freight,
making them strong candidates for BEV deployment.

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY

<!-- page: 65 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

## 4.5 Operational Archetypes

The operational archetypes below synthesize duty-cycle definitions with powertrain relevance, providing a
framework for matching technology to application.

**Archetype 1 — Long-Haul Sleeper/Team:** Albert Transport, MVT, and JoyRide are fleets in this archetype.
Prioritizes high energy density and fast refueling. Infrastructure independence is essential. Currently best served
by diesel; BEVs are viable only on specific corridors with strategic charging.

**Archetype 2 — Regional Linehaul (Long RTB):** Saia (BEV), UPS Salt Lake (CNG), and Schneider (renewable diesel)
are fleets in this archetype. Emerging BEV and hydrogen deployment space is suited for this archetype.
Structured routes enable infrastructure planning. Daily mileage requirements (250 to 500 miles) are achievable
with current BEV technology on favorable terrain.

**Archetype 3 — Regional RTB Distribution:** Wegmans, Frito-Lay, Penske, Pilot, and Nevoya are fleets in this
archetype. This aligns well with CNG/RNG (established infrastructure), BEV (depot charging), and hydrogen RTB
(where stations exist). Predictable daily patterns enable infrastructure investment.

**Archetype 4 — Port Drayage/Short-Haul Shuttle:** 4Gen represents this archetype. BEVs have already proven to
be effective. Short, repeatable corridors with depot charging create ideal operating conditions. Strong
regulatory drivers in port regions accelerate adoption.

**Archetype 5 — Mixed Regional/Urban LTL:** Saia and UPS represent this archetype. Supports a blend of
technologies. Linehaul segments may use different powertrains than P&D operations within the same network.
Operational flexibility essential.

powertrain technology.

Figure 22: Fleet classification by operational archetype

The duty-cycle framework established in this section directly influences how each powertrain technology
performs. Run on Less – Messy Middle data confirmed that duty cycle determines several critical operational
factors.
* **Energy consumption patterns:** BEV efficiency varied 50% to 70% across terrain and speed profiles;
diesel showed only 30% variation across extreme conditions.
* **Infrastructure requirements:** Long-haul operations require corridor infrastructure; RTB operations
enable depot-focused investment.
* **Charging/fueling strategy:** Overnight depot charging suits RTB operations; mid-route fast charging
enables extended BEV range.
* **Payload sensitivity:** Weight-limited applications face different trade-offs than volume-limited freight.

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

* **Speed band behavior:** CNG/RNG showed efficiency penalties at low speeds; diesel maintained stable
efficiency across speed bands.
* **Route predictability:** Predictable routes enable infrastructure planning; variable routing requires
infrastructure independence.
* **Driver hours-of-service capacity:** Daily mileage capability must align with HOS windows for single-driver
or team operations.
* **Economic implications of mileage intensity:** Higher daily mileage operations offer a structural
advantage for alternative powertrain economics. The elevated upfront capital costs of BEV, hydrogen,
and CNG tractors — often $100,000 to $300,000 more than equivalent diesel — require more miles over
which to spread the investment and capture operating cost savings. A truck running 500 miles per day
accumulates twice the mileage of one running 250 miles per day, accelerating the payback timeline
proportionally. This makes longer regional RTB routes and high utilization linehaul operations
particularly attractive for alternative powertrain deployment, even before considering fuel cost
differentials or incentive structures.

The cross-fleet analysis in Section 5 demonstrates these relationships through validated operational data across
all four powertrain technologies.

## 4.7 Terrain as a Duty-Cycle Dimension

Terrain emerged as one of the strongest predictors of efficiency variation in Run on Less – Messy Middle,
warranting explicit consideration in duty-cycle classification. See Figure 23.
* **Flat terrain (< 500 ft. elevation change per 100 miles):** This enables the highest BEV efficiency and
range. Albert Transport achieved 11.8 MPG on flat Midwestern corridors; Saia's Tesla Semi achieved 1.2
to 1.4 kWh/mi on flat Northern California routes.
* **Rolling terrain (500 to 1,500 ft. elevation change per 100 miles):** This had moderate efficiency impacts
across all technologies. Regenerative braking partially offsets BEV climb penalties.
* **Mountain terrain (> 1,500 ft. elevation change per 100 miles):** There are significant efficiency penalties,
especially for BEVs. Schneider's diesel achieved 9.0 MPG through western mountain passes with roughly
21% of miles on grades exceeding 3%.

For BEV deployment planning, terrain analysis should precede any commitment. The range implications of
terrain are far more significant for electric powertrains than for diesel or CNG.

## 4.8 Duty Cycles & Operational Archetypes Summary

This section establishes the duty-cycle framework used to interpret fleet-level results (Section 5) and cross-fleet
analytics (Section 6). The classification system ensures that operational context — daily mileage, terrain, route
structure, payload characteristics, and infrastructure access — is consistently incorporated into all performance
evaluations.

Key takeaways for technology selection decisions include the following.
* **Match powertrain to duty cycle:** Each technology has an operational envelope where it excels; forcing a
mismatch creates operational friction and suboptimal economics.
* **Consider terrain sensitivity:** BEV efficiency and range vary dramatically with terrain; diesel and CNG
show greater resilience to elevation changes.
* **Evaluate infrastructure alignment:** RTB operations enable depot-focused infrastructure; long-haul
requires corridor access or infrastructure independence.
* **Assess payload trade-offs:** Weight-limited applications (Category A) face different constraints than
volume-limited freight (Category C).
* **Plan for daily productivity requirements:** Technology must deliver required daily miles within HOS
constraints, not just energy efficiency.

## 4.9 Reflections for the Broader Industry

The 13 fleets operating 14 Class 8 tractors in Run on Less – Messy Middle represent a strategically selected
sample of the broader North American trucking industry. Understanding how these deployments relate to the
overall market provides essential context for interpreting results and assessing generalizability.

### 4.9.1 The Class 8 Tractor Landscape

Approximately 1.7 million Class 8 heavy-duty tractors operate in the US. This figure represents highway tractors
— the trucks pulling trailers in freight operations — and excludes vocational Class 8 vehicles such as dump
trucks, cement mixers, and refuse trucks. According to ACT Research, highway tractors account for
approximately 65% of heavy-duty truck production, with vocational vehicles comprising the remaining 35% [10].

Within the highway tractor segment, production historically splits between approximately 60% sleeper tractors
and 40% day cabs. A separate ACT Research datapoint reported by *Commercial Carrier Journal* (including severe
service) found an overall split of 46% sleepers/54% day cabs for all OEMs (2016), suggesting the mix can vary
depending on scope and definitions [16].

Annual new Class 8 tractor sales typically range from 180,000 to 220,000 units, depending on economic
conditions and freight demand. With a fleet of approximately 1.7 million tractors, natural fleet turnover runs
approximately 11% to 13% per year — though actual replacement rates vary based on trade cycle decisions,
economic conditions, and equipment availability.

### 4.9.2 Mapping Duty Cycles to the National Fleet

Based on NACFE's segmentation research, ACT Research production data, and industry analysis, the estimated
distribution of the 1.7 million Class 8 tractors across duty-cycle categories is shown in Figure 24.

**Note:** Sleeper tractors account for approximately 60% of tractor production, but not all sleepers operate in
traditional OTR duty cycles. A significant portion of sleeper-equipped tractors are deployed in regional linehaul
and long return-to-base operations, where fleets purchase sleeper configurations for operational flexibility even
when daily routes allow drivers to return home. Many fleets buy sleepers “in case they need them” for
occasional OTR assignments. The share estimates in Figure 24 reflect estimated operational duty cycles rather
than equipment configuration. Estimates are based on NACFE segmentation research and ACT Research
production data. Actual distribution varies by region and economic conditions.

The Run on Less – Messy Middle sample intentionally over-represents regional RTB and drayage operations
relative to their share of the national fleet. This reflects the study's focus on evaluating alternative powertrain
technologies, which are currently most viable in return-to-base applications where depot-based fueling or
charging infrastructure can be deployed. The three long-haul tractors — including JoyRide's BEV — provide
insights into the dominant segment of the national fleet while testing the boundaries of alternative powertrain
applicability.

### 4.9.3 Technology Penetration Context

Of the approximately 1.7 million Class 8 tractors in operation, the overwhelming majority — well over 99% —
are powered by diesel engines. Alternative powertrains remain at early-adoption scale.
* **Diesel and renewable diesel** dominate the market with ubiquitous fueling infrastructure and
established OEM offerings from all manufacturers.
* **Natural gas (CNG/LNG)** has an established presence in specific applications. Class 8 natural gas tractor
registrations surged nearly 50% year-over-year to approximately 2,300 units in 2024, driven by the
introduction of the Cummins X15N 15-liter engine [8]. Total CNG/LNG tractors in service likely number in
the tens of thousands.
* **Battery-electric tractors** remain a small fraction of the fleet. More than 15,000 medium- and heavy-duty
electric vehicles were deployed across the US in 2024, with Class 8 tractors representing a subset

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 70

---

<!-- page: 71 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

concentrated in California and specific regional applications [17] [18].
* **Hydrogen fuel cell tractors** remain at demonstration scale, with fewer than 500 Class 8 hydrogen
tractors operating in North America as of late 2024 [19] [20]. The bankruptcy of Nikola Corporation in
February 2025 highlighted the challenges facing hydrogen truck commercialization, with the company
producing approximately 600 trucks before filing for Chapter 11 [21].

### 4.9.4 Estimated Technology Adoption Ceilings During the Messy Middle

Based on infrastructure constraints, fuel availability, and OEM production capacity, NACFE estimates that
alternative powertrain adoption during the Messy Middle (through approximately 2035) may plateau at
technology-specific ceilings well below 100% penetration.
* **Renewable diesel and biodiesel**, while compatible with existing diesel infrastructure, may reach 10% to
15% of diesel consumption because of feedstock constraints and competing demand from aviation and
other sectors.
* **CNG/RNG adoption** may approach 10% to 12% of suitable duty cycles, limited by station development
economics and the concentration of RNG supply. The Cummins X15N engine expands addressable
applications but infrastructure remains a constraint.
* **Battery-electric truck deployment rates** depend heavily on infrastructure buildout and will remain
concentrated in RTB and regional applications where depot-based charging is viable. Public charging
network expansion could extend this ceiling over time.
* **Hydrogen fuel cell** near-term adoption will remain limited to specific corridors with dedicated fueling
arrangements until infrastructure matures significantly.

These are not ceiling predictions for ultimate technology penetration but rather estimates of what the industry
may achieve before infrastructure and technology maturation enable broader adoption.

The Run on Less – Messy Middle deployments therefore represent the leading edge of industry practice. While
the four diesel/renewable diesel/biodiesel tractors reflect mainstream operations, the BEV, CNG, and hydrogen
deployments represent technology still in early commercial phases. Results should be interpreted with this
context — these are indicators of what is possible and what challenges exist, not necessarily what typical fleet
operations would experience today.

### 4.9.5 Fleet Composition & Diversity

The trucking industry exhibits remarkable fragmentation. Of the approximately 928,000 for-hire carriers
registered with the Federal Motor Carrier Safety Administration (FMCSA), roughly 70% operate just one power
unit, and 97% have 10 or fewer trucks [11]. Small fleets (10 trucks or fewer) represent approximately half of all
Class 8 vehicles in service but account for only about 22% of new truck registrations, indicating these operators
primarily acquire used equipment.

Run on Less – Messy Middle participants represent a different cross-section — primarily medium to large fleets
with the operational scale and financial capacity to evaluate emerging technologies. These early-mover fleets
are not statistically representative of the broader industry but are indicative of where alternative powertrain

## 4.10 Considerations for Truck Acquisitions

Fleet operators evaluating powertrain alternatives face decisions that will shape their operations for years. The
findings from Run on Less – Messy Middle inform but do not prescribe these decisions. This section outlines key
considerations grounded in observable facts rather than speculative projections.

### 4.10.1 Replacement Cycles & Decision Timing

Class 8 tractors typically operate on replacement cycles of five to seven years, with variation based on fleet
ownership structure. Fleets that lease 90% or more of their equipment report average trade cycles of
approximately 5.3 years and 513,000 miles [22]. Fleets that own 90% or more of their equipment report longer
cycles — approximately 7.2 years and 736,000 miles [22]. Private fleets surveyed for the 2025 National Private
Truck Council (NPTC) benchmarking report showed accelerated replacement mileage of approximately 568,000
miles — the lowest recorded — reflecting strategies to cycle equipment before major maintenance
requirements [23] [24].

These replacement cycles create periodic decision windows when fleets evaluate their next equipment
acquisition. With approximately 180,000 to 220,000 new Class 8 tractors sold annually into a national fleet of
roughly 1.7 million units, the natural fleet turnover rate is approximately 11% to 13% per year. This means that
decisions made today affect vehicles that will remain in service through the late 2020s or early 2030s.

### 4.10.2 Infrastructure Availability by Powertrain

Infrastructure availability varies dramatically across powertrain technologies, directly affecting operational
flexibility and deployment viability.

**Diesel, Renewable Diesel and Biodiesel:** Diesel is effectively ubiquitous. Tens of thousands of truck stops, travel
centers, and fueling stations provide nationwide coverage. Renewable diesel (R99/R100) can use existing diesel
infrastructure without modification. Biodiesel also benefits from extensive existing logistics: 99%/100% biodiesel
(B99/B100) is already trucked to and stored in underground tanks at most major truck stops, where on-site
blending produces the B5 and B20 blends sold at the pump. This established distribution network means high-blend biodiesel deployment requires supply-chain coordination rather than new infrastructure construction.
Fleets operating diesel face no infrastructure constraints.

**CNG/RNG:** More than 700 public CNG stations operate in the US, with more than 1,200 total stations including
private facilities [7]. Station concentration is highest in California, Texas, and along major freight corridors. The
extensive US natural gas pipeline network (approximately 3 million miles) facilitates station development where
demand exists. Fleets can install private time-fill stations at terminals for overnight fueling, reducing
dependence on public infrastructure.

**BEV Charging:** Heavy-duty electric charging infrastructure is nascent but expanding rapidly, particularly in
California. Major investments are underway through programs like the Department of Energy's (DOE)
SuperTruck Charge initiative ($68 million allocated in early 2025) and private ventures like Greenlane, WattEV,
and Terawatt Infrastructure. Megawatt Charging System (MCS) technology, capable of adding 190 to 250 miles
of range in 30 to 45 minutes, is beginning commercial deployment. However, public charging sites specifically

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 72

---

<!-- page: 73 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

designed for Class 8 trucks remain concentrated in California and along limited Western corridors. Depot-based
charging is more practical for RTB operations, where fleets control the infrastructure.

**Hydrogen Fueling:** As of 2024, approximately 54 retail hydrogen stations operated in the US, with the vast
majority located in California [25]. Heavy-duty hydrogen fueling infrastructure remains extremely limited, with
only a handful of stations capable of serving Class 8 trucks. Air Products announced plans to build commercial-scale hydrogen stations capable of fueling up to 200 heavy-duty trucks per day, but deployment timelines
remain uncertain [26]. Hydrogen infrastructure constraints represent the most significant barrier to FCEV
deployment.

### 4.10.3 OEM Offerings by Powertrain

The availability of Class 8 tractors varies substantially by powertrain type.

**Diesel:** All seven major North American truck brands offer comprehensive diesel lineups covering all duty cycles.
Multiple engine options, configurations, and specifications are available for immediate order. This remains the
baseline against which all alternatives are compared.

**Natural Gas:** The introduction of the Cummins X15N 15-liter natural gas engine in late 2024 significantly
expanded OEM offerings. Kenworth (T680, T880), Freightliner (Cascadia), and Peterbilt (579) now offer natural
gas configurations with power and torque ratings competitive with diesel (up to 500 hp and 1,850 lb.-ft.). This
engine represents the first big-bore natural gas powertrain capable of true long-haul applications, tripling the
addressable market for natural gas trucks.

**Battery-Electric:** Several OEMs offer Class 8 BEV tractors — Freightliner eCascadia, Volvo VNR Electric, Peterbilt
579EV, Kenworth T680E, and Mack LR Electric. Tesla Semi entered limited production for select customers.
Windrose has announced that the model of its truck that operated in the Run is their production-intern model
although the company has yet to announce a US manufacturing plant. Range specifications typically fall between
150 to 300 miles depending on configuration, with some models claiming up to 500 miles under specific
conditions. BEV offerings are concentrated in day cab configurations suitable for regional and drayage
applications.

**Hydrogen Fuel Cell:** Class 8 hydrogen truck availability remains extremely limited. Kenworth and Toyota have
partnered on fuel cell truck development, with production expected to increase. Hyundai operates
demonstration vehicles. However, the bankruptcy of Nikola Motors and financial difficulties at Hyzon Motors
illustrate the commercial challenges facing hydrogen truck manufacturers. No major OEM currently offers
hydrogen Class 8 tractors for general purchase.

### 4.10.4 Key Acquisition Considerations

Based on Run on Less – Messy Middle findings and industry conditions, fleets evaluating future acquisitions
should consider the following. See Figure 25.
* **Duty-cycle match:** No single powertrain is optimal for all applications. The data consistently showed that
technology selection must align with operational requirements — daily mileage, terrain, route
predictability, payload sensitivity, and infrastructure access.

* **TCO uncertainty:** While TCO models suggest potential benefits for alternative powertrains under certain

these vehicles today should anticipate ongoing learning, potential software and hardware updates, and
evolving operational practices.

mandates, and incentive programs remain in flux. The withdrawal of California's Advanced Clean Fleets
waiver application and ongoing EPA regulatory uncertainty illustrate policy volatility that affects long-term planning.

* **Residual value uncertainty:** Used-vehicle markets for alternative powertrain Class 8 trucks are not yet
established. Fleets should factor in uncertainty about resale values when evaluating acquisition costs.
* **Maintenance and service networks:** Diesel trucks benefit from established dealer networks and
technician training. Alternative powertrains may require specialized service arrangements, particularly
for battery systems and fuel cell stacks.

### 4.10.5 What the Data Supports

Run on Less – Messy Middle documented that:
* Diesel remains capable, reliable, and supported by mature infrastructure across all duty cycles.
* CNG/RNG demonstrates competitive performance in regional applications where fueling infrastructure
exists, with the new Cummins X15N engine extending viability to longer-haul operations.
* BEV technology performs effectively in well-matched duty cycles — particularly RTB, drayage, and
regional applications with favorable terrain and reliable charging access.
* Hydrogen FCEV operations face significant infrastructure and availability constraints that currently limit
deployment to specific corridors and applications with dedicated fueling arrangements.

The Messy Middle designation reflects the reality that multiple technologies will coexist for the foreseeable
future, each serving different segments of the market based on operational requirements. Fleet decisions should
be grounded in specific duty-cycle requirements, available infrastructure, and realistic assessment of technology
maturity rather than assumptions about industry-wide transitions.

# 5 FLEET PERFORMANCE ANALYSIS

Run on Less – Messy Middle assembled 13 commercial fleets operating across four distinct powertrain
technologies to document real-world performance in revenue freight service. Over the course of three weeks in
September 2025, these fleets collectively accumulated more than 73,000 validated miles across varied terrain,
duty cycles, and operating conditions. The trucks were not demonstration vehicles on controlled routes; they
were working assets carrying commercial freight, meeting customer schedules, and operating within the
infrastructure, weather, and traffic conditions that define everyday freight operations.

This section presents detailed operational analyses for each fleet, organized by powertrain technology: diesel
and renewable diesel, CNG/RNG, BEVs, and FCEVs. See Figure 26.

The four powertrain categories represent different stages of commercial maturity and address different
segments of the freight market.

**Diesel, renewable diesel and biodiesel** remain the dominant technology for long-haul freight, with four fleets
demonstrating what optimized conventional operations can achieve. These fleets — Albert Transport,
Schneider, Frito-Lay, and Mesilla Valley Transportation — collectively logged more than 32,000 miles with fuel
economy ranging from 9.0 to 11.8 MPG depending on terrain and payload. Their performance establishes the
efficiency baseline that alternative powertrains must approach while also demonstrating the practical range,
operational flexibility, and infrastructure independence that have made diesel the industry standard.

**CNG/RNG** represents the most mature alternative-fuel technology currently in widespread commercial
deployment. Three fleets — Wegmans, UPS, and Kleysen Group — operated 15-liter natural gas powertrains
across regional and long-haul corridors, accumulating more than 20,000 miles. These operations provided early
real-world data on the Cummins X15N engine, which entered production in early 2025, across duty cycles
ranging from regional food distribution to high-GCW interstate triples and heavy-haul tanker operations [8].

**BEVs** are at an inflection point, with five deployments across four platforms logging more than 15,000 miles.
JoyRide, SAIA, 4 Gen Logistics, and Nevoya operated the Freightliner eCascadia, Volvo VNR Electric, Tesla Semi
(in both regional and long-haul configurations), and Windrose Technologies sleeper respectively across duty
cycles from urban multi-stop distribution to genuine long-haul corridor freight. Battery capacities spanning 438

Page 76

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

to 705+ kWh created fundamentally different operational envelopes, and strategic high-power DC fast charging along freight corridors proved as important as battery size in determining daily productivity.

FCEVs are in the earliest stage of Class 8 commercialization, yet their operational promise already is evident. Penske and Pilot operated Hyundai XCIENT fuel cell tractors — one of the world's first mass-produced FCEV heavy-duty trucks — across nearly 6,000 miles in Texas and California. While designed and optimized for regional haul duty cycles rather than cross-country long-haul, these deployments provided meaningful evidence of hydrogen's capabilities and constraints in revenue freight service.

Each fleet analysis in this section is built from validated operational data. The telemetry, resampled to standardized 10-second intervals from the varying native frequencies of each fleet's telematics system, captures speed, location, energy or fuel consumption, elevation, payload indicators, and operational status. Daily and run-level metrics have been cross-validated against fleet-provided dashboard data to ensure calculation accuracy. Where data anomalies existed — odometer jumps, missing intervals, or sensor artifacts — the analytical approach favored conservative, explainable methods over aggressive estimation.

The operational metrics reported for each fleet include total distance, active days, daily productivity ranges, fuel or energy efficiency by terrain and speed band, idle time and auxiliary energy use, and PMR estimates derived from observed best- and worst-day performance. Equipment specifications cover tractor configuration, powertrain details, energy storage, and key aerodynamic and technology features relevant to understanding each fleet's performance.

Critically, the commentary that accompanies each fleet's metrics goes beyond the numbers to explain what drove performance. A truck's efficiency reflects not just its powertrain but the interaction of terrain profile, speed discipline, payload mix, operating windows, and infrastructure positioning. These factors — often invisible in summary statistics — are explored in enough depth to support meaningful planning decisions.

The title of this Run captures an essential truth about freight decarbonization: the industry is not transitioning cleanly from one dominant technology to another. Instead, fleets face a complex, multi-decade shift in which diesel, natural gas, battery electric, and hydrogen will coexist — each finding applications where its particular strengths align with operational requirements. The Messy Middle is not a temporary inconvenience but a structural reality that demands clear-eyed assessment of what each technology can and cannot do today [1].

The analyses that follow are designed to cut through both skepticism and enthusiasm to present what actually happened when these trucks ran real freight. Diesel's efficiency and flexibility are documented alongside its carbon intensity and fuel price exposure. BEV's promise is balanced against its terrain sensitivity and infrastructure dependence. CNG's maturity is weighed against its energy density constraints. Hydrogen's refueling speed is set against its sparse infrastructure and unresolved cost economics.

Fleets making technology decisions in the coming years need exactly this kind of operational evidence — not projections or demonstrations, but validated performance from working assets in commercial service. The data in this section is offered in that spirit: as a practical resource for understanding where we are today and what still needs to happen for the Messy Middle to resolve into a cleaner, more efficient freight future.

With duty cycles defined, Section 5 applies this framework to document what each fleet achieved in revenue service.

## 5.1 Diesel, Renewable Diesel and Biodiesel Fleets

Diesel remains the operational baseline for long-haul freight in North America, and the four diesel and renewable-diesel fleets in this section provide a clear view of what the highest-performing operators can achieve today. These fleets span flat Midwestern corridors, rolling Western plateaus, and genuine cross-country routes that include high-altitude mountain passes. Across these varied geographies, all four fleets operated as working assets, carrying commercial freight at real gross vehicle weights and running full customer schedules — not controlled test loops.

Each fleet ran its normal duty cycle with conventional fueling, routing, and dispatching patterns. This makes the data in this section directly comparable to day-to-day operations across the industry rather than being limited to a demonstration environment. Because the daily data include full payloads, traffic variability, overnight scheduling, and typical driver behavior, the performance documented here represents the practical ceiling available from today’s diesel technology when equipment, routing, and operational discipline are aligned.

Across the Run, the diesel fleets also provide an important benchmark against the broader industry. NACFE’s 2024 Fleet Fuel Study found that typical long-haul fleets still operate near 6 MPG on average. NACFE tracks the Federal Highway Administrations (FHWA) combination truck fuel economy, which has improved from 5.85 MPG in 2013 to 6.91 MPG in 2022, with much of the improvement coming in the last two years [12] [13].

Against that backdrop, Albert Transport at approximately 11.8 MPG and Mesilla Valley Transportation at approximately 11.7 MPG, with Schneider and Frito-Lay also above the national baseline, effectively show nearly two times the fuel efficiency of a typical long-haul fleet. These results frame the diesel fleets in this section as practical stretch targets for what is achievable with existing assets today.

The four fleet summaries that follow present consistent, data-driven views of diesel and renewable-diesel performance, highlighting how terrain, duty cycle, equipment specification, driver behavior, and operational choices combine to produce real-world MPG outcomes.

### 5.1.1 Albert Transport — Freightliner Cascadia

Albert Transport operated a 2022 Freightliner Cascadia Class 8 sleeper tractor in long-haul freight service across the Midwestern and Eastern United States. The truck ran classic transcontinental linehaul freight almost entirely on flat Interstate corridors, providing a contemporary diesel baseline for high-efficiency operations. The Cascadia was equipped with a 14.8L Detroit DD15 engine rated at 455 hp, a Detroit DT12 automated manual transmission, full aerodynamic package, liftable 6×2 axle configuration, and battery-based HVAC system with solar assist — combining modern aerodynamics, powertrain automation, and auxiliary electrification typical of current long-haul tractors. See Figure 27.

### 5.1.1.3 Operational Metrics

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for Albert Transport’s 2022 Freightliner Cascadia that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

**Duty Cycle** — Flat-corridor long-haul linehaul. Transcontinental long-haul freight linking Midwestern and Eastern hubs on predominantly flat Interstate corridors, with trips largely contained within single-driver HOS limits. The duty cycle emphasizes steady cruise on long freeway pulls rather than stop-and-go regional delivery work, keeping urban exposure and intersection time to a minimum.

**Speed Distribution** — Extremely highway-centric. Roughly 96% to 97% of miles occur above 50 mph, including about 85% above 60 mph, confirming one of the most freeway-optimized duty cycles in the Run. The truck spends almost no time in low-speed urban environments, so fuel economy is dominated by aerodynamics, gearing, and cruise discipline at sustained highway speeds.

**Distance & Utilization** — High daily productivity. Across 16 active days the truck covered about 8,038 miles, averaging approximately 502 miles/day with 13 days above 400 miles and 6 days above 600 miles (daily range 206 to 773 miles). This pattern reflects intensive long-haul deployment and strong alignment between dispatch planning, equipment capability, and HOS constraints.

March 15, 2026

NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY

Page 81

---

<!-- page: 82 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

**Fuel Economy** — Exceptional for a fully loaded diesel. Run-level efficiency was approximately 11.8 MPG, with daily results ranging from 9.88 to 13.85 MPG while hauling commercial freight at typical highway weights. The highest-MPG days aligned with long, uninterrupted freeway segments on flat terrain, highlighting the combined impact of aerodynamics, speed management, and a flat corridor. Lower-MPG days corresponded to heavier traffic, routing variation, and unfavorable wind conditions — showing how conditions drive much of the remaining spread once the spec is optimized.

**Operating Windows** — Heavy overnight operations. Roughly two-thirds of the miles were accumulated between midnight and 10 am, with a pronounced peak between 1 am and 4 am. Running outside the worst congestion windows reduces stop-and-go losses, supports high average speeds, and helps meet morning delivery and appointment commitments in major metro areas.

**Idle Management and Auxiliary Loads** — eAPU reduces parasitic losses. Idle time was held near 3% of operating hours, enabled by battery-powered HVAC that avoids the 0.6 to 1.0 gallons per hour fuel use typical of older diesel idling practices. This demonstrates how relatively simple electrification of cab comfort systems can deliver meaningful fuel savings without changing the core powertrain.

> **Figure — Henry Albert quote block**
> Type: Photograph and callout
> Axes: not applicable
> Series: not applicable
> Values: not applicable
> Notes: Quote: "When you combine the driver embracing the technology and understanding the technology, it takes the fuel efficiency and performance levels out to areas that were incomprehensible a short time ago." — Henry Albert, CEO and Driver, Albert Transport. Includes photograph of Henry Albert.

**Payload Flexibility** — Minimal MPG penalty across the observed range. GCVW ranged from roughly 37,000 lbs. to nearly 80,000 lbs., with most days around 60,000 lbs. and above. Observed fuel economy remained strong across this range, reinforcing diesel’s relatively low sensitivity to payload in flat terrain and allowing dispatchers to prioritize freight and service requirements rather than range limitations.

**Liftable Axle Strategy** — Cumulative efficiency benefits. The 6×2 configuration with a liftable pusher axle — combined with a lift axle on the trailer — allowed flexible axle configurations ranging from three to five axles on the ground depending on load. The tractor's lift axle was raised for roughly half of the total miles. While lifting axles does not reduce total rolling resistance (the load simply redistributes to the remaining tires), observed fuel savings likely stem from reduced tire scrub, improved axle alignment, and aerodynamic gains that accumulate in a duty cycle dominated by steady freeway cruise.

**Range Advantage** — Infrastructure-independent operations. With a 150-gallon tank and approximately 11.8 MPG, Albert’s Cascadia has a theoretical PMR above 1,700 miles, although practical refueling occurs at 500- to 700-mile intervals aligned with driver breaks and appointment schedules. In this context, diesel range is

**Uptime & Activity Pattern** — High calendar utilization. The truck was active on 16 of 19 possible days during the Run, corresponding to approximately 84% calendar utilization. This high deployment rate, combined with consistently long daily distances, underscores that the observed efficiency was achieved in normal, revenue-generating service rather than a controlled demonstration.

**Range Capability** — Albert Transport’s PMR is approximately 1,588 miles. Based on observed best- and worst-day MPG, Albert’s 150-gal. tank supports an estimated PMR of approximately 1,588 miles (range 1,334 to 1,870 miles), one of the strongest diesel range capabilities observed in this Run on the routes studied.

Daily distance ranged from roughly 200 to nearly 800 miles, with MPG spanning from about 9.9 to 13.9 while hauling commercial payloads at typical highway weights. The highest-efficiency days combined long, uninterrupted freeway segments, stable weather, and minimal congestion, whereas lower-MPG days reflected more variable traffic, routing, and wind conditions — even on the same flat corridor.

Across the Run, the summary confirms that modern diesels can sustain double-digit MPG on flat terrain when aerodynamics, speed discipline, and overnight operations align, but that day-to-day variation from traffic and weather still drives a meaningful share of the efficiency spread.

Albert Transport operates one of the most freeway-optimized duty cycles in the Run, with roughly 96% to 97% of miles above 50 mph and about 85% above 60 mph. Urban and low-speed segments are limited almost entirely to terminal access and brief routing around incidents, so fuel economy is dominated by aerodynamic drag and cruise strategy rather than stop-and-go losses.

Despite this high-speed profile, the truck maintains a run-level efficiency near 11.8 MPG while hauling full commercial loads. This confirms that when a modern tractor is equipped with a full aerodynamic package, low rolling-resistance tires, an optimized rear-axle ratio, and an automated transmission, it can hold strong MPG even at sustained highway speeds. In this duty cycle, further gains are more likely to come from route and speed-management policies than from additional hardware changes alone.

Terrain Interpretation: Albert Transport operated across exceptionally flat Midwestern and Eastern corridors, with roughly 98% of documented miles on grades below 3% (elevation approximately -39 to 187 ft., mean approximately 45 ft.). This is one of the flattest terrain profiles in the entire Run, and it defines the operational context for Albert’s high fuel economy. Route geometry is dominated by valley floors, coastal plains, and prairie terrain along corridors such as I-70, I-80, and I-95, where slope variation is minimal and speed stability is high. Grade exposure above 3% accounts for less than 2% of miles and appears mainly as short, balanced climb-descent sequences rather than any meaningful mountain work.

Terrain & Performance Implications: Because gravitational work is such a small portion of the energy budget, Albert’s approximately 11.8 MPG efficiency is driven mainly by aerodynamic integration, disciplined cruise control operation, and the truck’s highly optimized 6×2 liftable axle specification. Flat terrain acts as an amplifier for good spec and driver practice. High-speed aero drag dominates, and the Cascadia’s full aero package delivers maximum benefit. This corridor pattern — long freeway runs with minimal vertical variation — shows what diesel can achieve under ideal geographic alignment, while also illustrating why fleets assign their most fuel-sensitive freight to these flatter lanes.

### 5.1.1.7 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of insights NACFE gained analyzing the operational metrics that were tracked for Albert Transport’s 2022 Freightliner Cascadia that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Aerodynamic package works as a system. The combination of tractor fairings, trailer skirts, gap management, and low-rolling-resistance tires helps Albert sustain approximately 11.8 MPG even when more than 85% of miles

March 15, 2026

NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY

<!-- page: 87 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

are at highway speeds above 60 mph. These results reinforce that fleets must treat aerodynamics as a full-vehicle system rather than a collection of optional add-ons.

Liftable 6×2 axle enables load-based efficiency. The lift axle was raised for roughly half of the total miles, aligning with lighter payloads and steady freeway running. This confirms that a properly spec’d 6×2 can improve fuel economy without compromising traction when fleets have the dispatch data and driver training to use it appropriately.

> **Figure — Greg Treinen quote block**
> Type: Photograph and callout
> Axes: not applicable
> Series: not applicable
> Values: not applicable
> Notes: Quote: "It’s really important as you’re purchasing a new truck to continue to evaluate what those specs look like and really understand what the OEM is doing to help improve those fuel efficiency numbers." — Greg Treinen, VP, On-Highway Development, Daimler Truck North America. Includes photograph of Greg Treinen.

Automation reduces driver-to-driver variability. The automated manual transmission and adaptive cruise control keep the Detroit DD15 operating in a narrow, efficient band, particularly on long, flat freeway pulls. This reduces the impact of individual driving style on fuel economy and illustrates how modern powertrains can deliver “house-standard” MPG when paired with consistent training and policies.

Auxiliary electrification cuts idle without sacrificing comfort. An eAPU with solar assist holds idle to close to 3% of operating hours, avoiding the fuel penalty of traditional diesel idling for cab comfort. For fleets, this shows that meaningful fuel savings can come from auxiliary systems — without changing the core engine or transmission.

Route and schedule discipline protect MPG. Operating mostly on flat Midwestern and Eastern corridors and concentrating about two-thirds of miles between midnight and 10 am helps avoid heavy congestion and frequent braking events. The combination of corridor selection and overnight scheduling is a major contributor to the strong fuel economy and is a lever fleets can act on without changing equipment.

Weather and wind still matter at the margins. Even on flat terrain, crosswinds and headwinds can move daily MPG by one to two points, reminding fleets that route selection and weather-awareness planning remain important levers even after specs are optimized.

Driver trust in automation underpins repeatable results. Achieving approximately 11.8 MPG requires drivers to trust automated manual transmissions, adaptive cruise, and lift-axle logic. Albert’s results underline the importance of training that explains not just how systems work, but why they improve efficiency.

Tire strategy contributes quiet, compounding gains. Wide-base singles and disciplined inflation management cut rolling resistance in a duty cycle where tires see almost uninterrupted highway use. The gains are small day by day but compound over thousands of miles per week.

Digital visibility connects field performance to fleet strategy. Albert’s telematics, fuel, and maintenance records give fleet managers the detail needed to separate route and weather effects from spec performance, turning one high-efficiency truck into a learning platform for the broader fleet.

### 5.1.1.8 Albert Transport Summary

Albert Transport’s Freightliner Cascadia deployment shows what modern diesel can deliver when the duty cycle, geography, and specification all point in the same direction. Over roughly 8,000 miles and 16 active days, the truck averaged about 11.8 MPG with 500-plus miles of daily productivity and high calendar utilization, all while hauling commercial freight at typical long-haul weights.

The efficiency is not the result of a single standout technology, but of a stack of integrated choices — full aerodynamic treatment on the tractor and trailer, a 6×2 liftable axle that adapts to lighter loads, automated manual transmission and cruise settings that keep the engine in its sweet spot, and eAPU that avoids idling during rest periods. Operating almost entirely on flat Midwestern and Eastern interstate corridors, the truck converts those technology and operational decisions into consistently low fuel use per mile.

For fleets running similar flat-corridor long-haul lanes, Albert’s performance provides a practical reference point. Low-double-digit MPG is achievable today with commercially available diesel equipment and disciplined operations, without changing fuel or business model. Within the wider diesel group, Albert also helps anchor the “flat-terrain baseline,” highlighting how much of the remaining variation in diesel performance across fleets is driven by geography, routing, and operating practice rather than fundamental engine efficiency.

### 5.1.2 Frito-Lay North America — Volvo VNL

Frito-Lay North America, a $23 billion division of PepsiCo, operates one of the largest private fleets in North America with roughly 22,000 vehicles spanning cargo vans through Class 8 tractors. For Run on Less – Messy Middle, the fleet deployed a Volvo VNL day cab equipped with the Optimus Technologies Vector System, a dual-tank fuel system that enables operation on B99/B100 biodiesel for driving while using conventional diesel for engine startup and shutdown — with engine waste heat keeping the biodiesel flowing in cold conditions. The tractor features a Volvo D13 engine with I-Torque spec, a 6×2 liftable axle configuration, Volvo I-Shift automated manual transmission, and a full suite of aerodynamic technologies including TruckWings and FlowBelow wheel covers, combined with Volvo’s I-See terrain-aware cruise control.

The truck operated out of Frito-Lay’s Topeka, Kansas, hub on regional and long-haul snack food distribution routes serving Texas, the Dakotas, and coast-to-coast lanes. The duty cycle combines high-mileage Interstate segments, regional distribution center turns, and limited mixed-traffic approaches to urban markets. Frito-Lay’s Topeka hub operates on-site fuel storage supporting the fleet's biodiesel program, allowing drivers to fuel without changes to route planning or schedules.

The Volvo truck with the Optimus system has an inherent operational benefit of running on either full ULSD diesel or B99/B100. This is valuable when needing fuel where biodiesel is not available. NACFE was unable to verify the specific fuel type used on individual legs during the Run period. Reported efficiency figures reflect actual operational performance as recorded during the demonstration.

**Speed Distribution** — Highway dominant with moderate urban exposure. Most miles occur at 55 to 70 mph on Interstates and major four-lane highways, with a smaller share in 25 to 45 mph mixed-traffic zones near plants and distribution centers. Fuel economy is driven primarily by aerodynamic drag and rolling resistance at cruise, with only limited energy loss in low-speed congestion or terminal moves.

**Distance & Utilization** — Consistent regional and corridor productivity. **Fuel Economy** — High efficiency on a well-optimized platform. Run-level fuel economy was competitive with comparable diesel trucks in similar duty cycles. Daily MPG spans from the high-8s on short or constrained days to above 10 MPG on long, uninterrupted highway runs.

**Operating Windows** — Mix of day and night operations. Operations span both daytime and overnight windows depending on route and delivery schedules. Night operations help reduce congestion in some corridors, while daytime work supports dock and store receiving hours. This mix exposes the tractor to a range of traffic conditions while holding MPG close to Frito-Lay's high-efficiency diesel standard.

**Operating Environment** — Predominantly flat and light-rolling corridors. The network is centered on Midwest and Plains routes with modest elevation change and relatively predictable weather. These conditions allow the Volvo VNL’s aero, transmission, and cruise-control systems to sustain high efficiency and highlight the underlying platform efficiency apart from terrain effects.

> Notes: Quote: "I'm just looking forward to driving this truck and showing what it will do for fuel mileage and showcasing what it's able to do and what we can do with it as a driver — how well it performs." — Tim Herring, Driver, Frito-Lay. Includes photograph of Tim Herring.

**Payload Profile** — Light-to-moderate density snack freight. Snack foods are relatively low density, so trailers tend to cube out before they weigh out. Payload-related MPG penalties are modest, and efficiency varies more with speed, terrain, and wind than with modest differences in load weight.

**Range Capability** — Frito-Lay’s PMR is shorter than other diesel fleets. Frito-Lay’s practical PMR is approximately 1,262 miles (range 1,083 to 1,421 miles), roughly 300 to 350 miles shorter than Albert and MVT but still providing ample range for regional snack-food distribution routes.

**Range and Fueling** — Dual-tank Optimus configuration. With standard tank capacity, the tractor retains practical range comparable to conventional diesel units. The dual-tank system is designed to accommodate either ULSD or B99/B100 operation depending on fuel availability.

**Uptime & Activity Pattern** — High-utilization private-fleet asset. The Volvo VNL operates as a normal revenue vehicle in Frito-Lay’s private fleet, with utilization patterns aligned to broader network needs rather than a special project. No additional downtime was incurred, demonstrating that the Optimus-equipped Volvo VNL was integrated into high-utilization operations at this site.

A small number of partial or constrained days sit at the lower end of the MPG range, driven primarily by short distance, local assignments, or congestion. Together, these examples show that the platform supports both long-haul and regional duty cycles without constraining range or forcing major scheduling changes.

### 5.1.2.5 Speed Variability

Speed-band analysis confirms that Frito-Lay's operation is dominated by highway speeds above 50 mph, with only a small share of miles at lower speeds around facilities and in local traffic. MPG shows the expected pattern: lower efficiency at low speeds (3.9 MPG in the 0 to 25 mph band) as a result of stop-start losses, rising through mid-range speeds, and peaking near 10.3 MPG in highway cruise bands above 50 mph.

The combination of aero devices, transmission strategy, and driver practice keeps efficiency remarkably stable across the 50 to 70 mph cruise range. With 82% of miles above 60 mph, the small exposure to low-speed operation helps maintain the strong 9.5 MPG overall average. See Figure 40.

### 5.1.2.6 Terrain & Elevation

Frito-Lay’s corridors are primarily flat to light rolling, with modest elevations typical of Midwest and Plains routes. Terrain analysis shows that the majority of miles fall in the 0% to 3% grade range, with only limited exposure to moderate or steep grades. MPG remains close to the Run average across these flat and light-rolling bands, with expected reductions on the small fraction of miles that involve more pronounced climbs. The terrain profile reinforces that high-blend biodiesel can deliver competitive MPG in the largely flat corridors where many regional food-distribution fleets operate today. See Figure 41.
