Centralized hubs simplify biodiesel deployment. Operating from major hubs like Topeka, Kansas allows Frito-Lay to control fuel storage and quality at a small number of sites, which in turn makes it easier to manage B99 year-round without adding complexity at every delivery point.

Trailer and route planning protect MPG as volume grows. Because snack loads cube out before they weigh out, careful trailer utilization and route consolidation help maintain high trailer fill and minimize empty miles — important complements to the fuel and tractor spec.

Technician training closes the loop on biodiesel concerns. Targeted training for shop staff on filters, cold-flow behavior, and tank management helps address lingering concerns about biodiesel reliability and turns B99 into a routine part of preventive maintenance.

B99 experience informs future zero-emission deployments. Operational lessons from B99 — on hub fueling, route predictability, and asset utilization — directly inform how Frito-Lay will plan future CNG, BEV, and hydrogen deployments in similar corridors.

Terrain Interpretation: Frito-Lay operated across predominantly flat to light-rolling Midwest and Plains corridors, with roughly 85% of documented miles on grades below 3% (elevation approximately 690 to 1,420 ft., mean approximately 1,000 ft.). This terrain profile is characteristic of central US freight distribution — gently undulating prairie, river valley crossings, and long Interstate stretches along corridors such as I-70, I-35, and I-44. Grade exposure above 3% accounts for approximately 15% of miles, appearing mainly as gradual transitions between plateau levels and occasional river bluff approaches rather than sustained mountain climbing.

Terrain & Performance Implications: The relatively benign terrain allows Frito-Lay's Volvo VNL to maintain consistent efficiency across the Run, with MPG ranging narrowly from approximately 9.5 on light-rolling segments to approximately 9.8 on flat corridors. Unlike fleets operating in western mountain terrain, gravitational work represents a modest share of the energy budget, so aerodynamic drag and rolling resistance dominate fuel consumption. The 14% of miles on moderate grades (3% to 6%) produces a measurable but contained efficiency penalty (approximately 8.8 MPG), while the small fraction of steeper segments (~1.2% at ≥6%) reflects localized features like interchange ramps and river crossings rather than true mountain passes. This profile makes Frito-Lay’s corridors well-suited for high-blend biodiesel deployment, where consistent operating conditions help maintain predictable fuel economy and simplify storage and handling logistics.

### 5.1.2.7 Operational Insights

Below are insights into operational factors that influenced Frito-Lay’s Volvo VNL performance in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Real freight, not a lab trial. Frito-Lay's Volvo VNL operated in normal regional service out of Topeka, Kansas, pulling revenue loads and running its regular return-to-base corridors rather than a dedicated test loop.

Regional RTB pattern simplifies fuel logistics. The RTB structure, with on-site heated B99 storage and controlled fueling, removes much of the cold-flow and availability risk that can complicate high-blend biodiesel deployments in dispersed linehaul networks.

Duty cycle plays to the fuel’s strengths. The highway-dominant duty cycle — with more than 80% of miles above 60 mph — keeps the truck in steady-state cruise conditions where diesel efficiency is highest, limiting exposure to the acceleration and idling losses inherent in stop-and-go operation.

Cold-weather envelope still matters. While the Run did not expose the fleet to the harshest winter conditions, the need for heated storage and temperature-aware handling remains a constraint for scaling B99 into colder climates or truly year-round operations without additional safeguards.

Maintenance and aftertreatment require monitoring, not reinvention. No unique aftertreatment issues or filter events emerged during the Run, but the fleet’s experience reinforces that high-blend biodiesel rollouts should be paired with close tracking of injector health, filter life, and DPF behavior.

Driver and dispatcher routines stay familiar. From the cab and dispatch perspective, the truck behaves like a conventional diesel. Range, refueling cadence, and shift patterns all remain within normal expectations, improving adoption likelihood compared to powertrains that require new charging or fueling windows.

Carbon reduction hinges on supply chain quality. The decarbonization benefit comes from sourcing verified low-carbon B99 and maintaining consistent quality. Unreliable supply or off-spec fuel can quickly erode both environmental and operational benefits.

Lessons for other regional fleets. Frito-Lay’s experience suggests that fleets with dense regional networks, controlled yards, and predictable dwell can integrate B99 with relatively low disruption, especially if they already manage on-site storage for conventional fuels.

PMR — Highway range remains robust. With 150 gallons of fuel on board and real-world efficiency near 9.8 MPG, Frito-Lay’s Volvo VNL maintained a PMR in the 1,300- to 1,500-mile band, giving dispatchers essentially the same routing flexibility they would expect from a comparable ULSD tractor.

### 5.1.2.8 Frito-Lay Summary

Frito-Lay's Volvo VNL — equipped with the Optimus Technologies Vector System for ULSD and biodiesel-capable operation — provides a practical example of how a modern, aero-optimized diesel platform can deliver high single-digit to low double-digit MPG in real-world regional and long-haul service using existing fueling infrastructure and operational practices. For fleets exploring biodiesel as part of a broader decarbonization strategy, the Optimus system's fuel flexibility illustrates how that transition can be managed without disruption to routes, schedules, or driver behavior.

### 5.1.3 Mesilla Valley Transportation — International LT

Mesilla Valley Transportation (MVT) is a long-haul truckload carrier founded in the early 1980s with operations across the lower 48 states and Canada. MVT is widely recognized for its focus on fuel efficiency and aerodynamics. The fleet was an early adopter of trailer skirts and gap-reduction devices and maintains average fuel economy around 8.65 MPG, roughly 40% better than typical North American truckload performance.

For Run on Less – Messy Middle, MVT deployed an International LT 73” Sky-Rise sleeper with a Cummins X15 engine, Eaton Endurant automated manual transmission, 6×2 axle configuration, and a full aerodynamic package, including tractor fairings and trailer skirts. MVT’s regular network links El Paso, Nashville, and California; during the Run, the truck operated primarily on the El Paso to Nashville corridor with segments through New Mexico and Utah, often with team drivers running 20+ hours per calendar day and hauling general freight at typical long-haul weights. See Figure 42.

This discipline keeps the Cummins X15 in an efficient band and helps sustain high MPG even as terrain and weather change.

Terrain Interpretation: MVT operated one of the most elevation-diverse diesel routes in the Run, spanning low-lying Gulf Coast segments to Rocky Mountain passes above 6,000 ft., with a total elevation range from approximately 120 ft. to approximately 8,600 ft. Approximately 8% to 9% of miles occurred on 3% to 6% grades, and nearly 1% on ≥6% grades, representing genuine mountain exposure. The bulk of distance (roughly 75%) fell within the 0% to 3% grade bands, reflecting long flat/rolling interstate stretches across Texas, New Mexico, and mid-continent states. This blend of desert basins, rolling foothills, and high-altitude segments creates one of the most diverse grade distributions in the dataset.

Terrain & Performance Implications: Terrain materially influences MVT’s approximately 11.7 MPG run-level efficiency. On flat and lightly rolling ground, the truck routinely delivers 11.8 to 12.2 MPG, matching the best diesel results in the program. But sustained 3% to 6% climbs drop MPG into the 10 MPG range, and steep mountain segments (≥6%) push instantaneous MPG into the 6 to 7 MPG band — consistent with expectations for heavy freight in mountain corridors. The broad terrain variation explains the fleet’s high day-to-day efficiency swing. Unlike Albert’s flat-corridor optimization or Schneider’s moderate Western profile, MVT’s terrain mix tests the powertrain across its full operating envelope, giving a truer picture of cross-country diesel behavior under real elevation stress.

National linehaul under real elevation stress. MVT’s International LT runs genuine cross-country freight, from low Gulf Coast segments to Rocky Mountain passes above 6,000 ft. On flat and lightly rolling segments, the tractor regularly delivers 11.8 to 12.2 MPG, comparable to the best diesel results in the Run. Careful speed management, tight gap control, and aero-optimized spec help keep MPG in double digits even as terrain oscillates.

Terrain is the primary driver of day-to-day variation. Extended 3% to 6% grades pull MPG into the approximately 10 MPG range, and short ≥6% mountain pulls push instantaneous efficiency into the 6 to 7 MPG band. This 4 to 5 MPG spread between flat and mountainous segments illustrates how significantly geography shapes fuel consumption — even for the same truck and driver.

Speed profile confirms true interstate duty. With roughly 85% of miles at or above 50 mph — and a majority in the 50 to 60 mph band — the operation reflects authentic interstate conditions rather than carefully curated, low-speed test routes. Low-speed segments are limited to facility access, urban crossings, and localized congestion.

Utilization reflects a working fleet asset. The tractor’s activity pattern shows high calendar utilization with multiple 800+ mile days, consistent with intensive national linehaul deployment rather than a lightly used pilot vehicle. The Run data capture the truck’s performance under real customer and schedule pressure.

Driver practices amplify the spec advantages. Long-tenured drivers in this type of fleet typically make systematic use of cruise control, momentum preservation on rolling terrain, and disciplined throttle application, which are essential for converting an aero-optimized spec into sustained high MPG in the field.

Route choice and lane assignment matter. MVT’s results highlight how much lane assignment influences fuel outcomes. The same tractor could deliver higher average MPG on flatter corridors or lower results on even more mountainous routes. actually

### 5.1.3.8 Mesilla Valley Transportation Summary

Mesilla Valley Transportation’s International LT provides a high-MPG diesel benchmark for long-haul, team-driven operations across multiple regions and elevation profiles. The combination of disciplined specs, aerodynamics, driver coaching, and high utilization yields fuel economy that sits well above typical truckload performance. In the Messy Middle, MVT’s results help define what “best-in-class diesel” looks like in everyday freight, offering a reference point for fleets seeking to close the gap with their existing diesel operations and for those evaluating when and how alternative powertrains can compete on long-haul corridors.

### 5.1.4.3 Operational Metrics

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for Schneider’s International LT that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

**Duty Cycle** — Long-haul beverage lanes in the desert Southwest. The International LT operated primarily between Arizona and Southern California on long Interstate corridors, hauling beverages at typical long-haul weights. The duty cycle was dominated by freeway cruising with limited urban exposure at pickup and delivery points, providing a clear view of how renewable diesel performs in classic over-the-road operations.

**Speed Distribution** — Highway-dominant with modest urban exposure. Most miles occur at 55 to 70 mph on Interstate routes, with a smaller share at 25 to 45 mph around depots and customer facilities. The speed profile is similar to Schneider’s high-efficiency conventional diesel tractors, making it possible to compare renewable diesel performance against an established baseline without changing routes or service patterns.

**Distance & Utilization** — Consistent long-haul productivity. Daily distance patterns show a mix of full long-haul days and shorter repositioning or delivery days, with averages aligned to a typical single-driver schedule. The tractor is clearly a working asset, not a limited-use demonstration truck, and the Run data reflect routine freight movements under normal customer commitments.

Terrain Interpretation: Schneider’s renewable-diesel International LT operated across mixed-elevation Western corridors that included extended inland climbs, plateau segments, and moderate mountain approaches. Route elevation ranged from about 300 ft. to more than 4,700 ft., with roughly 21% of miles on grades above 3%, markedly higher than Albert’s profile. Terrain exposure is characteristic of long-haul Western freight: desert basins, rolling interior plateau, and highway segments feeding into higher terrain along I-10, I-15, and I-40. The route set demonstrates real-world elevation diversity rather than flat-corridor optimization.

Terrain & Performance Implications: These elevation changes materially shape Schneider’s approximately 9.0 to 9.8 MPG performance. On 0% to 1% grades, MPG climbs into the 10 to 11 MPG range — similar to Albert’s best results — indicating strong aerodynamic and powertrain efficiency when grade load is negligible. But on 3% to 6% grades, MPG falls into the 7 to 8 MPG range, with steep segments dipping near 5 MPG, quantifying the cost of sustained climbing. In contrast to Albert, where aerodynamics dominate, Schneider’s profile shows a meaningful portion of energy going into gravitational work. This produces a wider efficiency spread across days and underscores the importance of terrain-aware planning for fleets operating in the West.

### 5.1.4.8 Schneider Summary

Schneider National’s International LT on renewable diesel illustrates how a large for-hire fleet can reduce carbon intensity using familiar diesel technology, conventional fueling infrastructure, and established operating practices. The Run on Less – Messy Middle data show that, on a long-haul desert Southwest corridor, renewable diesel delivers fuel economy comparable to Schneider’s best ULSD operations while fitting seamlessly into existing routes, schedules, and maintenance programs. In the context of the Messy Middle, Schneider’s experience positions renewable diesel as a practical, near-term decarbonization lever that can be deployed quickly while fleets continue to evaluate and adopt CNG, BEV, and hydrogen solutions.

### 5.1.5 Diesel Cross-Fleet Summary

Modern diesel performance spans a wide band of real-world duty cycles. The four diesel fleets — Albert Transport, Schneider National, Frito-Lay, and Mesilla Valley Transportation — captured the full spectrum of North American long-haul and regional service including ultra-flat Midwestern corridors, western mountain passes, mixed rolling plains, and high-velocity national lanes. Despite dramatically different terrain, payload, and dispatch profiles, all four fleets delivered consistent high-single- to low-double-digit MPG, confirming diesel’s maturity and adaptability across nearly every long-haul use case.

Efficiency is shaped more by geography and duty cycle than by technology. Albert’s record-high approximately 11.8 MPG reflected exceptionally flat Great Plains and Eastern Seaboard corridors with 85% of miles above 60 mph. Schneider’s approximately 9.0 to 9.8 MPG demonstrated that even with 75% of miles spent on grades above 3% in western mountain terrain, modern aerodynamics, liftable axles, and disciplined cruise control maintain competitive efficiency. Frito-Lay's 9.7 to 9.9 MPG on its Optimus-equipped Volvo VNL showed strong performance in highway-dominant regional service. MVT’s 11.5 to 11.7 MPG across cross-country lanes — from near sea level to nearly 9,000 ft. — confirmed that diesel efficiency remains resilient across substantial elevation and climate variability. See Figure 58.

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 120

Fuel efficiency is directly relevant to CO₂ emissions — better fuel economy means lower carbon output per mile. These recent model-year diesels also meet current criteria pollutant standards through modern aftertreatment systems.

The aerodynamic configuration of the trailer, tractor-to-trailer gap distance, trailer weight, tire selection, and bogie positioning also significantly affect performance. Higher fuel economies are achievable with well-configured trailers, but fleets — particularly for-hire carriers — may not always control which trailers are assigned to their tractors.

Highway speed stability remains diesel’s defining strength. Across fleets, MPG remained relatively stable from 50 to 65 mph, particularly in flat or lightly rolling terrain. Albert held approximately 12.3 MPG above 60 mph; MVT held approximately 12.4 MPG in the 50 to 60 mph band; and Schneider achieved approximately 11.5 MPG in 60+ mph highway segments despite significant elevation gain. The combination of optimized aerodynamics, automated transmissions, and modern cruise control systems enables consistent powertrain operating points, which allows for high-speed efficiency to remain remarkably stable across fleets.

Terrain impacts efficiency more than speed. Elevation gains per 100 miles was the biggest reason for daily and fleet-level MPG variation. Albert’s operation was nearly level (elevation change under 200 ft.), producing tight MPG clustering (9.9 to 13.8). Frito-Lay’s moderate plains terrain kept MPG near approximately 10 MPG even on light rolling grades. Schneider’s Western operation produced the broadest spread (from low-5 MPG on steep climbs to more than 10 MPG on level segments), illustrating the nonlinear fuel cost of repeated climbs. MVT's ability to maintain more than 11 MPG across major mountain corridors reflects disciplined speed management (consistently below 60 mph on grades) as much as aerodynamic and powertrain optimization — a reminder that driver behavior remains a critical efficiency lever.

Payload sensitivity remains minimal across diesel fleets. All four fleets demonstrated limited MPG degradation with heavier loads. Albert saw only a 5% to 10% penalty between light and heavy payloads. Schneider observed similar resilience across beverage and dry van loads. MVT, operating with heavier transcontinental freight, showed stable MPG even at high gross weights.

Operational discipline is a major differentiator in diesel performance. Across fleets, high efficiency was associated with minimal idle time (less than 3% to 5%) and optimized overnight running that avoids peak traffic. Both Albert and MVT concentrated 60% to 70% of their miles in the midnight to 10 a.m. window, while Schneider and Frito-Lay operated with disciplined low-idle HVAC strategies.

These factors produced measurable MPG gains independent of terrain or speed.

Select electrification and mechanical optimization amplify diesel efficiency. All fleets leveraged non-powertrain technologies — eAPU, liftable axles, wide-base singles, and predictive cruise control — to capture incremental MPG improvements. These enhancements produce cumulative effects (3% to 15% combined) and represent the core of diesel’s ongoing efficiency trajectory — small, compounding improvements rather than a single breakthrough technology.

Fuel type does not materially alter diesel behavior at highway speeds. Frito-Lay's operation achieved strong efficiency (9.7 MPG overall) on flat Midwest corridors, consistent with what well-optimized diesel platforms deliver in highway-dominant duty cycles.

---

<!-- page: 121 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 121

While difficult to quantify, these results also illustrate the importance of experienced, skilled drivers who prioritize fuel economy alongside their other responsibilities. Route familiarity — knowing where grades begin, how traffic patterns shift, and when to anticipate slowdowns — is likely a contributing factor in achieving the higher MPG results documented here. Albert's performance, in particular, reflects this dynamic, combining the highest MPG with consistently high daily miles.

Collectively, these fleets demonstrate diesel’s ceiling — and its limits. When optimized, modern diesel equipment can routinely deliver 10 to 12 MPG in flat or lightly rolling terrain and maintain 8 to 10 MPG in heavily mountainous regions. The range abundance (1,200 to 3,000 miles per fill, depending on tank configuration), minimal range-weight sensitivity, and universal infrastructure confirm diesel’s position as the most operationally flexible long-haul technology today. At the same time, terrain, payload, and stop-start patterns still drive large day-to-day variation — showing where decarbonization alternatives (like CNG/RNG or hydrogen) can offer competitive or superior performance depending on the use case.

### 5.2 CNG/RNG Fleets

Compressed and renewable natural gas remain the most mature alternative-fuel options for long-haul and regional trucking, and the three CNG/RNG fleets in this section — Kleysen Group, UPS and Wegmans — demonstrate the breadth of real-world performance possible when natural-gas powertrains are paired with appropriate duty cycles. These fleets span moderate-grade northeastern corridors, high-elevation western interstate triples, and heavy-haul tanker operations in the Canadian prairies. Despite significant differences in geography, payload, and operating intensity, all three fleets delivered strong day-to-day consistency and confirmed the conditions under which modern 15-liter natural-gas powertrains perform most competitively.

This represents early real-world feedback with a newly available class of engine — the Cummins X15N natural gas engine introduced to production in early 2025 [8]. The results show support that this new engine performs similarly to its diesel counterparts in demanding, heavy-haul duty cycles. Efficiency figures for these CNG fleets — ranging from 4.5 to 6.7 mi/DGE — should not be compared directly to the diesel fleets in Section 5.1, which averaged 9 to 12 MPG. The duty cycles were fundamentally different.

All three CNG fleets operated at gross combination weights between 100,000 and 140,000 lbs., pulling doubles or triples, while the diesel fleets operated at standard 80,000 lbs. GCW with single trailers. Effectively, the CNG trucks were hauling roughly twice the freight payload per trip. When normalized using freight ton-miles per gallon, CNG performance is competitive with diesel — a 140,000 lbs. double at 4.6 mi/DGE delivers equivalent freight efficiency to an 80,000 lbs. single at 11 MPG. The apparent efficiency gap reflects payload differences, not powertrain limitations.

Across fleets, duty cycle and corridor, design mattered more than raw powertrain capability. Wegmans’ regional corridors through upstate New York — dominated by flat to lightly rolling terrain — produced a validated approximately 4.6 MPG performance with stable mid-400-mile daily productivity, matching the characteristics of mid-density food distribution. UPS’s Salt Lake City triples operation provided the opposite extreme — GCWs above 100,000 lbs., mean elevations above 4,600 ft., repeated basin climbs and descents, and 90% of miles above 60 mph. In that demanding western environment, the CNG Kenworth T680 still delivered approximately 6.7 mi/DGE across more than 9,000 miles with daily mileage variation under 25 miles. Kleysen offered a third anchor point — CNG tanker operations across Alberta's prairie and foothill corridors, where the Kenworth T880 maintained mid-4s mi/DGE while completing 400 to 575-mile corridor days in cold-weather mid-continent conditions.

---

<!-- page: 122 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 122

Terrain remains the primary differentiator of CNG/RNG performance. Wegmans operated on a modest 253-meter elevation band with 53% flat terrain, producing stable MPG with predictable penalties only on the 5% of miles above 3% grade. UPS ran with more than half of its distance on descents or climbs, accumulating more than 273,000 ft. of elevation gain and loss while maintaining a tight efficiency band — highlighting how disciplined lane design, high-speed stability, and consistent GCW can mitigate the variability introduced by western grades. Kleysen demonstrated that while heavy combination weights magnify the fuel cost of steep grades, the fleet’s 94% flat-or-rolling exposure kept overall efficiency competitive even with significant vertical work concentrated into a small share of miles.

Across all three fleets, speed stability emerged as the defining advantage of natural-gas long-haul. Each fleet recorded its best performance at sustained highway cruise — UPS above 60 mph in the low-7s mi/DGE, Wegmans at 50 to 60 mph near 5.8 MPG, and Kleysen above 60 mph around 5.0 mi/DGE in demanding tanker service. Conversely, low-speed exposure (less than 40 mph) universally produced the weakest results — reinforcing that the CNG fleets in this study operated most efficiently in steady-state highway conditions.

Payload and GCW sensitivity followed predictable patterns. Both Wegmans and Kleysen operated at comparable GCW ranges — approximately 140,000 lbs. loaded with empty or near-empty returns — while UPS ran triples above 100,000 lbs.

Infrastructure positioning — not tank size — was the binding constraint across fleets. All three tractors carried roughly 175 DGE of onboard CNG storage, yielding theoretical ranges between approximately 800 and approximately 1,200 miles depending on duty cycle. Yet all fleets relied on corridor-aligned daily refueling — Wegmans at depot-adjacent stations, UPS at its Salt Lake CNG hub, and Kleysen at public fast-fill stations in Alberta. Predictable lane structures, not ubiquitous station density, enabled consistent planning and removed range anxiety from day-to-day operations.

Collectively, the CNG/RNG fleets illustrate where natural gas powertrains excel in the Messy Middle:
* Highway-dominant duty cycles with predictable lanes
* Moderate terrain or balanced climb/descent profiles
* GCW-stable operations with low idle and disciplined speed control
* Corridors where CNG/RNG infrastructure already exists or can be co-located

They also demonstrate the natural limits — low-speed urban routes, repeated steep-grade exposure, and highly variable payload operations remain challenging for fuel-economy consistency. Within the right operational envelope, however, CNG/RNG provides a mature, repeatable, lower-carbon solution — one that can shoulder everything from food distribution to western triples to Canadian heavy-haul tanker work without sacrificing daily productivity.

### 5.2.1 Kleysen Group — CNG Kenworth T880

Kleysen Group operated a Kenworth T880 sleeper tractor powered by a Cummins X15N natural-gas engine on heavy-haul fuel tanker work across Alberta’s prairie and foothill corridors. See Figure 59. The truck ran long regional and corridor routes between Edmonton, Grande Prairie, Fort McMurray, and Calgary, hauling dense Class 3 petroleum products at combination weights typically between 110,000 and 140,000 lbs. GCW. This application provides a demanding heavy-haul benchmark for CNG performance at higher gross weights than most diesel and BEV fleets in the Run.

Operations were centered on Alberta’s mid- to high-elevation prairies and transition zones into the foothills, with route elevations ranging from roughly 774 to 3,700 ft. and a mean near 2,200 ft. The duty cycle combined long stretches of flat and lightly rolling prairie freeway with a smaller share of steeper coulees, river valleys, and foothill grades where the Cummins X15N must pull hard uphill and rely heavily on engine braking on the way back down. The result is a genuine heavy-haul profile — high speeds, high GCW, and meaningful but not dominant exposure to grades above 3%.

The T880’s sleeper configuration is notable for integrating 175 DGE of CNG storage — matching the capacity of day cab CNG tractors despite the reduced frame-rail space inherent to sleeper packaging. This tank arrangement enables 700- to 800-mile theoretical range per fill, supporting multi-day regional operations without the range compromises that have historically limited CNG to day cab applications.

During the Run, the T880 accumulated just more than 6,100 miles across 13 active days, with daily distance ranging from short repositioning moves in the 250- to 300-mile range up to demanding 575-mile corridor days. The tractor worked as a regular fleet asset rather than a demonstration unit, with refueling at public fast-fill CNG stations aligned to driver breaks and lane endpoints. Kleysen’s deployment illustrates how CNG can be integrated into heavy-haul bulk operations in cold-weather, mid-continent corridors when infrastructure, specifications, and operating practices are aligned. See Figure 60.

### 5.2.1.3 Operational Metrics

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for Kleysen’s Kenworth’s T888 that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Duty Cycle – Heavy-haul corridor tanker work. The Kleysen T880 operated as a heavy-haul tanker tractor, moving dense Class 3 petroleum products between Alberta terminals and industrial sites. The duty cycle combines long regional turns with predictable lane structures, where trucks routinely operate at GCW in the 110,000 to 140,000 lbs. range and must meet strict service windows in challenging weather.

Speed Distribution – True high-speed highway operation. Nearly 80% of distance occurs above 60 mph, with another 12% in the 50 to 60 mph band. Only around 4% to 5% of miles fall below 40 mph, confirming that yard moves, terminal access, and low-speed maneuvering are a very small share of total exposure. This is a genuine high-speed corridor profile, not a mixed urban or P&D application.

Distance & Utilization – Strong daily productivity under heavy weights. Over 13 active days the truck accumulated just over 6,100 miles, averaging in the high-400-mile range per active day. Long days in the 500- to 575-mile band represent full heavy-haul turns between major terminals, while shorter days capture repositioning or partial-lane assignments. This pattern shows that CNG can support typical heavy-haul daily targets, not just light regional work.

---

<!-- page: 127 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 127

Speed-Based Efficiency – Best at high-speed cruise despite heavy GCW. Overall efficiency is approximately 4.6 mi/DGE, with the less than 60 mph band delivering the best performance at roughly 5.0 mi/DGE — slightly above the fleet average despite the aerodynamic penalty of higher speeds. The 50 to 60 mph band sees efficiency soften to approximately 3.7 mi/DGE as grades, traffic, and heavy combination weights pull fuel economy down. At low speeds (0 to 40 mph) mi/DGE falls into the 2.3 to 3.3 range as stop/start operation dominates.

> **Figure — Sarah Abernathy, Advanced Powertrain Marketing Manager, Kenworth**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Quote: "Natural gas is suited for quite a few applications, and now with the 15-liter engine we're seeing it used for longer haul applications." — Sarah Abernathy, Advanced Powertrain Marketing Manager, Kenworth

Terrain Distribution – Flat and rolling prairie with targeted steep segments. Approximately 60% of distance occurs on very flat ground (0% to 1% grade), with another 34% in the 1% to 3% “light rolling” band. Only about 5% to 6% of distance is above 3% grade, but those segments involve significant climbs and descents through coulees and foothills. This mix confirms that Kleysen is not operating on mountain passes every day, but vertical work is still meaningful.

Terrain-Based Efficiency – Gravity penalties are concentrated but real. On flat terrain the fleet achieves approximately 4.7 mi/DGE and climbs only about 1,100 ft. per 100 miles. In the 1% to 3% rolling band, elevation gain per 100 miles jumps to about 4,400 ft. while mi/DGE softens only slightly to approximately 4.5. The 3% to 6% grades and 6% or greater segments — together just 6% or more of the distance — carry much higher elevation gain (≥10,000 to 23,000 ft. per 100 miles) and see mi/DGE fall into the 4.0 to 2.9 range, illustrating where heavy-haul CNG pays the steepest fuel penalty.

Payload / GCW – Operating near upper combination weights. Combination weights regularly exceed 110,000 lbs. GCW and can reach toward 140,000 lbs. depending on product and trailer configuration. This puts the CNG tractor in a significantly heavier band than most diesel and BEV fleets in the Run, making the observed mid-4s mi/DGE performance more notable than the raw number alone suggests. See Figure 63.

Refueling & Range – Corridor-aligned fast-fill strategy. With a 175 DGE CNG system and approximately 4.6 mi/DGE, theoretical range is on the order of 800 miles. In practice, refueling occurs every 400 to 600 miles at public fast-fill CNG stations placed along the heavy-haul corridors. This pattern aligns refueling with natural breaks and endpoint terminals, keeping operational planning similar to diesel lanes while retaining a healthy range buffer.

PMR – Probable Maximum Range. Based on 175 DGE onboard storage and observed fleet efficiencies, PMR is approximately 650 to 750 miles per fill under heavy-haul conditions, with a conservative reserve. Most documented daily runs fall well inside this envelope, confirming that range was not the limiting factor in lane design or schedule planning.

### 5.2.1.4 Sample Daily Summary

The sample daily summary for Kleysen, shown in Figure 64 reflects how CNG performs under genuine heavy-haul conditions across Alberta’s prairie and foothill corridors. Long-distance corridor days — such as the 574-mile turn on September 9 — deliver mi/DGE in the low-to-mid 4s, consistent with what the fleet sees on its regular tanker lanes at combination weights exceeding 110,000 lbs. Shorter days, like the 298-mile run on September 17, tend to cluster at or above the fleet average, particularly when favorable terrain and steady cruise speeds align.

Day-to-day variability tracks the expected operational drivers — route mix between flat prairie and foothill grades, loading configuration, and seasonal weather — rather than any limitation of the CNG powertrain itself. The daily summary table in Figure 64 confirms that, for dispatchers and drivers hauling Class 3 petroleum products on predictable corridor lanes, CNG delivers repeatable performance and familiar operational planning, even at the heaviest gross combination weights in the Run.

### 5.2.1.6 Speed & Efficiency

Kleysen spends nearly 80% of its distance above 60 mph, and that band delivers the best efficiency at about 5.0 mi/DGE, slightly above the fleet average of approximately 4.6. The 50 to 60 mph band carries roughly 12% of miles but sits closer to 3.7 mi/DGE, reflecting heavy combination weights and more variable traffic or grades.

At the low-speed end, the 0 to 40 mph bands represent only approximately 45% to 50% of distance yet show the weakest fuel economy (roughly 2.3 to 3.3 mi/DGE) as stop-start operation, maneuvering, and terminal work dominate. Overall, the profile confirms that Kleysen’s CNG trucks are doing true heavy-haul highway work, and the fleet’s mi/DGE is being earned at high speeds with substantial payloads rather than in light urban cycles.

### 5.2.1.8 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational insights NACFE gathered from the data collected from the Kleysen CNG truck that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Heavy haul proves CNG viability at high GCW. Operating routinely between approximately 110,000 and 140,000 lbs. GCW, Kleysen shows that CNG can support true heavy-haul tanker work when spec’d correctly, not just lighter regional van freight.

---

<!-- page: 133 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 133

> **Figure — Marcin Jedrzejczak, Driver, Kleysen Group**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Quote: "We want the truck to do the same job as a diesel. We operate in cold climates and in high temperatures and I have no complaints about how the truck performs." — Marcin Jedrzejczak, Driver, Kleysen Group

Speed discipline captures efficiency in a demanding environment. By keeping nearly 80% of miles above 60 mph on rural highways, the fleet keeps the X15N in a steady-state operating zone where combustion and gearing work efficiently despite the weight and terrain.

Terrain is meaningful but not overwhelming. Most miles are on flat or lightly rolling prairie, where mi/DGE is in the mid-4s. Short, steep segments are where fuel use spikes, reinforcing the value of route planning that minimizes repeated exposure to high grades.

Public fast-fill infrastructure is sufficient when lanes are deliberate. Refueling at public CNG stations along established corridors supports daily operations without forcing large detours. The key enabler is consistent lane structure and predictable station coverage, not universal CNG availability.

Cold-weather regional context matters. Alberta’s climate adds winter considerations — traction, cold starts, and driver comfort — but the combination of a natural-gas APU and stable fueling infrastructure allows CNG to function as a year-round option rather than a fair-weather technology.

Range is more than adequate for daily assignments. With a practical heavy-haul PMR in the 650 to 750-mile range and typical daily runs in the 400- to 575-mile band, the tractor operates with comfortable range margin. Dispatch does not need to design lanes around fuel limits.

Driver experience remains close to diesel. Interviews and operational feedback indicate that uphill performance and grade handling are comparable to diesel at equivalent GCW, with the primary differences being fueling location and slightly different engine sound and feel.

Use case framing: “corridor heavy haul” rather than general freight. Kleysen’s results are very transferable to other bulk and heavy-haul fleets running repeat lanes with limited urban exposure and predictable refueling, rather than to mixed local/regional operations.

### 5.2.1.9 Kleysen Group Summary

Kleysen’s heavy-haul Kenworth T880 demonstrates that CNG is not limited to lighter regional van freight or fair-weather duty. In the Alberta corridors, a natural-gas tractor hauling fuel tankers at 110,000 to 140,000 lbs. GCW delivered mid-4s mi/DGE in a high-speed, mid- to high-elevation environment with meaningful but manageable grades. The combination of modern Cummins X15N powertrain, suitable gearing, and deliberate lane selection

---

<!-- page: 134 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 134

allowed the fleet to integrate CNG into one of its more demanding applications rather than treating it as a niche pilot.

The results highlight both the strengths and boundaries of CNG in heavy-haul work. On flat and lightly rolling terrain, the technology performs reliably and efficiently enough to be competitive when paired with favorable fuel economics and emissions benefits. On steeper grades, the penalty is real but contained when those segments are a small share of total miles. For fleets operating similar corridor-style heavy-haul lanes with access to CNG infrastructure, Kleysen’s experience provides a realistic benchmark for what CNG can achieve today — and a reminder that thoughtful route and lane design are as important as the powertrain itself.

### 5.2.2 UPS — CNG/RNG Kenworth T680

UPS operated a CNG/RNG-fueled Kenworth T680 day cab with a Cummins X15N engine in high-elevation interstate linehaul service based out of Salt Lake City, Utah. The tractor pulled triple dry-van trailers on fixed lanes, providing a demanding test of natural-gas performance at high GCW and high average speeds. At this location, more than half of the linehaul tractors are CNG, and more than 90% of the medium-duty package cars are also CNG, making Salt Lake one of UPS’s most mature natural-gas hubs. Despite the geography, the fleet’s routing strategy keeps a majority of miles on high-speed interstate pavement rather than local roads or urban connectors.

### 5.2.2.3 Operational Metrics

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for UPS's Kenworth T680 that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Duty Cycle — High-elevation interstate triples. UPS runs this CNG T680 in dedicated linehaul service out of Salt Lake City, hauling triple trailers on western interstates. The duty cycle is almost entirely interstate running at high GCW over long distances, with turns designed to fit comfortably within single-driver HOS limits.

Speed Distribution — One of the most highway-optimized profiles in the Run. About 90% of driving distance occurs above 60 mph, including the vast majority at typical interstate cruise speeds. Speeds between 50 and 60 mph add another approximately 6% to 7% of miles, while less than 2% to 3% of distance is spent below 40 mph. This profile confirms a true highway linehaul application with minimal exposure to congested surface streets or local delivery work.

Distance and Utilization — Consistent, near-max daily miles. Over 13 active days, the truck covered roughly 9,015 miles, averaging approximately 694 miles per day with a median of approximately 696 miles. Daily distance clustered tightly between 676 and 699 miles, indicating a highly standardized lane where route design, freight volume, and driver scheduling are tightly aligned.

---

<!-- page: 137 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 137

Fuel Economy — Stable efficiency under demanding conditions. Run-level performance is roughly 6.7 to 6.8 mi/DGE, with day-to-day efficiency ranging from about 6.05 to 7.17 mi/DGE. That 18% to 19% spread is narrow for a long-haul operation and is notable given the combination of high elevation, triple trailers, and mixed climbing and descending. Much of the variation is likely tied to wind, traffic, and weather rather than changes in route design.

> **Figure — Karl Rasmussen, Driver, UPS**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Quote: "I've enjoyed it quite tremendously. The main part is knowing it's helping with the environment and keeping it clean. It's nice to see the trucking industry change like that." — Karl Rasmussen, Driver, UPS

Operating Windows — Daytime-dominant parcel linehaul. Most driving occurs between roughly 9 am and 10 pm local time, with peaks in the mid-day and early evening. This pattern reflects parcel network timing. Freight is moved between hubs to meet afternoon sort windows and next-day package commitments rather than shifted fully into overnight hours.

Idle Time — Low and disciplined. Idle time is on the order of 1% to 2% of total engine-on time, indicating tight coordination between dispatch, gate operations, and driver practices. Minimal idle reflects both schedule reliability and a culture of fuel discipline, and it helps protect CNG economics at a site where fuel costs and sustainability metrics are closely watched.

Payload/GCW — Heavier than typical U.S. linehaul. The triple-trailer combination routinely operates at GCWs above 100,000 lbs., significantly higher than the 80,000 lbs. standard for US single-trailer operations. That additional mass makes the achieved 6.7 mi/DGE more impressive, as every climb is undertaken with substantially more weight than a typical linehaul diesel tractor pulling a single 53’ trailer.

Refueling and Range — Large buffer beyond daily need. With roughly 175 DGE onboard and approximately 6.7 mi/DGE during the Run, the theoretical range approaches 1,180 to 1,200 miles per fill. In practice, the truck is fueled daily at the fleet's on-site CNG station, leaving a wide range buffer for weather, traffic disruptions, or temporary station issues and allowing refueling to be handled as a routine end-of-shift task.

PMR — Probable Maximum Range in daily operation. Based on observed efficiency and typical refueling practices, the PMR for this configuration is on the order of 900 to 1,000 miles per fill with a comfortable reserve. In this dedicated-lane context, UPS chooses not to use the truck's full range capability, instead prioritizing consistent daily fueling and predictable operations.

### 5.2.2.4 Sample Daily Summary

The sample daily summary for UPS, shown in Figure 71, highlights the exceptional consistency of CNG performance on a dedicated high-elevation interstate lane. Daily mileage clustered tightly between 676 and 699 miles across all 13 active days — a narrower band than any other fleet in the Run — reflecting a standardized route design where freight volume, driver scheduling, and lane structure are precisely aligned. Efficiency proved equally repeatable: representative days like September 25 reached 7.17 mi/DGE under favorable conditions, while even the lower end of the range on days like September 19 stayed above 6.6 mi/DGE, all while pulling triple trailers at combination weights exceeding 100,000 lbs. across corridors ranging from 1,600 to 6,600 ft. in elevation.

The modest day-to-day variation — an 18% to 19% spread from best to worst — is driven by wind, weather, and minor traffic differences rather than any inconsistency in the CNG powertrain or fueling process. The daily summary table in Figure 71 underscores that, for a fleet running dedicated corridor linehaul at scale, CNG delivers the kind of predictable, shift-after-shift performance that lets dispatchers plan around freight commitments rather than fuel uncertainty.

### 5.2.2.6 Speed & Efficiency

UPS’s CNG T680s spend about 90% of its miles above 60 mph, and that band delivers the best efficiency at roughly 7.2 mi/DGE, slightly above the fleet average of 6.7 mi/DGE. The 50 to 60 mph band covers only about 6% to 7% of distance and runs closer to 5.2 mi/DGE, reflecting segments where posted speeds, congestion, or grades pull the truck off optimum cruise.

At the low end, speeds below 40 mph make up only approximately 2% of distance but show much poorer fuel economy — down in the 2.4 to 4.0 mi/DGE range — as stop–start behavior, idling at lights, and low-gear operation dominate. The combination of high average speed, high-elevation corridors, and strong mi/DGE at great than 60 mph reinforces that UPS is using CNG in a genuinely demanding interstate application, not a gentle urban or mixed cycle. In practice, the duty cycle keeps the engine in its highway efficiency sweet spot almost all day, which is exactly where natural gas linehaul can be most competitive.

UPS’s CNG T680 runs in genuine mountain-basin territory. Operations stretch from roughly 1,600 ft. to over 6,600 ft. with a mean elevation around 4,660 ft., and cumulative climb and descent each exceeded 270,000 ft. over the Run. Less than half of the distance is on near-flat grades (−1% to +1%), and the rest is split between descending and climbing segments that reflect repeated ascents and descents on western interstate corridors.

Efficiency is strongest on moderate descents (about 18% of distance) where mi/DGE climbs into the mid-7s, and on steep descents where similar gains are seen despite engine-braking needs. On flat high-plains freeway, the truck delivers around 6.6 mi/DGE, essentially defining the baseline for this high-elevation corridor. Climbing segments tell the other half of the story. Moderate grades around 1% to 3% knock efficiency down into the high 5s, and the approximately ~9% of miles on greater than 3% grades pull efficiency to roughly 5.1 mi/DGE as the X15N works against both gravity and heavier-than-typical GCWs.

Taken together, the terrain profile confirms that UPS’s CNG operation is not a low-elevation test case — it is a high-elevation, rolling-to-mountainous corridor where vertical work is a material part of fuel consumption. The fact that the fleet still delivers around 6.71mi/DGE in this environment, with triple trailers and long daily distances, underscores how much duty-cycle matching and operational discipline matter in natural-gas linehaul.

### 5.2.2.8 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for the UPS CNG Kenworth T680 that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Dedicated lane discipline drives exceptional consistency. Daily miles clustered within an approximately 20-mile band across the entire Run, which is rare even in highly optimized long-haul operations. That consistency indicates tightly defined lanes, predictable freight flows, and dispatch practices built around repeatable turns rather than ad-hoc assignments.

Natural gas efficiency holds up at altitude when duty cycle is right. Operating around 4,600 ft. average elevation, the turbocharged Cummins X15N retained full power while benefiting from reduced aerodynamic drag versus sea-level operations. Efficiency remained strong because the duty cycle is dominated by steady interstate cruise, keeping the powertrain on a predictable operating curve most of the time.

Highway-optimized duty cycle maximizes CNG/RNG strengths. With roughly 90% of miles above 60 mph and minimal low-speed exposure, the UPS operation is almost a textbook case for where CNG linehaul works best — long, steady runs where aerodynamics, gearing, and disciplined speed control matter more than transient stop-and-go behavior.

Triples at high GCW provide a stringent test. Regular operation above 100,000 lbs. GCW means every climb is done with more mass than typical US single-trailer operations. The achieved 6.7 mi/DGE shows that, in the right application, natural-gas tractors can shoulder heavier-than-standard combinations without collapsing efficiency.

Idle control and schedule reliability go hand in hand. Low idle percentages reflect both driver discipline and a network where gate queues, sort delays, and congestion are managed carefully. For natural gas, minimizing idle helps preserve the economic advantage of lower fuel cost per unit energy.

Range is a tool, not a constraint, in this application. With a theoretical range well above 1,000 miles per fill, UPS chooses to refuel daily at the home base rather than pushing tanks to their limit. That approach simplifies operations and keeps range out of the day-to-day dispatch conversation even in a CNG context.

I'm happy to drive this truck because the performance and the longevity equal what the diesels provide." — Gene Horne, Driver, UPS

Terrain-aware network design matters. While the corridor is undeniably mountainous in elevation terms, interstate highways naturally follow basins and passes that balance climbs and descents. This characteristic of Western interstate design helps keep efficiency penalties manageable for CNG operations on major freight arteries.

CNG/RNG supports a broader fleet-level sustainability strategy. At this Salt Lake hub, CNG tractors and CNG-powered package cars together represent a significant share of UPS’s on-road assets. The T680’s performance shows that natural gas can carry a substantial fraction of regional linehaul work on predictable routes, not just niche or pilot lanes.

### 5.2.2.9 UPS Summary

UPS’s CNG/RNG Kenworth T680 demonstrates that natural-gas powertrains can support high-elevation, high-speed interstate linehaul with heavy triple-trailer combinations when the duty cycle and network are designed around their strengths. Despite operating between roughly 1,600 and 6,600 ft. and pulling GCWs above 100,000 lbs., the truck delivered about 6.7 mi/DGE over more than 9,000 miles, with daily distance and efficiency clustered into narrow, repeatable bands.

The operation benefits from almost ideal conditions for CNG — a dedicated lane, strong site infrastructure, minimal idle, and a highway-dominant speed profile that lets the X15N work at steady load for most of the day. Terrain still imposes a cost — climbing segments pull efficiency down into the 5 to 6 mi/DGE range — but careful routing keeps the time spent on the steepest grades limited, and long descents provide partial compensation even without regenerative braking.

In the broader Messy Middle context, UPS’s results illustrate where CNG and RNG are most compelling — high-volume, corridor-style linehaul where infrastructure can be concentrated, routes are predictable, and the fleet can operate natural-gas equipment at scale. For fleets with similar profiles, this case suggests that natural gas is not only a viable alternative to diesel, but a proven, repeatable option for demanding western interstate service.

### 5.2.3 Wegmans — CNG/RNG Peterbilt 567

Wegmans Food Markets operated a Peterbilt 567 day cab powered by CNG/RNG in heavy regional grocery distribution service from its Rochester, New York base. The tractor was equipped with a Cummins X15N 15-liter natural gas engine, Allison 4000 automatic transmission, and a Cummins 175 DGE Gen 3 back-of-cab fuel system. The unit hauled dry van and refrigerated trailers in double 53 ft. configurations, using nearby public CNG infrastructure rather than a private depot station. This deployment represents a mature CNG powertrain in everyday grocery freight, not a demonstration loop. See Figure 76.

### 5.2.3.3 Operational Metrics

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for Wegman’s Peterbilt 567 that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Duty Cycle — Heavy regional doubles in grocery distribution. Wegmans deployed the CNG Peterbilt 567 on regional return-to-base grocery distribution routes, hauling double 53’ refrigerated and dry van trailers with very heavy GCW. The duty cycle combined structured store replenishment, regional transfers, and occasional extended corridors, all anchored to nightly returns to the Rochester distribution center. This is a demanding, real-world grocery profile rather than a lightly loaded trial.

Speed Distribution — Highway-dominant, not urban. About 90% of miles occurred above 50 mph, with nearly 77% above 60 mph, confirming that the CNG tractor spent most of its time on freeways and major highways. Only a small fraction of distance was logged in the 0 to 25 mph band, covering yard work, local store access, and steeper highway grade transitions. This profile plays to CNG’s strength in steady-state cruise rather than stop-and-go urban delivery.

Distance & Utilization — Wide envelope, with true high-mile days. Over 12 active days the truck covered roughly 5,400 miles, averaging about 450 miles per active day. Daily miles ranged from a few miles on partial or

---

<!-- page: 149 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026 Page 149

repositioning days to more than 900 miles on the longest recorded days. Most operational days fell into the 400- to 650-mile envelope typical of single-driver regional work, with the highest-mile days indicating multi-segment routes or refueling en route.

Payload & GCW — Very heavy doubles work. Average freight moved was on the order of 55,000 to 60,000 lbs., with GCW frequently landing in the 120,000 to 140,000 lbs. range when pulling double 53’ trailers. At these weights, the CNG X15N was working at the top of its intended envelope, yet drivers reported hill-climb performance very similar to comparable diesel doubles. Both powertrains pulled steep grades steadily, with speeds dropping into the mid-20 mph range but without loss of lane capability or excessive performance complaints. See Figure 80.

Speed-Based Efficiency — Clear highway sweet spot. Efficiency peaked in the 50 to 60 mph band at roughly 5.8 to 5.9 mi/DGE, about 15% to 20% above the fleet average, reflecting the CNG powertrain’s sweet spot when aerodynamic drag and engine load are well balanced. At freeway speeds above 60 mph, the fleet still held around 5.1 mi/DGE, indicating that the Peterbilt 567 and X15N combination can sustain typical highway cruise speeds without a sharp efficiency penalty. In contrast, the 0 to 25 mph band delivered roughly 1.9 mi/DGE, illustrating how yard work, steep ramp approaches, and local store access drive down fuel economy when the truck is repeatedly accelerating a heavy double combination.

Terrain & Elevation — Rolling, hilly New York, not flat Midwestern baseline. The CNG tractor operated at relatively low absolute elevations (roughly 200 to 1,030 ft.) but in terrain that included a meaningful mix of rolling and hilly segments. Moderate grades were common and steep grades, while a small fraction of total distance, carried noticeable efficiency penalties. Interviews indicated that hills did not cause operational problems; instead, they behaved much like diesel — climbs were steady but slower under maximum GCW.

Idle Time — Moderate but managed. Idle time was on the order of 4% of engine-on hours, driven mainly by loading/unloading, staging at the distribution center, and store-access dwell. This represents reasonable idle discipline for a regional grocery operation, though opportunities remain for further improvement via tighter dock scheduling or idle-reduction strategies.

Refueling & Range — Daily work within a comfortable PMR buffer. With a 175 DGE onboard capacity and observed mid-4s mi/DGE, theoretical range is around 850 miles per full fill. Allowing for a 10% to 15% operational reserve yields a practical PMR in the 700- to 800-mile range. Most daily assignments (400 to 650 miles) fit comfortably inside that envelope, and the nearby commercial CNG station allowed refueling to be integrated into normal staging and pre-trip windows rather than driving route design. See Figure 81.

PMR — Practical maximum range for this duty cycle. Based on fuel capacity and observed efficiency, Wegmans’ CNG Peterbilt had a practical maximum range of roughly 700 to 800 miles per full fill with reasonable reserve. In everyday operations, that provided an ample buffer to complete regional grocery doubles routes without range becoming a binding operational constraint.

### 5.2.3.4 Sample Daily Summary

Daily miles varied from roughly 220-mile mid-length turns to full 900+ mile days combining multiple route segments, yet mi/DGE remained within a recognizable band across the mix. Higher-efficiency days — such as the 465-mile run on September 21 at 5.19 mi/DGE — reflect favorable routing and lighter congestion, while lower-performing days like September 23 at 3.91 mi/DGE on similar distance point to the influence of terrain, traffic, and wind conditions rather than any shortfall in the CNG powertrain.

### 5.2.3.6 Speed & Efficiency

Wegmans’ CNG tractors spend about 90% of their miles above 50 mph, with nearly 77% above 60 mph, confirming a highway-dominant regional RTB duty cycle rather than stop-and-go urban delivery. Efficiency is

---

<!-- page: 153 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

weakest in the 0 to 25 mph band (approximately 1.9 mi/DGE), where yard work, steeper access ramps, and local store approaches drive short, inefficient moves. As speeds rise into the 40 to 60 mph range, mi/DGE climbs into the 5.1 to 5.8 band, reflecting steadier cruise and fewer interruptions. At freeway speeds above 60 mph, Wegmans still maintains roughly 5.1 mi/DGE even under heavy doubles GCW, which is consistent with a well-spec'd CNG tractor, disciplined driving, and relatively moderate New York highway grades.

Taken together, the speed profile shows that this application is using CNG in exactly the environment where it performs best — extended, predictable highway runs with limited time spent in low-speed congestion. The results also highlight how quickly efficiency collapses when heavy CNG doubles are forced into stop-and-go movement — reinforcing the case for keeping this technology on highway-dominant lanes.

Figure 85: Wegmans’ percent of miles in various terrain types

Terrain & Efficiency: The Wegmans operation runs in moderately varied New York terrain with an elevation range of about 253 m (approximately 830 ft.) and a mean around 160 m (approximately 524 ft.), typical of rolling hills and river valleys rather than flat Midwestern plains or extreme mountain passes. Cumulative climb and descent are almost perfectly balanced, confirming that the route set cycles up and down rather than pushing the truck in one direction over long climbs.

Just over half of all operations (approximately 53.5%) occur on flat or near-flat segments between −1% and +1% grade at about 4.6 mi/DGE — essentially the fleet average. Moderate grades, both up and down, account for roughly 30% of driving and perform at or slightly above average: modest climbs (1% to 3%) run close to 5.0 mi/DGE and modest descents (−3% to −1%) reach around 5.2 mi/DGE. These bands represent the “normal” rolling work of upstate New York and show that CNG remains viable so long as grades are not sustained and extreme.

Steep grades — both up and down — are a small but important part of the story. They represent only about 5.4% of operations but carry penalties of 11% to 22% relative to the fleet average. Climbs above 3% grade fall to around 3.8 mi/DGE, and steep descents show lower apparent mi/DGE as engine braking replaces throttle and no regenerative recovery is available. This confirms that while the CNG tractor can pull the hills at heavy GCW with diesel-like performance, there is a substantial fuel cost penalty in those segments.

---

<!-- page: 155 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

Overall, the terrain results place Wegmans in a middle ground — more challenging than the flat diesel baseline represented by fleets like Albert, but less extreme than high-elevation Western corridors. In that context, achieving roughly 4.6 mi/DGE with very heavy doubles confirms that CNG can be a workable option in rolling territory, provided route design avoids long runs on the steepest grades.

### 5.2.3.8 Operational Insights

Below are some insights NACFE gained from analyzing data on the Wegmans truck that participated in the Run.

**CNG can carry heavy doubles in hilly regional work.** Operating at GCW often in the 120,000 to 140,000 lbs. range with double 53’ trailers, Wegmans demonstrated that a CNG-powered Cummins X15N engine can handle demanding grocery doubles work in rolling and hilly terrain. Drivers consistently described hill performance as comparable to diesel, with acceptable but slower climbs on the steepest segments.

**Highway-dominant duty cycle is a good fit for CNG.** With roughly 90% of miles above 50 mph and nearly 77% above 60 mph, the duty cycle plays to CNG’s strengths in steady-state cruise. The limited exposure to low-speed, stop-and-go operation helps contain the efficiency penalties that CNG experiences when repeatedly accelerating high-GCW combinations.

> **Figure — Photograph of Dan Farley**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Photograph of Dan Farley, Transportation Team Leader, Wegmans, alongside a quote: "We always want to lead the charge on doing the right thing. We want to be an innovator, not a follower. Our alternative fuel initiative is a great example. We see an opportunity to be sustainable, and we try to be the first ones on board." — Dan Farley, Transportation Team Leader, Wegmans

**Speed management offers efficiency upside but has real-world constraints.** The clear efficiency peak around 50 to 60 mph (approximately 5.8 to 5.9 mi/DGE) suggests that modest speed reductions from higher cruise bands can deliver measurable fuel savings. In practice, however, grocery operations must balance these gains against delivery windows, store schedules, and driver productivity expectations that often favor sustained 60 to 65 mph cruising.

**Terrain matters, but route design keeps it in check.** The terrain analysis shows that Wegmans’ New York corridors are not flat, but they avoid extended exposure to the steepest grades. Most miles are on flat to moderate grades where efficiency stays near or above the fleet average. This reinforces the value of route planning that steers heavy CNG doubles toward corridors with short, manageable climbs, even if total distance is slightly higher.

**Public CNG infrastructure can work when it is close and reliable.** Instead of a private depot station, Wegmans relied on a nearby commercial CNG station located close to the Rochester distribution center. Fast-fill refueling

March 15, 2026 Page 155

---

<!-- page: 156 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

was integrated into existing staging and pre-trip windows, so adding CNG did not fundamentally change the shape of the workday. This experience suggests that for regional RTB operations, a well-located public station can be sufficient if it is close, reliable, and sized for heavy truck throughput.

**Idle performance is reasonable but improvable.** An idle share of around 4% is respectable for regional grocery work but still represents an area for incremental improvement. Better alignment between dock scheduling, staging, and driver breaks could shave idle time further, contributing small but meaningful fuel savings over a large annual mileage base.

**Range is comfortably above daily requirements.** With a practical PMR in the 700- to 800-mile range and typical daily assignments in the 400- to 650-mile band, the CNG system provides ample headroom. This margin gives dispatch flexibility to accommodate detours, weather, or traffic without turning range into an operational constraint, as long as refueling at the nearby station remains reliable.

**Technology positioning — strong in this niche, not universal.** Wegmans’ results highlight where CNG is well aligned — highway-dominant regional haul with predictable routes, nearby CNG infrastructure, and corporate sustainability goals. At the same time, the relatively modest mi/DGE compared with optimized diesel underscores that CNG is not a universal replacement; instead, it is a targeted solution that works best when duty cycle, geography, and infrastructure are deliberately matched.

Wegmans’ CNG Peterbilt 567 ran a classic regional grocery pattern — structured store routes, refrigerated doubles, and nightly returns to a distribution center just minutes from a commercial CNG fast-fill station. In that world, range was rarely the limiting factor. Some days were short, focused on partial routes or repositioning; others stretched into the 600- to 900-mile band with multi-segment runs. In both cases, the truck returned to base, refueled at the nearby station, and rejoined the normal distribution rhythm.

The routes crossed both flat interstate stretches and the hilly terrain of upstate New York, including grades that pushed a fully loaded double combination down into the mid-20 mph range on the steepest climbs. From the driver’s perspective, the CNG tractor behaved much like a diesel in those conditions. It might not charge up the hill, but it pulled steadily and predictably. Variations in daily mi/DGE were driven more by route choice, traffic, weather, and stop count than by any single hill.

Because the fast-fill station sits close to the distribution center, refueling was simply folded into existing staging and pre-trip windows rather than added as a separate operational burden. For similar regional RTB duty cycles, the Wegmans experience suggests that the hard work lies in choosing the right public station and aligning routes to it — not in worrying whether a properly spec’d CNG tractor can cover the miles.

### 5.2.3.9 Wegmans Summary

Wegmans’ CNG deployment shows that a modern natural-gas tractor can handle demanding regional grocery doubles work when duty cycle, geography, and infrastructure are aligned. The Peterbilt 567 with Cummins X15N engine operated at very heavy GCW in rolling and hilly New York terrain, delivered roughly 4.6 mi/DGE, and kept daily range well within a comfortable 700- to 800-mile practical window. For this fleet, CNG delivered diesel-like operational performance with lower fuel cost and a meaningful emissions benefit, particularly when paired with RNG.

At the same time, the results underline CNG’s boundaries. Efficiency penalties at low speeds and on steep grades remain significant, and the technology depends on reliable, well-placed infrastructure in a way that diesel

March 15, 2026 Page 156

---

<!-- page: 157 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

does not. Range was not the constraint in this application; station access and duty-cycle fit were. Wegmans’ experience suggests that CNG is best viewed as a targeted tool for highway-dominant regional haul rather than a universal replacement for diesel.

Within the broader Messy Middle, Wegmans provides a useful benchmark — a high-GCW, double-trailer grocery operation using CNG/RNG in everyday service, not a controlled trial. The lesson is that when fleets can match natural-gas equipment to highway-dominant routes, rolling rather than mountainous grades, and dependable fueling, CNG can play a durable role in regional freight decarbonization — especially as RNG availability grows.

### 5.2.4 CNG/RNG Cross-Fleet Summary

The three CNG/RNG fleets — Wegmans, UPS, and Kleysen — represent a remarkably wide span of real-world natural-gas use cases, ranging from double-53’ grocery trailers at 120,000 to 140,000 lbs. GCW, to triple-trailer high-elevation interstate linehaul, to heavy regional haul across Canadian prairies and foothills. Together, they demonstrate that modern natural-gas tractors can reliably support heavy freight with high daily productivity, predictable range, and stable efficiency when duty cycles align with the platform’s strengths.

Efficiency outcomes across the fleets were shaped far more by geography, grade exposure, and GCW than by engine technology. Wegmans, the heaviest of the three, delivered roughly 4.6 mi/DGE while regularly pulling double 53’ trailers through New York’s rolling and occasionally steep terrain. See Figure 86.

March 15, 2026 Page 157

---

<!-- page: 158 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

The fleet feedback and performance achieved by the new 15-liter natural gas engine highlights this as an excellent choice that competes well with equivalent diesels in difficult, heavy-haul duty cycles.

High-speed stability emerged as a defining strength across all three fleets. Wegmans kept about 90% of its miles above 50 mph, UPS operated with 94% above 55 mph, and Kleysen ran almost 80% above 60 mph, confirming that CNG/RNG performs best when duty cycles favor steady-state cruise. Efficiency in the 50 to 60 mph band was consistently the strongest, and performance at greater than 60 mph remained remarkably competitive — even under high GCW — thanks to modern aerodynamics, powertrain controls, and disciplined speed management.

Terrain produced the largest efficiency swings, most notably under very heavy GCW. Wegmans experienced efficiency as high as approximately 5.3 mi/DGE on flat ground and as low as the mid-2s under steep grades with 140,000 lbs. GCW. UPS, operating at altitude with substantial climb/descent exposure, showed a wider terrain spectrum yet maintained tight daily efficiency thanks to predictable corridors and disciplined cruise. Kleysen’s prairie and foothill environment produced a clear terrain gradient as well: strong performance on flat segments (approximately 4.7 mi/DGE) and softer values on 3% to 6% grades where gravitational load became dominant.

Payload sensitivity was more pronounced under CNG than under diesel. All three fleets operated well above 100,000 lbs. GCW, and performance differences closely tracked weight and grade combinations. Wegmans’ extremely heavy doubles, UPS’s triples, and Kleysen’s dense regional loads each demonstrated that CNG can support high GCW work, but with efficiency penalties that rise more quickly than in diesel when terrain steepens.

MPG is important in freight hauling, but a more telling metric is freight-ton-MPG — the amount of freight carried times the fuel economy. A single 80,000 lbs. GVW truck at 11 MPG carrying 35,000 lbs. of freight achieves approximately 192 ton-MPG. A 140,000 lbs. GVW double 53’ rig carrying 64,000 lbs. of freight at 6 MPG achieves the same 192 ton-MPG — demonstrating that these heavy combinations deliver equivalent freight efficiency despite lower mi/DGE figures.

Operational discipline played an outsized role. UPS, for example, delivered 13 consecutive days between 676 and 699 miles, one of the tightest daily envelopes in the entire Run, highlighting the impact of fixed lanes, consistent grade cycles, and well-positioned CNG/RNG infrastructure. Wegmans benefited from a public fast-fill station located minutes from its Rochester depot, allowing heavy double-trailer operation without range constraints. Kleysen relied on public fast-fill sites along prairie corridors with strong speed stability, and its results reflected the benefits of a predictable, high-speed regional loop.

Collectively, these three fleets demonstrate that CNG/RNG is a mature, reliable, and highly capable regional and linehaul technology when matched to the right corridors. Its strengths — steady highway efficiency, 700- to 800-mile practical range, fast refueling, and the ability to run on renewable natural gas — are most fully realized in predictable routes with moderate terrain and high-speed cruise. Its constraints — greater sensitivity to steep grades, heavy urban stop-and-go, and infrastructure location — are manageable with route selection and corridor planning. For fleets operating in suitable lanes today, these results show that CNG/RNG offers a realistic, immediately deployable pathway to lower-carbon freight while maintaining diesel-like productivity and uptime.
