## 5.3 Battery Electric Vehicle Fleets

Four battery electric fleets participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle across five distinct operational deployments, collectively logging more than 15,000 validated miles. Four different BEV platforms — Freightliner eCascadia, Volvo VNR electric, Tesla Semi, and the Windrose sleeper — tackled duty cycles ranging from urban multi-stop distribution to genuine long-haul corridor freight. These were not controlled demonstration runs on hand-picked routes. Drivers moved commercial freight on regular schedules, encountered real traffic and weather, carried payloads ranging from roughly 38,000 lbs. to more than 80,000 lbs., and charged when infrastructure and operational dwell time allowed rather than when conditions were ideal.

The operational envelope varied significantly by platform and application. Nevoya’s 438 kWh Freightliner eCascadia averaged about 173 miles per active day in Central Valley regional distribution, operating primarily within a practical 150 to 200 mile single-charge range on flat-to-rolling terrain when payloads and traffic were favorable. Saia’s first Tesla Semi deployment worked Northern California LTL routes, averaging roughly 80 miles per active day with dense urban and terminal activity and substantial idle time, using its large battery primarily for operational flexibility rather than maximum daily range. Saia’s second Tesla Semi — representing the 500-mile configuration — operated as a true long-haul asset, averaging about 370 miles per active day with multiple days above 500 miles and a validated 660-mile maximum, enabled by strategic mid-route DC fast charging along established corridors.

4Gen’s 565 kWh Volvo VNR electric ran regional drayage and distribution in Southern California’s Inland Empire, averaging approximately 200 miles per active day while demonstrating both shorter urban duty cycles and extended days above 380 miles when mid-route charging was available. JoyRide’s 705 kWh Windrose sleeper delivered the longest BEV performance in the Run, with an 875-mile validated maximum and an average of roughly 380 miles per active day across Western highway corridors, approaching conventional diesel single driver-day capabilities when charging and duty cycle aligned.

What emerges from these five deployments is a technology at an inflection point. Battery capacities spanning roughly 438 to 700+ kWh create fundamentally different operational envelopes — not incremental improvements, but categorical differences in which duty cycles become viable. In practice, operational single-charge ranges clustered around 150 to 200 miles for mid-pack regional platforms and extended into the 350- to 450-mile band for the largest packs under favorable conditions and disciplined planning. Strategic placement of high-power DC fast charging along freight corridors proved as important as battery size.

The difference between a 200-mile regional asset and a truck capable of 400- to 600-mile days often depended on whether charging was available at natural dwell points rather than on peak nameplate range. Terrain, speed profiles, payload mix, and idle energy use drove efficiency variation — and therefore range — well beyond the spread seen in diesel MPG, reinforcing the need for route-specific validation before making planning assumptions.

The data in this section documents both BEV successes and boundaries. Uptime remained high across fleets, with no evidence in the validated periods that reliability, rather than operations, dominated deployment decisions. Regenerative braking recovered a meaningful share of traction energy — typically 9% to 20% depending on duty cycle, terrain, and speed profile — but did not eliminate the energy penalties of steep grades, heavy payloads, or extended high-speed operation. Charging coordination proved manageable when infrastructure aligned with existing freight patterns, yet the longest days universally required deliberate mid-route charging integrated with driver breaks and terminal schedules.

March 15, 2026 Page 159

---

<!-- page: 160 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

Performance proved sensitive to factors that diesel fleets often treat as secondary — grade profiles that barely register in diesel fuel economy can create substantial BEV range impacts, payload shifts can drive tens of miles of range difference, and the gap between manufacturer range statements and real-world operational results remain significant.

Taken together, these four fleets, four platforms, and five distinct operational stories provide concrete planning parameters for where battery-electric technology fits today. In corridors with favorable terrain, sufficient infrastructure, and duty cycles matched to each platform’s operational envelope, BEVs are already a practical option for drayage, many regional applications, and emerging long-haul use cases. Where routes involve sustained grades, sparse infrastructure, extreme climates, or very high daily mileage expectations, additional planning — and, in some cases, additional technology development — will be required before BEVs can fully replace diesel.

### 5.3.1 4Gen Logistics

4Gen Logistics operated a 2024 Volvo VNR Class 8 battery-electric day cab on regional drayage and distribution routes across Southern California’s Inland Empire, serving the Los Angeles metro area and surrounding communities. The VNR electric used in the Run featured the six-pack battery configuration with approximately 565 kWh of installed capacity powering a dual-motor electric drivetrain through Volvo’s integrated 2-speed I-Shift gearbox in a 6×2 configuration. The tractor was equipped with a day cab roof fairing, cab extenders, aerodynamic hood mirrors, an external sun visor, and an aero bumper, along with a full suite of advanced driver assistance systems. **Distance and utilization:** 2,612 miles over 13 active days (approximately 200 to 205 miles per active day), with days ranging from short urban assignments to extended runs above 380 miles.

**Energy efficiency:** Fleet-level efficiency approximately 0.623 mi/kWh (approximately 1.605 kWh/mi), with daily performance spanning roughly 0.37 to 0.66 mi/kWh depending on route mix, traffic, and terrain.

**Speed profile:** Mixed profile with a majority of miles at 40 to 60 mph but a significant amount at 0 to 40 mph, reflecting both freeway drayage and urban/surface-street work in the LA Basin and Inland Empire.

**Regenerative braking:** Regen share typically ranges between approximately 11% to 20% overall, rising on higher-grade segments and downhill returns from inland climbs as the electric VNR recovered energy on descents.

**Terrain exposure:** Approximately 79% of miles on 0% to 3% grades and approximately 21% on 3%+ grades, with elevation gains exceeding 10,000 to 24,000 ft. per 100 miles in the moderate and steep bands, respectively.

**Charging and infrastructure:** About 0.73 charge events per 100 miles (one per approximately 137 miles), using depot and corridor DC fast charging integrated with port and DC dwell time rather than dedicated “charging-only” trips.

**Operating windows:** Multi-shift capability with days spanning from compact shifts to long windows combining drayage, regional moves, and repositioning.

**Probable Maximum Range (PMR):** Practical single-charge range with reserve is approximately 180 to 220 miles on typical port-to-inland duty cycles, with extended days above approximately 300 to 380 miles clearly requiring planned mid-route charging.

### 5.3.1.7 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of operational insights about the 4Gen electric Volvo VNR that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

**Drayage and regional suitability.** 4Gen’s deployment shows that a 565 kWh VNR Electric can handle demanding drayage and regional work in the Inland Empire when routes are built around return-to-base patterns and corridor charging nodes.

> **Figure — Photograph of Brad Bayne**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Photograph of Brad Bayne, VP of strategic Initiatives, Duncan Trucking, alongside a quote: "With EVs we're probably in the same range of what our diesel fleet would be as far as the actual fuel costs go." — Brad Bayne, VP of strategic Initiatives, Duncan Trucking

**Freeway plus urban mix.** The combination of freeway links and surface-street congestion drives a wide efficiency band, underscoring that route mix within a day matters as much as total mileage for BEV planning.

**Terrain as a real cost driver.** Climbs out of the basin and into foothills produce high elevation gain per 100 miles and noticeably lower mi/kWh, while downhill segments recover only part of that cost even with strong regen.

**Regen as a partial offset.** Regenerative braking’s 11% to 20% contribution helps tame the energy cost of repeated climbs and stop-and-go work but does not fully erase the penalty of steep or prolonged grade exposure.

**Charge-event density and port workflows.** A charge roughly every 130 to 140 miles proved workable because charging could be folded into port queues, DC dwell, and shift transitions instead of requiring separate charging legs.

March 15, 2026 Page 167

---

<!-- page: 168 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

**Battery-size trade-offs.** The 565 kWh pack provides enough buffer for 200-mile days in complex terrain without daily “range anxiety,” but extended days above that threshold still rely on reliable corridor charging.

**Multi-shift flexibility.** Variable operating windows demonstrate that BEVs can support more than a simple eight-hour shift model when infrastructure and dispatch are aligned with equipment capabilities.

**Corridor selection within one region.** While flat, free-flowing routes yield the best absolute kWh/mi, BEVs hold a comparative advantage over diesel in stop-and-go traffic and variable-grade corridors where regenerative braking recovers energy that conventional trucks would lose to brake heat and idle fuel burn. Fleet planners may find that assigning BEVs to these duty cycles — rather than reserving them for the "easiest" routes — maximizes the technology's value relative to diesel alternatives.

**Good-case vs realistic-case range.** Best-day performance around 0.66 mi/kWh illustrates the “ceiling” under ideal route and traffic combinations, while typical days remind planners to size range expectations to mixed, not idealized, conditions.

**Evidence of operational maturity.** The lack of chronic downtime in the validated window and seamless integration into normal freight flows indicate that, for 4Gen, the VNR Electric is behaving as a mainstream asset rather than a pilot-only experiment.

### 5.3.1.8 4Gen Logistics Summary

4Gen’s Volvo VNR Electric deployment demonstrates that a mid-pack battery-electric platform can support demanding regional drayage and distribution in a mixed urban, freeway, and foothill environment when supported by appropriate infrastructure. The fleet’s performance — 2,612 miles at approximately 0.53 mi/kWh, with a probable single-charge range of 180 to 220 miles and extended days above 380 miles using mid-route charging — provides a concrete example of BEV viability in one of North America’s most complex logistics corridors. The results underscore that matching duty cycle, terrain, and charging access is essential: where those factors align, regional BEVs can operate as working assets rather than experimental pilots.

### 5.3.2 JoyRide — Windrose Technologies Sleeper

JoyRide operated a Windrose battery-electric Class 8 sleeper tractor on long-haul regional and corridor routes across the Western US. The truck used a 705 kWh Li-Iron Phosphate (LFP) battery pack in an 800 V architecture, powering a high-output permanent magnet synchronous motor through an integrated drivetrain. The tractor was equipped with a single-bunk sleeper, camera mirror system, comprehensive aerodynamic treatments, and dual HVAC systems for cab and sleeper. See Figure 95.

The Windrose sleeper operated across Western interstate corridors from South Texas to the Los Angeles Basin and Northern California, crossing the Southern Rockies, Mojave Desert, Sierra Nevada, and Grapevine Pass. The route spanned elevations from roughly -98 ft. to 4,990 ft., with multiple days reaching peaks above 4,000 ft. and daily elevation gains often exceeding 10,000 ft. to 15,000 ft. While interstate design keeps most grades below 3%, the cumulative climbing on this cross-country route was substantial — making the BEV’s performance more notable, not less.

During the Run, JoyRide’s BEV recorded 14 active days totaling 5,725 miles. The truck averaged approximately 409 miles per active day (median approximately 400 miles), with multiple days above 400 miles and a validated maximum of 875 miles. The sleeper operated as a working long-haul/regional asset, carrying payloads between approximately 39,700 and 81,600 lbs., with a significant share of miles near the high end of the GVW envelope. Charging relied exclusively on high-power DC fast charging at public corridor locations.

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

JoyRide’s Windrose truck operated across Western interstate corridors from South Texas to California, crossing the Southern Rockies, Mojave Desert, Sierra Nevada, and Grapevine Pass — terrain that included sustained climbs into the 4,000 ft. to 5,000 ft. range and daily elevation gains often exceeding 10,000 ft. to 15,000 ft. Elevation ranged from roughly -98 ft. to 4,990 ft., with mean altitude around 1,600 ft. The grade distribution shows approximately 76% of miles on grades below 1% and approximately 96% below 3%, but this reflects interstate highway design rather than easy terrain — even significant mountain passes involve long approaches at gentle grades, so the share of miles on steep grades remains small even on demanding routes.

Flat terrain (0% to negative grades) showed strong efficiency (approximately 0.558 mi/kWh) with modest elevation gains (approximately 1,574 ft. per 100 miles) and moderate regen (approximately 12.8%). Light rolling terrain (1 to 3% grades, approximately 20% of miles) maintained similar efficiency (approximately 0.535 mi/kWh) despite higher elevation gains (approximately 8,700 ft. per 100 miles), indicating manageable grade variations rather than sustained climbs.

Only approximately 4% of miles occurred on grades above 3%. Moderate grades (3% to 6%) showed continued workable efficiency (approximately 0.542 mi/kWh) but with very high elevation gains (approximately 20,900 ft. per 100 miles) and elevated regen (approximately 35%), suggesting balanced climb/descent pairs. Steep segments (6% or greater, approximately 0.7% of miles) exhibited severe efficiency penalties (approximately 0.315 mi/kWh) despite maximum regen (approximately 36%) and massive elevation gains (approximately 44,500 ft. per 100 miles), emphasizing the energy intensity of sustained mountain climbing.

The grade distribution data — with most miles on gentle grades — reflects interstate highway design standards rather than easy terrain. The route crossed multiple mountain ranges including climbs out of the Mojave, the approach to Las Vegas from the Hoover Dam corridor, passes through New Mexico, and the Grapevine into California’s Central Valley. Daily elevation gains of 10,000 to 15,000+ ft. on the cross-country legs demonstrate that this was genuine mountain freight, not a cherry-picked flat corridor. The Windrose sleeper’s ability to execute 400 to 875 mile days on this route — including the 875-mile maximum with more than 15,000 ft. of climbing — shows that large-capacity BEVs can handle real western interstate terrain when charging infrastructure and duty cycles are aligned. Routes with even more sustained grades (higher percentage of miles above 6%) would require additional planning, but this deployment demonstrates BEV capability well beyond flat-terrain regional work.

### 5.3.2.7 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational insights gained from analyzing data on the JoyRide Windrose truck that were tracked during Run on Less – Messy Middle.

**Long-haul range capability.** JoyRide’s 875-mile maximum and approximately 409-mile average demonstrate that 700+ kWh BEVs can approach diesel-like single-driver days when routes, charging, and duty cycles are carefully matched.

**Highway-optimized platform.** With approximately 84.5% of miles above 50 mph and stable efficiency around 0.57 to 0.58 mi/kWh at higher speeds, the Windrose platform clearly prioritizes aerodynamic and drivetrain performance for sustained highway use.

**Terrain and corridor selection.** Operating across Western interstates from South Texas to California — including the Southern Rockies, Mojave climbs, Sierra Nevada, and Grapevine — the Windrose sleeper demonstrated BEV capability on genuine cross-country freight routes. While interstate design keeps most instantaneous grades

March 15, 2026 Page 176

---

<!-- page: 177 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

below 3%, cumulative daily elevation gains of 10,000 to 15,000+ ft. confirm this was not a flat-corridor demonstration.

**Charging tempo and productivity.** A 0.44 charge-event density and favorable charge-to-drive ratio indicate that high-power charging and partial-charge strategies can be integrated without overwhelming daily duty cycles.

**LFP chemistry benefits.** The 705 kWh LFP pack brings thermal stability and cycle-life advantages that support deeper discharges and repeated long days without the same degradation concerns as smaller NMC-based packs.

> Type: photograph
> Axes: none

**SOC strategy in practice.** Operating with mean SOC in the low-60s and occasionally dipping very low on extended days reflects a balanced approach that preserves reserve most of the time while exploiting full capacity when needed.

**Payload realism.** A GCVW range up to approximately 81,500 lbs. confirms that observed performance is based on real long-haul freight, not lightly loaded demonstration work.

**Efficiency variation and planning.** Day-to-day swings between roughly 0.46 and 0.67 mi/kWh show that even highway-dominant BEVs require conservative planning buffers and route-specific validation.

**Regen in highway applications.** Regen’s approximately 15% contribution is helpful but secondary in this highway-heavy cycle, reinforcing that long-haul BEV success hinges more on aero, speed, and charger placement than on braking recovery.

**Sleeper configuration advantages.** The sleeper layout and bunk HVAC support longer-duration duty cycles and flexible rest during charging or overnight stops, making this configuration more naturally suited to long-haul BEV work than day-cab designs.

### 5.3.2.8 JoyRide Summary

JoyRide’s Windrose sleeper shows that large-capacity BEV platforms can compete with diesel in long-haul corridor service across genuine Western interstate terrain — including crossings of the Southern Rockies, Mojave Desert, Sierra Nevada, and Grapevine Pass. The Run’s 5,725 miles at approximately 0.545 mi/kWh, average approximately 409-mile days, and an 875-mile maximum (with over 15,000 ft. of elevation gain)

demonstrate that BEV technology can reach into operational spaces once assumed to be diesel-only. The results confirm that success depends on adequate corridor charging infrastructure and highway-dominant duty cycles, but they also show that “favorable terrain” for BEVs now includes cross-country routes that would have seemed out of reach just a few years ago.

### 5.3.3 Nevoya — Freightliner eCascadia

Nevoya operated a Freightliner eCascadia Class 8 battery-electric day cab on regional distribution routes in California’s Central Valley. The eCascadia used in the Run employed a 438 kWh usable battery capacity powering dual Detroit eAxles (425 to 470 HP configurations) in a 6×4 configuration. The truck was equipped with dual CCS charging ports for high-power DC fast charging, along with day cab roof fairing, cab side extenders, drive wheel aero covers, aero mirrors, and Right Side Guard Assist blind spot collision avoidance radar. See Figure 103.

> **Figure 103 — Nevoya’s Freightliner eCascadia at their Central Valley distribution facility**

Following an early LA-based deployment that required a mid-run vehicle transition, the validated period for this analysis reflects operations across the Stockton–Modesto–Turlock corridor, with route extensions to Merced and Fresno. The truck operated largely on flat valley-floor terrain between –16 and 279 ft. elevation along I-5, CA-99, I-580, and CA-120, serving agricultural, DC-to-DC, and regional freight flows.

Nevoya joined the demonstration late in the study window, with validated telemetry spanning September 21 through October 9 — capturing the final days of the official Run period plus continued operation afterward. Over this extended window, the eCascadia recorded 13 active days with daily mileage ranging from short urban assignments to extended regional runs above 300 miles. The truck functioned as a regular work asset, carrying commercial payloads averaging around 63,000 lbs., with peak loads near 79,500 lbs. Charging relied on high-power DC fast charging at the depot and selected public sites, using the dual CCS ports and integrating charging into natural operational dwell.

### 5.3.3.4 Sample Daily Summary

The sample daily summary for Nevoya illustrates how a 438 kWh eCascadia performs across the range of regional distribution assignments in California's Central Valley. Daily mileage ranged from short urban shuttles of roughly 70 miles to extended corridor runs approaching 350 miles, with fleet-level efficiency averaging approximately 0.60 to 0.67 mi/kWh on the flat-to-rolling terrain that defines the Stockton–Modesto–Fresno corridor. The highest-efficiency days combined steady 50 to 60 mph freeway cruise on I-5 or CA-99 with moderate payloads and minimal congestion, while lower-efficiency days reflected heavier loads, more stop-and-go exposure at distribution centers, or occasional route extensions into foothill terrain. See Figure 107.

Across the validated period, the pattern confirms that the eCascadia functions as a productive daily asset when routes stay within the 150- to 200-mile single-charge envelope and charging integrates naturally into loading and staging dwell. Extended days above 250 miles were achievable but required deliberate charging coordination. For fleet planners considering regional BEV deployment, Nevoya's daily spread shows that Central Valley terrain is near-ideal for mid-pack battery platforms — and that predictable route structures, more than raw battery capacity, determine day-to-day operational success.

Speed band variability analysis segments each fleet's total miles into standardized speed ranges (0 to 25, 25 to 40, 40 to 50, 50 to 60, and > 60 mph) and measures efficiency within each band. This reveals how sensitive each powertrain is to its operating speed profile. See Figure 108.

Nevoya’s eCascadia operation is strongly freeway-centric, with more than 80% of miles in the 50 to 60 mph band as the truck shuttles along Central Valley corridors. This plays directly to BEV strengths — steady-state cruising at moderate speeds is where the dual Detroit eAxle drivetrains operate most efficiently, with approximately 0.674 mi/kWh in the primary highway band. Lower-speed urban operation represents a small share of total miles but a much larger share of regenerative opportunity, especially at 0 to 25 mph where regen share approaches 30%. Overall energy performance is dominated by highway efficiency rather than regen recovery.

### 5.3.3.6 Terrain & Elevation

Nevoya operates in a predominantly low-elevation Central Valley corridor, with nearly 90% of miles on grades below 3%. This terrain profile supports strong overall efficiency (approximately 0.60 to 0.67 mi/kWh in these bands) with elevation gains of roughly 1,200 to 4,600 ft. per 100 miles. The valley is not perfectly flat, but it avoids the sustained mountain grades that would severely impact BEV range.

Overall (driving with GPS): The Nevoya truck had total driving miles of approximately 2,231 miles with an elevation range of approximately 0 ft. to 295 ft. (mean approximately 82 ft.), 9.8% regeneration and 0.538 mi/kWh. See Figure 109.

| Grade Band | % Distance | Elevation Gain<br>per 100 miles | Avg<br>mi/kWh | Avg<br>Regen % |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Flat (0% to 1%) | 48% | 1,539 ft. | 0.597 | 6.2% |
| Light Rolling<br>(1% to 3%) | 41.5% | 4,585 ft. | 0.571 | 9.5% |

The remaining approximately 10% of miles on steeper grades (3% to 6% and 6% or greater) reveal BEV behavior on more challenging terrain. These segments show elevation gains of 10,000 to 23,000 ft. per 100 miles, with efficiency falling into the 0.38 to 0.61 mi/kWh range while regen percentages climb to 14% to 19%. Regen partially offsets climbing energy but does not fully compensate. Net energy consumption still rises substantially on steep grades.

The Central Valley’s flat-to-gently-rolling profile provides near-ideal terrain for the eCascadia’s capabilities. Most energy is spent at steady highway speeds with modest elevation change, enabling predictable range and high utilization. On steeper segments, the combination of increased energy use and higher regen underscores that route selection and grade profiles must be considered when planning BEV deployments beyond the valley floor.

### 5.3.3.7 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational insights gained from analyzing data from the Nevoya eCascadia that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

**Central Valley corridor fit.** Nevoya’s results show that a 438 kWh eCascadia can comfortably support regional distribution in flat-to-rolling Central Valley terrain when daily ranges stay within a 150- to 200-mile envelope and charging is available at natural dwell points.

**Highway-dominant efficiency.** With approximately 80% of miles at 50 to 60 mph, energy use is driven by steady freeway cruising where the Detroit eAxle powertrain operates efficiently and range planning becomes highly predictable.

**Payload-sensitive range.** The approximately 25% efficiency spread between light and heavy payload days translates into approximately 40 miles of range difference, making payload-aware dispatch and route planning more important for BEVs than for comparable diesel operations.

March 15, 2026 Page 184

---

<!-- page: 185 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

**Charging integrated into workflow.** A roughly 1:1 charge-to-drive time ratio reflects charging that is largely absorbed into loading, staging, and break windows rather than creating distinct “charging shifts” separate from freight work.

**High SOC operational strategy.** Maintaining SOC mostly above 80% while occasionally dipping into single digits reflects a range-confidence approach prioritizing operational readiness over battery longevity optimization—the truck stays topped up and ready for any dispatch need, supported by confidence in local charging access.

> **Figure — Photograph of Ivan Torres**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none
> Series: none
> Values: none
> Notes: Photograph of Ivan Torres, Driver, Nevoya, alongside a quote: "The electric truck is smooth and quiet. I like not stopping for fuel—just plug in at the yard and go." — Ivan Torres, Driver, Nevoya

**Terrain as a quiet enabler.** Operating with nearly 90% of miles on grades below 3% keeps gravitational penalties modest, allowing the 438 kWh pack to behave like a “regional workhorse” rather than being consumed by repeated long climbs.

**Limited but meaningful regen.** Regen’s approximately 9% contribution helps on ramps and grade breaks but, in a freeway-heavy application, remains a secondary lever behind basic kWh/mi efficiency for determining practical daily range.

**Charge-event density as an infrastructure signal.** The 0.76 charge events per 100 miles provide a concrete planning benchmark — regional corridors need chargers roughly every 100 to 150 miles to support similar operations without forcing out-of-route detours.

**Evening-heavy operations and logistics.** Nevoya’s evening-weighted mileage shares align BEV operations with agricultural and distribution center schedules as well as potentially favorable power-cost windows, showing that electrification can mesh with existing logistics rhythms.

**Transition resilience.** The mid-run equipment and location change that limited the total active days underscores that fleet-level resilience — backup units, redundant sites, flexible routing — is as important as vehicle reliability for BEV deployment.

### 5.3.3.8 Nevoya Summary

Nevoya’s eCascadia deployment shows that a 438 kWh regional BEV can operate as a productive asset in a highway-focused Central Valley corridor when supported by sufficient charging infrastructure and favorable

### 5.3.4.4 Sample Daily Summary

The sample daily summary for Saia's urban P&D Tesla Semi captures the stop-dense, dwell-heavy character of less-than-truckload city operations. Daily mileage ranged from roughly 47 to 130 miles across 13 active days, with validated efficiency spanning a wide band of approximately 0.45 to 0.80 mi/kWh. The best-efficiency days reflected favorable routing with a higher share of freeway connector segments and less terminal dwell, while the lowest-efficiency days correlated with dense urban stop sequences, extended dock time, and short-distance repositioning between customer sites where fixed energy overheads — HVAC, power steering, air compressor — consumed a disproportionate share of the energy budget. See Figure 115.

Despite the modest daily mileage, the Tesla Semi's large battery pack meant that range was never a binding constraint; the truck typically returned to the terminal with substantial state-of-charge remaining. Regenerative braking contributed approximately 18 to 29% of traction energy on representative days, with the strongest recovery on stop-dense urban routes where frequent deceleration and ramp approaches provided sustained regenerative opportunity.

For LTL operators evaluating BEV suitability, Saia's P&D deployment demonstrates that battery-electric trucks can absorb the inefficiencies inherent in multi-stop urban freight without range anxiety, provided that depot charging is available at the home terminal.

Saia’s Tesla Semi operation shows a distinctly mixed-duty profile characteristic of regional LTL distribution. While highway operation (50 to 60 mph) represents the largest single category at nearly 40% of miles, almost half of all miles occur at urban and surface street speeds below 40 mph, reflecting the multi-stop nature of LTL operations. These driving-specific efficiencies are higher than the overall fleet efficiency once idle energy is included, underscoring the impact of dwell on total energy use.

5.3.4.6 Terrain & Elevation

Overall, Saia 1 operates on exceptionally flat terrain in the Northern California corridor, with more than 90% of miles on grades below 1% and an elevation range of roughly 0 to 157 ft. (mean approximately 4 ft.). Average elevation gain is modest — only around 1,000 ft. per 100 miles across all operations, and roughly 585 ft. per 100 miles in the flat band.

Small shares of distance occur on ramps and localized grades (1% to 3% and above), where elevation gain per 100 miles jumps into the five-figure range and regen share rises into the mid-teens. These segments illustrate how BEV efficiency responds sharply once grades increase, despite meaningful regen recovery.

Saia’s Tesla Semi operates in an exceptionally flat corridor, representing near-ideal BEV conditions. The minimal elevation change means gravitational work is limited and energy use is dominated by speed profile, dwell, and payload rather than sustained climbing. When grades do increase, efficiency penalties are pronounced even though regen contribution rises. For this deployment, the terrain profile helps explain why the Tesla Semi can maintain reasonable energy performance despite carrying a large battery. Mountain passes and sustained climbs would show very different results and would require more conservative range planning.

Saia 1 operates on exceptionally flat terrain in the Northern California corridor, with over 87% of miles on grades below 1% and an elevation range of 0 to 157 ft. (mean approximately 33 ft.). Small shares of distance occur on ramps and localized grades (1% to 3% and above), where elevation gain per 100 miles jumps into the four-to-five-figure range and regen share rises sharply into the 40 to 50% range. These segments illustrate how BEV efficiency responds sharply once grades increase, despite meaningful regen recovery. The high regen percentage in the 1% to 3% band reflects the frequent braking cycles inherent to LTL multi-stop operations rather than sustained downhill running.

Saia’s Tesla Semi operates in an exceptionally flat corridor, representing near-ideal BEV terrain conditions. The minimal elevation change means gravitational work is limited and energy use is dominated by speed profile, dwell time, and payload rather than sustained climbing. The relatively high overall regen share (27.8%) reflects the stop-and-go nature of LTL multi-stop distribution — frequent braking at terminals and customer stops — rather than terrain-driven regeneration. When grades do increase, efficiency penalties are pronounced even

5.3.4.8 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational insights gained from analyzing the data from the Saia Tesla Semi (#1) that were tracked in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

LTL duty-cycle suitability. The 79-mile average and approximately 130-mile maximum per active day show that a large-battery BEV can comfortably cover regional multi-stop LTL routes where range is rarely the limiting factor.

Efficiency and idle-heavy context. The approximately 0.569 mi/kWh fleet result reflects a duty cycle where idle energy and low-speed work weigh heavily, implying that more highway-oriented use of the same truck would likely produce higher overall efficiency.

> **Figure — Photograph of Matt Copot**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: n/a
> Series: n/a
> Values: n/a
> Notes: “We evaluate what works from a business case perspective, where technologies exist and how we can leverage those, particular emerging technologies, and how we can help our customers work toward reducing mileage as well as increasing utilization.” — Matt Copot, VP Fleet Management, Saia

Battery capacity as operational buffer. An estimated 800 to 900 kWh pack functioned more as a comfort margin and scheduling tool than a strict range limiter, with most days using only a modest share of available energy.

Charging within normal workflows. Charging was largely absorbed into existing dwell at depots and facilities, with occasional “out-of-route” trips to Tesla chargers behaving much like diesel detours to fuel stops.

SOC strategy and confidence. Operating in the mid-SOC bands with little time above 80% reflects confidence in local infrastructure and dispatch discipline, not a need to keep the truck constantly near full.

Evening-weighted operations. An evening-heavy mileage pattern matched customer receiving windows and lighter traffic, while also creating opportunities to align charging with off-peak or lower-cost hours.

Multi-stop idle realities. The 2.2:1 idle-to-drive ratio reinforces that for BEV LTL work, dock time and stop density must be explicitly modeled in energy budgets rather than treated as negligible.

Speed profile and range levers. The contrast between efficient 50 to 60 mph segments and energy-intensive urban work shows why BEV range planning must start with speed and stop patterns, not just distance.

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

Flat terrain as a silent advantage. The very flat Bay Area-Central Valley corridor minimizes climbing penalties, allowing speed and payload to dominate energy use in a way that would not hold in hillier territory.

Regen as a supporting actor. Roughly 20% regen contribution is meaningful but not decisive, underscoring that the primary path to better performance remains reducing unnecessary stop-and-go and idle time.

Utilization as a reliability signal. Two-thirds calendar utilization with no persistent outage patterns shows the Tesla Semi functioning as a normal working asset, not a fragile pilot truck.

5.3.4.9 Saia 1 Summary

Saia’s regional Tesla Semi deployment shows battery-electric Class 8 tractors can effectively support LTL distribution routes with 50- to 130-mile daily ranges, heavy terminal activity, and significant idle time. The Run’s 1,025-mile dataset demonstrates a fleet efficiency of approximately 0.569 mi/kWh, average daily range of 79 miles, and meaningful regenerative recovery, supported by depot-based charging integrated into natural dwell time. For fleets with similar flat terrain, cube-limited freight, and access to high-power depot charging, this deployment provides quantified parameters for realistic BEV adoption in regional LTL service.

5.3.5 Saia 2 — Tesla Semi

Saia’s second deployment used a Tesla Semi Class 8 battery-electric day cab in a 500-mile configuration on long-haul routes in Northern California, connecting terminal operations across the Bay Area and Central Valley. The truck used a tri-motor electric drivetrain and 6×4 configuration, supported by a large battery pack estimated in the 800 to 900 kWh range and a highly aerodynamic cab. Although Saia’s LTL network predominantly uses double-trailer configurations, at the time of the Run the Tesla Semi was approved to pull single 53 ft. trailers only. See Figure 118.

Duty cycle and geography: Long-haul terminal-to-terminal LTL linehaul in Northern California, connecting Bay Area and Central Valley terminals across flat-to-rolling interstate corridors.

Distance and utilization: 4,841 miles over 13 active days (approximately 372 miles per active day, approximately 457-mile median), with several days above 500 miles and a validated maximum of 660 miles.

Saia 2 operated as a true long-haul application, with more than 70% of miles at 50 to 60 mph on interstate corridors that include significant grades and elevation changes. A smaller share of miles occurs at low speeds in terminal, urban, and approach segments, where regen provides nearly one-fifth of traction energy. 5.3.5.6 Terrain & Elevation

Saia 2’s route features flat-to-rolling terrain with meaningful but not extreme elevation change. Nearly half of miles occur on grades above 1%, with moderate (3% to 6%) and steep (6% or greater) segments representing a small share of total distance but accounting for a large share of elevation gain. These bands show elevation gains above 10,000 ft. to 29,000 ft. per 100 miles, consistent with long climbs and extended downgrades along mountain-influenced corridors.

Efficiency in the steepest band remains in the mid-0.3 to low-0.4 mi/kWh range while regen share approaches 20%, demonstrating that the Tesla Semi can recover substantial energy on extended descents but cannot fully offset the energy cost of sustained climbs. The observed performance matches driver feedback that wind and terrain conditions noticeably influence SOC and range.

5.3.5.7 Terrain & Grade Summary

Saia 2’s route set features flat-to-rolling terrain with meaningful but not extreme elevation change. Nearly 67% of miles occur on grades above 1%, a notably higher share than Saia 1’s regional LTL operation, consistent with the longer interstate linehaul corridors that connect NorCal terminals. Moderate (3% to 6%) and steep ($\ge$6%) segments represent smaller shares of total distance but account for a large share of elevation gain, with the

Below is a summary of the operational insights obtained by analyzing data from the Saia Tesla Semi 2 that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

> **Figure — Photograph of Dan Priestley**
> Type: photograph

Long-haul viability demonstration. Saia’s 372-mile average and 660-mile maximum validate that large-pack BEVs can support genuine long-haul linehaul when corridors, charging, and duty cycle are aligned.

Battery capacity as a step change. The estimated 800 to 900 kWh pack transforms the use case from regional only to long-haul-capable, enabling many 400-mile legs on a single charge with reserve.

Highway-centric efficiency. With roughly two-thirds of miles at 50–60 mph, energy use is dominated by steady cruising where truck aerodynamics and motor efficiency carry most of the performance burden.

Strategic corridor charging. A low charge-event density of 0.35 per 100 miles implies corridor charging roughly every 250 to 350 miles, placing a premium on well-sited high-power stations along primary freight routes.

Assertive SOC utilization. Operating with mean SOC in the high-40s and limited time near full reflects intentional use of the battery’s working range to maximize daily productivity. See Figure 125.

Saia’s second Run on Less – Messy Middle deployment confirms that a large-pack Tesla Semi configured for 500-mile range can support genuine long-haul service when duty cycles, terrain, and charging infrastructure are aligned. The 4,841-mile dataset — with average 372-mile days, multiple 500+ mile days, a 660-mile maximum, and fleet efficiency around 0.530 mi/kWh — demonstrates that BEVs can take on long-haul work that was previously considered diesel-only. Battery capacities ranged from 438 kWh (Nevoya’s Freightliner eCascadia) to an estimated 800 to 900 kWh (Saia’s Tesla Semi configurations), enabling duty cycles from regional distribution to genuine long-haul — but only where duty cycles, terrain, and infrastructure were aligned. Battery management strategies varied:
● Nevoya and 4Gen maintained high SOC bands, often above 70% to 80%, prioritizing operational readiness.
● Saia 1 and Saia 2 adopted deeper cycling approaches (mean SOC in the mid-40s) to maximize usable range.
● JoyRide’s LFP-based sleeper used a moderate SOC band, balancing LFP’s tolerance for deeper cycling with long-haul needs.

5.3.6.1 What Worked Well

What follows is a look at what worked well for the BEVs that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle including the impact of terrain, charging strategies and regenerative braking.

Highway-focused duty cycles on favorable terrain: Where routes combined flat-to-rolling topography and sustained highway speeds, BEVs delivered predictable and competitive performance:
• Regional platforms (438 to 565 kWh) clustered around 0.53 to 0.60 mi/kWh.
• Long-haul platforms (705 to 900 kWh) achieved 0.52 to 0.56 mi/kWh on typical days, with best days approaching or exceeding 0.65 to 0.70 mi/kWh.
• Corridors like the Central Valley, Inland Empire, and Western basins and plateaus provided the most favorable conditions — modest elevation changes and high highway percentages.

Strategic charging integrated with real operations: When chargers were placed at depots, terminals, and natural break points, fleets held charge-to-drive ratios to manageable levels. In these cases:
• Most charging occurred during loading/unloading, driver breaks, or shift changes, not as separate “stop just to charge” events.
• High-power corridor charging at 250- to 300-mile intervals enabled the longest days for Saia 2 and JoyRide without requiring wholesale schedule redesign.

Regenerative braking as a meaningful but secondary contributor: Regen typically contributed approximately 9% to 20% of traction energy, peaking in urban and steep-grade segments and moderating total energy use on rolling corridors. However, regen did not erase the energy penalties of heavy payloads, steep grades, or long high-speed runs. Steady-state highway efficiency remained the dominant determinant of daily range.

5.3.6.2 Challenges & Constraints

The following is a look at some of the challenges and constraints of moving to BEVs including range, terrain, infrastructure, and weight.

Range & variability vs. diesel: Despite impressive maximum single-day performances (660 to 875 miles), average daily ranges were more modest:
● Regional BEVs averaged approximately 170 to 200 miles per active day.
● Long-haul BEVs averaged approximately 370 to 380 miles per active day.

Day-to-day efficiency variability within a single fleet was substantial — often 40% to 60% from best to worst days — driven by payload, speed profile, congestion, weather, and route mix. This is much greater variability than typical diesel fuel economy (10% to 20%), and it requires larger planning buffers for BEVs.

Terrain sensitivity: All deployments deliberately avoided sustained mountain passes. While 80% to 96% of miles occurred on grades below 3%, the limited exposure to steeper grades (6% or greater) showed large efficiency

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 206

---

<!-- page: 207 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

penalties, even with high regen. Sustained climbs can reduce efficiency by more than half relative to flat segments, significantly shortening range and requiring more conservative planning. See Figure 129.

> **Figure 129 — Grade Impact on BEV Efficiency Efficiency Drops 40-50% on Steep Grades**
> Type: bar chart
> Axes: Grade Band vs Efficiency (mi/kWh)
> Series: Nevoya, 4Gen, Saia 1, Saia 2, JoyRide
> Values: Flat (0-1%): Nevoya approx 0.45, 4Gen 0.59, Saia 1 0.46, Saia 2 approx 0.69, JoyRide approx 0.70; Light Rolling (1-3%): Nevoya 0.28, 4Gen 0.19, Saia 1 0.37, Saia 2 approx 0.49, JoyRide approx 0.79; Moderate (3-6%): Saia 2 0.23, JoyRide approx 0.49; Steep ($\ge$6%): Saia 2 0.25, JoyRide approx 0.49
> Notes: Efficiency declines with increasing grade. Figure 129: Grade impact on BEV efficiency

All deployments deliberately avoided sustained mountain passes. While 80% to 96% of miles occurred on grades below 3%, the limited exposure to steeper grades (6% or greater) showed large efficiency penalties, even with high regen. Sustained climbs can reduce efficiency by more than half relative to flat segments, significantly shortening range and requiring more conservative planning.

Infrastructure dependence & regional limits: Every extended-range day (300 to 875 miles) depended on mid-route DC fast charging. Observed charge event densities (0.35 to 0.88 per 100 miles) translate to infrastructure needs of:
● 100- to 150-mile intervals for regional deployments, and
● 200- to 300-mile intervals for long-haul BEVs.

Today, this level of corridor coverage is primarily available in parts of California and selected Western corridors; it is not yet typical for many rural or cross-country routes.

Weight and payload trade-offs: Large battery packs (700 to 900 kWh) carry meaningful weight penalties relative to diesel tractors, reducing payload capacity by several thousand pounds if no weight exemptions are available. Cube-limited freight can absorb this penalty; dense commodities near 80,000 lbs. GVW cannot without operational adjustments (smaller loads, more trips, or different equipment strategy).

5.3.6.3 BEV Key Takeaways

After analyzing the data, the NACFE team determined key takeaways about BEVs.

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 207

---

<!-- page: 208 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

1. BEVs are already a practical fit in specific, well-defined applications. The most successful deployments share common traits — flat-to-rolling terrain, return-to-base or corridor operations, moderate daily ranges, cube-limited freight, and access to depot and corridor fast charging. In these conditions, BEVs functioned as working assets, not demonstration trucks.

2. Route-specific validation is essential. The large day-to-day swings in efficiency and range mean operators cannot rely simply on fleet averages or manufacturer claims. Extended pilots on target routes, with verified telematics data, are necessary to establish realistic PMR bands and to understand how payload, speed, and terrain interact in practice.

3. Infrastructure planning is a critical prerequisite for deployment. Successful deployments matched charger placement and power to existing freight patterns. Regional BEVs need dependable access at depots, DCs, and urban nodes. Long-haul BEVs need ultra-high-power charging at 200- to 300-mile intervals along freight corridors. Without this planning, even the best vehicles face tight range constraints.

4. Battery size is a strategic choice, not a simple race to the largest pack. Smaller packs (approximately 440 to 565 kWh) worked well for 150- to 220-mile regional applications with return-to-base operations. Larger packs (approximately 700 to 900 kWh) enabled 350- to 450-mile legs and occasional 500- to 875-mile days, but at higher weight and capital cost. The “right” battery size depends on route structure, payload mix, infrastructure, and business objectives. There is no single universal optimum.

5.3.7 BEV Cross-Fleet Insights

Run on Less – Messy Middle BEV deployments show that Class 8 battery-electric trucks can successfully support commercial freight operations today — but in carefully chosen applications with appropriate infrastructure and realistic expectations. The most compelling results came from corridors and duty cycles intentionally matched to BEV strengths. The same vehicles would face tighter constraints on mountain routes, in infrastructure-poor regions, or in applications that require the full payload and daily range flexibility of diesel.

For fleet operators, the validated data provide both encouragement and caution. BEVs are ready for drayage, many regional distribution routes, and emerging long-haul applications in corridors with the right terrain and infrastructure. They are not yet drop-in replacements for diesel everywhere. Success will require starting with favorable applications, conducting rigorous route-specific validation, building or accessing suitable charging infrastructure, and planning for variability rather than assuming diesel-like uniformity in range.

5.4 Hydrogen Fuel Cell Vehicle (FCEV) Fleets

Hydrogen fuel cell technology is in the early stage of Class 8 commercialization, yet its operational promise is already evident. Two hydrogen fleets — Penske and Pilot — participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle, collectively accumulating 5,897 validated miles across 30 active days.

Both deployed Hyundai XCIENT fuel cell Class 8 tractors — among the first FCEV Class 8 trucks in commercial service in North America — across two sharply different environments: the flat, overnight regional corridors of Dallas-Fort Worth and the diverse freight corridors of California. The XCIENT platform was designed and optimized for urban and regional duty cycles. It has not been tested or proven for North American long-haul operations where sustained high-speed highway running; plus 600+ mile daily productivity are baseline expectations.

March 15, 2026
NACFE
NORTH AMERICAN COUNCIL FOR FREIGHT EFFICIENCY
Page 208

---

<!-- page: 209 -->

Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

The XCIENT's 56 mph governed speed — a design choice consistent with regional optimization — means drivers can cover approximately 560 miles in a 10-hour HOS window, compared to 650 to 700 miles at typical diesel highway speeds. Both Run deployments operated the XCIENT within duty cycles reasonably aligned with its design intent, though Pilot's extended corridor operations occasionally approached the platform's operational limits.

These were not controlled demonstrations. Both fleets ran real freight, on commercial schedules, with commercial payloads, using developing hydrogen infrastructure rather than idealized test conditions. The two deployments provide early but meaningful evidence of where hydrogen fits today, how it performs under real operational variability, and what infrastructure and economic barriers remain.

Pilot's California corridors delivered 7.80 mi/kg $H_2$ with a wide $\pm$17% daily variation reflecting real-world terrain, grade, and traffic. Daily distances averaged 272 miles per day, with a maximum of 427 miles. Operations spanned elevations from -33 ft. to 1,640 ft., with significant periods at highway speeds (61% at 50 to 60 mph), and meaningful time on 3% to 6% and 6% or greater grades.

Penske's Texas regional operation produced a more stable efficiency band at approximately 7.5 mi/kg, averaging 121 miles per day across 15 days. Nearly 90% of miles were run at 50 to 60 mph on flat terrain with an elevation range of only 374 ft. to 863 ft., providing an ideal profile for assessing hydrogen performance without topographic distortion.

The common lesson: hydrogen FCEVs can deliver 350 to 450 miles of single-tank practical ranges, offer significantly faster refueling than battery-electric alternatives, and sustain full-day schedules without the charging dwell constraints of BEV long-haul platforms. But both fleets also confirmed that infrastructure availability, hydrogen cost, and throughput constraints (mobile or fixed) remain the limiting factors — not the vehicle technology itself.

The truck hauled commercial automotive and retail freight on tightly grouped corridors, running predictable third-shift operations with limited route variation.

5.4.1.3 Operational Metrics

Below is a summary of the operational metrics that were tracked for Penske’s Hyundai XCIENT hydrogen fuel cell truck that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Duty Cycle: Highly structured overnight RTB freight with repetitive corridors. This predictability simplifies hydrogen consumption planning and ensures stable daily operations.

Distance & Utilization: Daily mileage was tightly clustered (approximately 100 to 160 mi), enabling consistent hydrogen budgeting and minimal on-route refueling risk.

Efficiency (mi/kg $H_2$): The truck delivered approximately 7 to 8 mi/kg across the Run, driven primarily by flat terrain and disciplined freeway speeds.

Payload Context: Though precise GVW data were not logged, Penske confirmed that loads were commercial freight, not reduced-weight demos.

Idle Time: Idle share below 3%; fuel cell systems incur minimal hydrogen penalty while idling, unlike diesel.

Refueling Strategy: Mobile 700-bar fast refueling required coordinated planning across hydrogen delivery, cooldown, and dispatch — but performed reliably with rapid fills once aligned.

PMR Practical Range: Usable hydrogen mass (68.6 kg) and observed efficiency yield a PMR of approximately 350 to 450 miles, offering surplus range relative to the actual duty cycle.

5.4.1.4 Sample Daily Summary

The sample daily summary for Penske reveals the most operationally consistent fleet in the Run. Daily mileage was tightly clustered between roughly 100 and 160 miles across 15 active days, with validated hydrogen efficiency ranging from approximately 6.8 to 9.6 mi/kg. This narrow mileage band reflects the structured, return-to-base nature of Penske's automotive-parts delivery operation out of Lancaster, Texas — routes that repeat with high predictability day after day. Efficiency variation within that band was driven primarily by minor differences in traffic density, wind exposure, and loading configuration rather than by route or terrain changes, since virtually all miles occurred on the same flat-to-gently-rolling Dallas-area corridors. See Figure 134.

The XCIENT's governed 56 mph top speed, combined with Penske's disciplined overnight schedule, produced a remarkably stable speed profile — nearly 89% of miles in the 50 to 60 mph band. This consistency translated into predictable hydrogen consumption that simplifies refueling logistics, a critical consideration when relying on mobile 700-bar hydrogen delivery. For fleet operators evaluating early hydrogen deployments, Penske's daily pattern demonstrates that structured RTB corridors with predictable mileage, stable terrain, and coordinated refueling represent the operational sweet spot where current FCEV platforms deliver reliable, repeatable performance.

5.4.1.7 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational insights gathered from analyzing data from Penske’s fuel cell truck that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Duty-cycle fit: Hydrogen performance aligned well with predictable overnight RTB corridors requiring minimal route variability.

Mobile-refueler coordination: Reliable but required tight synchronization with hydrogen delivery schedules and cooldown cycles.

Hydrogen supply logistics: Throughput and delivery cadence shaped fueling availability more than onboard range.

Vehicle reliability: No stack derates, temperature faults, or compressor issues were reported by the fleet throughout the Run.

Driver experience: Smooth torque, low noise, and familiar handling; governed top speed required route planning.

Low-speed penalties: Yard segments showed outsized hydrogen use per mile in tight maneuvering.

Thermal stability: Mild Texas temperatures enabled stable compressor and stack thermal control; no heat-related derates.

Economics: Hydrogen cost-per-mile exceeded diesel and CNG; cost — not vehicle capability — is the primary barrier.

Scalability: Single mobile dispenser adequate for one truck but not for multi-unit scaling.

Lesson learned: Successful hydrogen deployment relies on integrated planning between fleet, station operator, and dispatch as much as vehicle readiness.

5.4.1.8 Penske Summary

Penske's Hyundai XCIENT deployment demonstrates hydrogen's viability in structured regional corridor operations. Consistent 7 to 8 mi/kg efficiency and substantial range headroom confirm technical feasibility. At the same time, infrastructure availability, fuel economics, and vehicle cost remain the primary barriers to scaling hydrogen in similar duty cycles.

Terrain Influence: Operation included foothill approaches and transitions with grades up to and above 6%, resulting in significant hydrogen consumption penalties.

5.4.2.4 Sample Daily Summary

The sample daily summary for Pilot captures the widest efficiency range among the hydrogen fleets — and among all non-BEV participants in the Run. Daily mileage ranged from short 84-mile partial days to extended 427-mile corridor runs, with validated hydrogen efficiency spanning roughly 6.2 to 10.8 mi/kg. This $\pm$38% variation around the 7.80 mi/kg fleet average was driven overwhelmingly by terrain: California corridor routes that included sustained foothill climbs and grade transitions produced significantly higher hydrogen consumption per mile, while favorable flat-to-rolling segments on I-15 and I-10 corridors yielded efficiency well above the fleet average. See Figure 142.

Unlike Penske's tightly clustered RTB operation, Pilot's duty cycle demanded genuine route variety — multiple customers, varying distances, and mixed terrain — making it a more demanding test of the XCIENT platform's real-world capabilities. For fleet planners, Pilot's daily pattern illustrates that hydrogen works well in mixed regional corridors but that route-level energy budgeting — accounting for specific grade profiles rather than flat-terrain assumptions — is essential for reliable daily planning.

Approximately 82% of Pilot’s miles occurred on grades below 3%, producing extremely stable efficiency. Moderate grades (3% to 6%) reduced mi/kg due to increased vertical work, while grades of 6% or greater were rare but hydrogen-intensive. The terrain confirms a flat-to-light-rolling corridor — ideal conditions for early-stage hydrogen evaluation. These bands delivered strong efficiency (8 to 10 mi/kg).

The remaining miles included foothill approaches and pass-adjacent routes, where grades ranged from 3% to 6% and greater than 6%. These segments produced significant hydrogen penalties, dropping efficiency into the 3 to 7 mi/kg range depending on load and duration. The terrain profile shows hydrogen's capability in diverse California-grade environments while also illustrating the cost of sustained climbs.

5.4.2.7 Operational Insights

Below is a summary of the operational insights gained from analyzing data from Pilot’s Hyundai XCIENT fuel cell truck that participated in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

Corridor suitability: Hydrogen matched California's mixed corridor profile well, delivering consistent long-haul productivity when infrastructure was accessible.

Fixed-station advantages: The fixed 700-bar station provided stable pressure and predictable cooldown cycles, enabling highly repeatable fast refueling.

Supply-window sensitivity: Two non-operational days tied to hydrogen supply timing — not vehicle reliability — underscored infrastructure dependency.

Vehicle reliability: No stack derates, compressor faults, or thermal-management issues were reported by the fleet during the Run.

Driver feedback: Drivers highlighted predictable refueling, quiet operation, and strong highway-grade performance.

> **Figure — Photograph of Bill Zobel**

How the other technologies progress, how hydrogen progresses, how quickly the price of the fuel can come down, what customers really think of the trucks and their ability to fit their total cost of ownership.” — Bill Zobel, Director Alternative Fuels, Pilot

Terrain influence: Efficiency dropped 25% to 60% on steep or sustained grades. Hydrogen planning must consider California's topographic variability.

Thermal stability: Mild seasonal temperatures supported steady stack cooling. No heat-related derates were reported by the fleet.

Hydrogen cost: Delivered cost-per-mile was significantly higher than diesel or electricity, even with strong efficiency days.

Operational flexibility: Hydrogen enabled diesel-like scheduling flexibility without BEV charging dwell time.

Speed: The XCIENT speed limiter being set at 56 mph was not an issue in California operations where maximum highway speed for trucks is posted as 55 mph.

Lesson learned: Hydrogen viability depends on infrastructure availability, supply timing, and route topology just as much as on vehicle capability.

5.4.2.8 Pilot Summary

Pilot's deployment demonstrates that hydrogen fuel-cell trucks can deliver diesel-like daily ranges, rapid refueling, and strong performance across varied terrain when corridor infrastructure is in place. Efficiency ranged from 6.19 to 10.96 mi/kg, reflecting both the diversity of California routes and the sensitivity of hydrogen to terrain and traffic. While vehicle reliability and operational throughput were strong, the Run highlighted that hydrogen's commercial viability depends primarily on fuel cost, station density, and supply-chain resilience, not on the vehicle technology itself.

Commercial scale: Both fleets represent pilot-scale deployments using the only commercially available FCEV Class 8 platform in North America.

Range & performance: Hydrogen delivered 350- to 450-mile PMR, confirming diesel-like day range under both stable terrain (TX) and rolling/foothill terrain (CA).

Refueling: Both fleets achieved rapid refueling, which is the strongest operational advantage hydrogen holds over BEV long-haul applications.

Refueling methodology: Both fleets used fast fill hydrogen refueling, demonstrating diesel-like scheduling flexibility. Timed fills — dispensing hydrogen over several hours at lower cost — were not used during the Run but could offer economical overnight refueling for depot-based operations like Penske's RTB corridors.

Terrain sensitivity: Penske's stable flat terrain produced narrow efficiency bands (approximately 7 to 8 mi/kg), while Pilot's mixed grades produced a broad efficiency spread (6 to 11 mi/kg).

Infrastructure dependency: Penske relied on a single mobile refueler, while Pilot depended on California's limited fixed-station corridor — both situations highlight hydrogen's vulnerability to infrastructure throughput and supply timing.

Driver experience: Drivers in both fleets consistently cited smooth torque delivery, low noise, and familiar handling.

Reliability: Together the fleets reported no stack derates, no compressor faults, and minimal maintenance — strong evidence of early mechanical maturity.

5.4.3.1 Hydrogen Cross-Fleet Lessons Learned

Hydrogen fuel cell vehicles demonstrated strong technical capability under real freight conditions — stable efficiency and full-day range without operational dwell time. Penske showed that hydrogen excels in flat, structured RTB corridor work, while Pilot proved the technology can perform across diverse California terrain, including moderate grades and extended corridor mileage.

However, both deployments underscored that infrastructure — not vehicle performance — is the decisive constraint in urban and regional routes. The Hyundai XCIENT is not yet optimized for long-distance on-highway operations.

Hydrogen availability, supply-window timing, compression and cooldown cycles, and station throughput influenced operations more than route topology or speed profiles. Delivered hydrogen cost per mile remains significantly higher than diesel or electricity, despite excellent per-kg efficiency.

In early 2026, hydrogen's role is best aligned with corridor applications that already have station assets, supportive policy, and predictable routing. These deployments represent meaningful progress for hydrogen trucking, demonstrating strong vehicle reliability and range performance — but commercial scaling will depend on fuel cost trajectory, station network growth, and multi-truck throughput capability and further vehicle powertrain optimization.

5.5 All Fuel Cross-Fleet Summary & Comparative Analysis

Run on Less – Messy Middle assembled 13 fleets across four powertrain technologies, collectively logging more than 73,000 validated miles in revenue freight service. This cross-fleet analysis synthesizes the operational findings to identify where each technology excels, where challenges remain, and what factors matter most for real-world deployment decisions. The goal is not to declare a winner but to map the operational envelope for each technology — the duty cycles, terrains, and operating conditions where it can deliver competitive performance today.

The data confirms a central reality of the Messy Middle — there is no single technology capable of addressing every freight application with optimal economics, environmental performance, and operational simplicity. Instead, fleets face technology-selection decisions that depend heavily on corridor characteristics, infrastructure availability, payload requirements, and operational priorities. The evidence presented here is intended to support those decisions with validated performance data rather than projections or manufacturer claims.

## 5.5.2 Operational Comparison by Technology

This section looks at daily productivity and utilization, terrain sensitivity, and infrastructure dependency. 

### 5.5.2.1 Daily Productivity & Utilization

Daily mileage capability emerged as a primary differentiator across technologies. Diesel and CNG fleets demonstrated the highest sustained daily productivity, with MVT's diesel averaging 770-mile averages and 1,175-mile days are only possible with team drivers swapping places behind the wheel — single-driver HOS would cap daily miles much lower (typically to a maximum of 500 to 600 miles). UPS's CNG triples operation similarly achieved 694-mile daily averages with exceptional consistency. These results reflect both the operational maturity of combustion powertrains and the infrastructure independence that enables route optimization around freight needs rather than fueling constraints.

BEV daily productivity varied dramatically by platform and duty cycle. JoyRide's 705 kWh Windrose sleeper achieved an average of 382 miles per active day with an 875-mile validated maximum — approaching conventional diesel team-driver capabilities when charging and duty cycle aligned. SAIA's 500-mile Tesla Semi configuration averaged 361 miles per day with multiple days above 500 miles. In contrast, regional BEV deployments like Nevoya (173 mi/day) and 4Gen (189 mi/day) operated within tighter envelopes matched to their battery capacity and infrastructure access. The spread between best-case BEV productivity and regional operational realities underscores that battery capacity and charging infrastructure jointly determine operational viability.

Hydrogen fuel cell fleets showed moderate daily productivity constrained primarily by the XCIENT platform's 56 mph governed speed. Pilot averaged 272 miles per active day with a 427-mile maximum, while Penske's highly structured RTB operation produced 121-mile daily averages. These results reflect platform design choices (regional optimization rather than long-haul specification) and infrastructure positioning rather than fundamental technology limitations. Future hydrogen platforms designed for higher sustained speeds could approach diesel daily productivity while maintaining hydrogen's refueling-speed advantage over BEV.

---

<!-- page: 230 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 230

### 5.5.2.2 Terrain Sensitivity & Efficiency Variation

Terrain proved to be the most powerful predictor of efficiency variation across all technologies, but the magnitude of that variation differed substantially by powertrain. Diesel demonstrated remarkable terrain resilience. Albert Transport achieved 11.78 MPG on flat Midwestern corridors while Schneider delivered 9.03 MPG through demanding western mountain passes including sustained grade segments. This variation, while meaningful, remained operationally manageable; even Schneider's mountain operations produced viable fuel economy without range concerns.

BEV efficiency showed far greater terrain sensitivity. Flat-terrain operations like SAIA's Northern California routes achieved 1.2 to 1.4 kWh/mi, while elevated grades drove consumption to 1.8 to 2.0+ kWh/mi — representing 50% to 70% efficiency swings. Critically, these efficiency variations translate directly to range variations. A truck consuming 1.3 kWh/mi has 50% more range than one at 2.0 kWh/mi from the same battery pack. Regenerative braking recovered 9% to 20% of traction energy depending on duty cycle, partially offsetting grade penalties on descents but was unable to fully compensate for steep-grade energy costs. For BEV deployment planning, corridor-specific terrain analysis proved essential. Aggregate "expected range" figures from manufacturer specifications bore limited relationship to actual operational range in varied terrain.

CNG/RNG efficiency showed substantial terrain sensitivity, with grade-by-grade analysis revealing efficiency swings of 47% to 62% between flat cruising and steep climbing segments — comparable in magnitude to BEV terrain effects. However, unlike BEVs where efficiency variations translate directly to range constraints, CNG's large fuel capacity and fast refueling mean terrain penalties affect operating cost rather than operational viability. UPS's Salt Lake triples operation — with more than half the distance on climbs or descents and 273,000 ft. of cumulative elevation change— achieved approximately 6.7 mi/DGE overall, demonstrating that disciplined lane design balancing climbs with descents can produce strong average efficiency even in challenging terrain.

Hydrogen fuel cells showed surprisingly stable efficiency across the operational conditions documented. Pilot's California operations (elevation range -33 to 1,640 ft. with varied terrain) achieved 7.80 mi/kg, while Penske's flat Texas corridors produced 7.5 mi/kg — only a modest variation despite substantially different profiles. This apparent stability may reflect limited exposure to extreme conditions (neither fleet operated sustained steep mountain grades), but it suggests that the fuel cell hybrid architecture — with battery buffering and regenerative capability — helps moderate efficiency variation across rolling terrain, potentially simplifying range prediction for hydrogen deployments.

### 5.5.2.3 Infrastructure Dependencies

Infrastructure availability fundamentally shapes operational viability, and the technologies in this Run span the full spectrum from ubiquitous to severely constrained. Diesel and renewable diesel benefit from essentially unlimited infrastructure — fuel stops can be planned around driver breaks and freight requirements rather than vehicle needs. CNG/RNG occupies a middle position, while public fast-fill stations remain sparse compared to diesel, the mature fleets in this Run successfully operated by aligning corridors with existing infrastructure or co-locating private stations with depots. All three CNG fleets relied on predictable lane structures and daily refueling patterns rather than station density.

BEV infrastructure requirements proved more complex. Depot charging provided reliable overnight energy replenishment for regional operations, but extended daily productivity required strategic mid-route DC fast charging. SAIA's and JoyRide's long-haul BEV achievements depended on specific high-power charging sites (Tesla megawatt chargers for the Semi, public DCFC networks for JoyRide) positioned at natural dwell points along established corridors. The infrastructure constraint for BEV is not merely station count but the intersection

---

<!-- page: 231 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 231

of station location, power level, and operational dwell time. A truck that can theoretically accept 350 kW charging gains nothing if available stations deliver 150 kW or require significant out-of-route driving.

Hydrogen infrastructure represents the most severe constraint. Both FCEV fleets relied on mobile 700-bar dispensers or limited fixed stations, with refueling coordination requiring explicit planning around hydrogen delivery, equipment cooldown, and dispatch alignment. The logistics of ensuring hydrogen availability at the right place and time added operational complexity absent from diesel or mature CNG deployments. This infrastructure gap — not vehicle technology — remains the binding constraint on hydrogen adoption at scale.

## 5.5.3 Technology-Specific Operational Envelopes

This section focuses on the operating environments that each of the four powertrain solutions is best suited for as well as the factors that constrain further deployment of each technology.

### 5.5.3.1 Diesel & Renewable Diesel

Diesel and renewable diesel are best suited for long-haul transcontinental operations, mountain corridor freight, high-daily-mileage requirements, weight-sensitive heavy loads, and any application where infrastructure flexibility and operational simplicity are paramount. The diesel fleets in this Run demonstrated that optimized conventional operations could achieve nearly double typical industry efficiency (11.8 MPG versus approximately 6 MPG fleet averages) while maintaining full operational flexibility [12].

Carbon intensity faces mounting regulatory and customer pressure, and this is a primary constant of diesel. Fuel price volatility creates cost uncertainty. Maintenance complexity (emissions systems, oil changes, filter replacements) adds lifecycle costs. For fleets with sustainability commitments or operating in regions with aggressive zero-emission mandates, diesel's long-term viability is increasingly questioned even where operational advantages remain clear.

### 5.5.3.2 CNG/RNG

CNG/RNG are best suited for highway-dominant regional and long-haul operations with predictable lanes and corridors where CNG infrastructure already exists or can be strategically positioned. The Cummins X15N engine demonstrated diesel-competitive pulling power and durability across demanding applications including heavy-haul tanker and triple-trailer configurations.

Efficiency degrades significantly in low-speed stop-and-go conditions are the primary constraints of CNG/RNG. Infrastructure positioning requires careful corridor-by-corridor planning. RNG availability and pricing can be volatile. Vehicle premium and range trade-offs (compared to diesel tank capacity) require operational adjustments.

### 5.5.3.3 BEVs

BEVs are best suited for regional distribution, drayage, urban delivery, and predictable corridor operations with favorable terrain and accessible charging infrastructure. With larger battery packs (565 to 705+ kWh) and strategic charging access, BEVs are now demonstrating viability in selected long-haul corridors where terrain, infrastructure, and duty cycles align. Zero direct emissions and lower energy/maintenance costs create compelling economics where daily mileage requirements match platform capability.

Terrain sensitivity creates large efficiency and range variations that complicate planning are the primary constraint of BEVs. Charging infrastructure remains sparse on many freight corridors. Battery weight reduces payload capacity for weight-sensitive freight. High-speed operation (65+ mph) substantially increases energy

---

<!-- page: 232 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 232

consumption. Cold-weather performance impacts have not been fully characterized in this Run. For mountain corridors, high-daily-mileage requirements, or operations far from established charging networks, BEV deployment requires careful route-specific validation.

### 5.5.3.4 Hydrogen Fuel Cell

Hydrogen fuel cells are best suited for regional operations with structured refueling access, applications requiring zero direct emissions, and weight-sensitive freight where hydrogen's mass advantage over batteries preserves payload capacity. When infrastructure aligns, hydrogen can maintain diesel-like operational patterns without extended charging dwell.

Infrastructure is the binding constraint — station availability, reliability, and throughput limit where hydrogen can operate viably. Current production platforms (XCIENT) are optimized for regional rather than long-haul duty cycles. Hydrogen fuel cost and availability create significant operating-cost uncertainty. The total cost of ownership case for hydrogen remains challenging against alternatives at current pricing and scale.

## 5.6 All Powertrain Key Takeaways 

The NACFE team offers some key takeaways for all the powertrains in Run on Less – Messy Middle. The bottom line is that each technology has its place in trucking’s Messy Middle but no one technology is right for all use cases. 

1. **No single technology addresses all freight applications optimally.** Technology selection must be application-specific, considering corridor terrain, daily mileage requirements, infrastructure availability, payload characteristics, and operational priorities. The Messy Middle is defined by this multi-technology reality.
2. **Terrain is the strongest predictor of efficiency variation, especially for BEV.** Diesel showed an approximate 30% efficiency spread across extreme terrain variation; BEV showed 50% to 70%+ variation, translating directly to range differences. Route-specific terrain analysis should precede any BEV deployment commitment. See Figure 148.
