Figure 148: Terrain sensitivity by powertrain technology

3. **Infrastructure determines operational viability as much as vehicle capability.** CNG, BEV and hydrogen fuel cell deployments succeeded where charging/refueling aligned with freight patterns. Infrastructure gaps — not vehicle technology — constrain where alternative powertrains can operate effectively.
4. **Optimized diesel remains the productivity and flexibility benchmark.** MVT's 800+ mile daily averages with 1,175-mile peak and Albert's 11.8 MPG demonstrate what mature technology with ubiquitous infrastructure delivers. Alternative powertrains must approach these operational characteristics — not just energy efficiency — to achieve broad adoption in long-haul applications.
5. **BEV is viable for regional and selected long-haul corridors today.** With appropriate battery capacity (565+ kWh) and strategic charging access, BEVs demonstrated 350 to 500+ mile daily operations and 875-mile maximums. The technology is past demonstration phase for suitable applications.
6. **CNG/RNG delivers diesel-competitive performance in appropriate duty cycles.** The Cummins X15N engine handled heavy-haul tanker work, triple-trailer configurations, and regional distribution with efficiency and reliability comparable to diesel. CNG/RNG offers a mature, lower-carbon alternative for fleets with compatible corridors — with RNG providing a pathway to near-zero carbon intensity using proven technology.
7. **Hydrogen shows operational promise but faces infrastructure and cost barriers.** Weight advantages and operational flexibility create genuine benefits, however, infrastructure scarcity and high fuel costs currently limit hydrogen to demonstration-scale deployment with significant fleet/infrastructure coordination.

---

<!-- page: 234 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 234

8. **Real-world data differs from manufacturer specifications.** Advertised ranges, efficiency figures, and operational capabilities consistently diverged from validated operational results. Fleets should demand operational validation data from comparable duty cycles before making procurement decisions.
9. **Duty cycle design can mitigate technology limitations.** All successful deployments reflected thoughtful matching of vehicle capability to operational requirements. UPS's CNG triples, SAIA's long-haul BEV, and Penske's RTB hydrogen all succeeded through operational design — not despite constraints but by working within them.
10. **The multi-decade transition requires portfolio thinking.** Fleets will need to operate multiple powertrain technologies simultaneously, matching each to appropriate applications while infrastructure matures and technology costs decline. The Messy Middle demands flexibility, not single-technology commitment.

## 5.7 All Powertrain Conclusions 

Run on Less – Messy Middle confirms that freight decarbonization is not a simple technology swap but rather a complex, multi-decade transition requiring operational adaptation, infrastructure development, and technology-specific deployment strategies. The 13 fleets and 14 the trucks documented here demonstrate that each powertrain technology has a legitimate operational envelope where it can deliver competitive performance today — and equally, boundaries beyond which it struggles to match conventional operations.

Diesel remains the benchmark for operational flexibility, daily productivity, and infrastructure independence. Fleets achieved up to 11.8 MPG while covering 500 to 800+ miles daily across varied terrain — performance that alternative powertrains must approach to achieve scale adoption. Renewable diesel offers an immediate decarbonization pathway using existing assets and infrastructure.

CNG and RNG represent established decarbonization pathways capable of handling demanding freight applications — from regional food distribution to heavy-haul tanker operations — with lower carbon intensity than petroleum diesel. Highway-dominant duty cycles with predictable infrastructure access create the operational sweet spot.

Battery-electric technology has passed the demonstration phase for regional and selected long-haul applications. With 565 to 705+ kWh battery packs and strategic charging placement, BEVs achieved 350 to 500+ mile daily operations and demonstrated 875-mile single-day capability. Terrain sensitivity and infrastructure dependence require corridor-specific validation, but viable BEV deployment is no longer theoretical. These results were achieved with early-generation production platforms, and significant improvement in efficiency, range, charging speed, and weight optimization should be expected as BEV tractor technology matures.

Hydrogen fuel cells showed genuine operational promise — stable efficiency and weight advantages — but infrastructure scarcity and cost remain prohibitive constraints. Current deployments succeed through intensive coordination rather than operational simplicity.

The Messy Middle will persist for years as infrastructure expands, technology costs decline, and operational experience accumulates. Fleets navigating this transition need the kind of validated operational data this Run provides — not projections or demonstrations, but real performance from working assets in commercial service. The path forward requires matching each technology to its operational envelope while continuously reassessing as capabilities evolve.

The fleet-level analyses above provide the foundation for the cross-fleet synthesis in the following section.

# 6 All Powertrains Cross-Fleet Analytics

Run on Less – Messy Middle confirmed that advancements in freight efficiency are emerging from multiple technologies rather than converging on a single dominant pathway. Diesel, renewable diesel, biodiesel, CNG/RNG, battery-electric, and hydrogen fuel cell fleets all demonstrated productive, real-world operation when matched to the appropriate duty cycle and infrastructure. Section 5 documented these fleet-level results in detail. This section examines the cross-fleet patterns behind them.

The analytics presented here reflect a dataset spanning long-haul, regional, drayage, and LTL operations across 13 fleets and 14 deployments, 73,000+ validated miles, and 194 active operational days. High-frequency telematics produced more than 2 million data points, enabling granular analysis of how efficiency varies with instantaneous operating conditions. This variation made it possible to compare how different powertrains respond to identical physical forces — payload, grade, speed, idle time, and stop density.

The sample size — 14 trucks representing four powertrain technologies — is small by statistical standards, and readers should interpret cross-fleet comparisons with appropriate caution. However, these were not laboratory tests or controlled demonstrations on idealized routes. Every mile represents real freight moved under commercial operating conditions, with drivers responding to actual traffic, weather, maintenance needs, and dispatch requirements. The value of this dataset lies not in statistical power but in the operational authenticity that controlled studies cannot replicate. Where patterns emerge across multiple fleets facing similar conditions, those patterns merit attention even if sample sizes preclude conventional statistical significance testing.

Across all fleets, daily operational patterns reflected a blend of dispatch cadence, charging or fueling access, route assignments, and driver availability. Several trucks experienced significant downtime during the Run due to maintenance, charging infrastructure issues, or operational constraints — realities that commercial fleets face regularly.

The sections that follow examine analytical methodology, cumulative terrain impacts, time-of-day patterns, route consistency, infrastructure dependencies, cross-technology comparisons, and the analytical priorities that emerged from the Run. The goal is not to declare a technology winner but to provide fleet planners with validated insights for matching powertrains to appropriate applications.

This section focuses on operational performance metrics. Economic analysis — including TCO, fuel and electricity pricing, maintenance costs, and capital investment considerations — will be addressed in a separate NACFE report. Similarly, emissions analysis covering well-to-wheel carbon intensity, criteria pollutant reductions, and lifecycle environmental impacts will be presented in a dedicated NACFE emissions report on Run on Less – Messy Middle.

## 6.1 Analytical Methodology & Energy Normalization 

A unified analytical framework was applied to compare energy use across fuels. All datasets were normalized to common fields including distance, energy consumption, payload category, elevation change, speed distribution,

---

<!-- page: 236 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 236

and operational state. This allowed fleets with different telematics systems and energy sources to be evaluated on comparable terms.

Normalization was especially important for energy-equivalent comparisons. Diesel and renewable diesel fleets were analyzed in MPG. CNG/RNG fleets were evaluated in mi/DGE. Battery-electric fleets were evaluated in mi/kWh, with separate accounting for driving energy, regenerative recovery, charging losses, and idle consumption. Hydrogen fuel cell fleets were standardized to 68.6 kg of usable onboard hydrogen, representing the verified usable portion of the 70-kg rated tank system used by both hydrogen participants — ensuring consistency with Section 5 hydrogen analysis.

Elevation impacts were derived through digital elevation model (DEM) cross-referencing with GPS coordinates, enabling grade-band analysis across all fleets. BEV regenerative braking percentages were integrated into the energy-normalization workflow, with fleet-specific values ranging from approximately 9% to approximately 203.0% depending on duty cycle characteristics. Distance calculations used speed-based methodology (speed × time interval) where odometer data showed anomalies, ensuring consistent mileage accounting across platforms.

All results should be interpreted as reflecting real-world operational behavior rather than controlled benchmarking. The goal of this analysis is to compare patterns and identify operational factors — not to prescribe a single dominant solution.

See Figure 149. This metric reveals fundamental differences in operational intensity that terrain grade distributions alone may obscure.

### 6.2.1 Gravitational Work Intensity Spectrum

Climbing intensity, from Albert Transport's 312 ft. of climb per 100 miles on flat Midwestern corridors to 4Gen's 5,313 ft. per 100 miles in California port drayage operations represents a 17x range. This variation has profound implications for energy consumption — particularly for BEVs where gravitational work directly depletes stored energy with only partial recovery through regenerative braking.

The highest-intensity operations were not necessarily the highest-elevation fleets. 4Gen's California drayage — operating at relatively low absolute elevations — accumulated 5,313 ft. of climb per 100 miles because of repeated short climbs accessing port facilities and crossing urban terrain features. Pilot's hydrogen operation similarly recorded 4,965 ft. per 100 miles traversing California's rolling inland corridors. These findings underscore that cumulative gravitational work depends more on route topology (frequency and magnitude of grade changes) than on absolute elevation or elevation range.

### 6.2.2 Technology-Specific Implications

For diesel and CNG powertrains, cumulative elevation gain translates to fuel consumption with relatively linear efficiency penalties. MVT's diesel operation accumulated 905,909 ft. of total vertical work (climbing plus descending) yet maintained 770-mile daily averages — demonstrating that combustion powertrains absorb gravitational work demands without fundamental operational constraints.

For battery-electric vehicles, high climbing intensity creates compounding challenges. 4Gen's 5,313 ft. per 100 miles represents roughly 7x the gravitational work intensity of JoyRide's 726 ft. per 100 miles, yet both operated

---

<!-- page: 239 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 239

BEV platforms. This disparity helps explain their productivity difference: JoyRide averaged 409 miles per day on low-intensity Texas corridors while 4Gen averaged 189 miles per day on high-intensity California port routes — despite comparable battery capacities. Regenerative braking recovers 11% to 23% of traction energy depending on duty cycle, but the recovery is inherently incomplete, and high climbing intensity depletes range regardless of descent recovery.

### 6.3.1 Peak-Hour Operations Dominance

Nine of 14 deployments operated with more than 50% of miles during peak hours (morning plus afternoon, 6 a.m. to 6 p.m. local time), reflecting the daytime scheduling requirements of LTL, regional delivery, and drayage operations. Diesel fleets averaged 65% peak-hour operation, with Frito-Lay (94%) and Schneider (92%) concentrating nearly all miles during daytime hours. Kleysen’s (90%) natural gas tractor also spent nearly all miles during daytime hours.

BEV deployments averaged 70% peak-hour operation, with Saia 1's LTL routes showing 98% daytime concentration. These patterns reflect customer delivery windows, receiver operating hours, and driver shift structures rather than efficiency optimization — demonstrating that operational requirements often override theoretical scheduling advantages.

Five deployments achieved majority overnight operation: Penske (100%), Albert (72%), Saia 2 (61%), MVT (53%), and Pilot (52%). Penske's complete overnight concentration — the most extreme in the study — demonstrates deliberate scheduling around hydrogen station availability and traffic avoidance. Albert's overnight pattern reflects long-haul linehaul scheduling on Midwestern corridors. Saia 2's 61% overnight operation shows that BEV linehaul can achieve overnight scheduling when route structure permits, in contrast to Saia 1's 98% daytime LTL operation serving terminal delivery windows.

### 6.3.2 Peak Traffic Exposure Patterns

CNG/RNG operations showed 76% peak-hour concentration (UPS), reflecting daytime regional delivery routes where RNG's lower carbon intensity addresses urban air quality objectives during high-traffic periods. Hydrogen fleets averaged just 24% peak exposure — the lowest of any technology — with Penske's 0% peak operation reflecting complete overnight scheduling around station availability.

Battery-electric deployments showed significant variation in time-of-day patterns driven by duty cycle rather than charging optimization. Saia 1's LTL operation concentrated 98% of miles during peak hours, reflecting terminal-to-terminal scheduling requirements. In contrast, Saia 2's linehaul operation achieved 61% overnight — demonstrating that the same fleet can operate different BEV duty cycles with very different time profiles. Nevoya's 81% peak concentration reflects California regional delivery windows, while 4Gen's 63% peak operation is consistent with port operating hours.

Fleets with disciplined lane structures achieved remarkably tight operational envelopes, while variable-dispatch operations showed substantially higher day-to-day variation regardless of powertrain technology.

### 6.4.1 Fixed-Lane Operations Excellence

Only two fleets — UPS CNG and Penske hydrogen — achieved fixed-lane classification (CV less than 10%). UPS's 0.8% coefficient of variation across 13 consecutive days represents the most remarkable consistency in the entire Run, with daily mileage ranging only from 676 to 699 miles. This 23-mile spread — less than 4% of the

---

<!-- page: 242 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 242

average — reflects systematic optimization of routes, schedules, and infrastructure access that transforms operational variability into a controlled parameter.

Penske's hydrogen operation demonstrated similar consistency (1.7% CV) at a much lower productivity level (136 to 145 miles daily). The tight consistency reflects fixed regional routing designed around predictable hydrogen station access. However, the 5x productivity difference between UPS (694 mi/day) and Penske (140 mi/day) illustrates that consistency alone does not determine productivity; infrastructure density and vehicle range fundamentally shape the achievable operational envelope.

### 6.4.2 Variable-Dispatch Challenges

Three of five BEV deployments fell into the variable-dispatch classification (CV greater than 40%), with coefficients of variation ranging from 47.5% to 61.6%. This pattern reflects the compounding uncertainties of charging coordination, route flexibility, and demand-driven dispatch. However, Saia's Tesla Semi deployments demonstrate that BEV platforms can achieve route consistency. Saia 2's linehaul operation shows a CV of just 10.7% on full-operation days (classified as Fixed-Lane when accounting for downtime), while Saia 1's regional LTL achieved a semi-fixed status at 35.4% CV.

JoyRide's 875-mile maximum daily distance demonstrates BEV capability when conditions align. The 47.5% CV reflects route variation and charging logistics rather than vehicle limitations. For fleets considering BEV deployment, the consistency analysis suggests that fixed-lane applications with predictable charging access will achieve systematically better results than variable-dispatch operations requiring dynamic infrastructure coordination. Saia 1's semi-fixed performance on LTL routes — in contrast to Saia 2's variable long-haul linehaul — illustrates how the same fleet can experience fundamentally different consistency profiles depending on duty cycle characteristics.

Infrastructure availability fundamentally shapes operational viability, and the four powertrain technologies span the full spectrum from ubiquitous access to severe constraint. See Figure 153. This section quantifies how infrastructure density affects practical range, daily productivity, and operational planning requirements.

Figure 153: Infrastructure and operational characteristics by technology

### 6.5.1 Range Utilization Analysis

Range utilization — the ratio of average daily mileage to maximum daily mileage — reveals how infrastructure constraints shape operational planning. Diesel fleets averaged 63% range utilization, reflecting that daily productivity is driven by driver hours and freight demand rather than fuel access. CNG achieved the highest utilization at 78%, with UPS's fixed-lane operation reaching 99.3% — essentially running at maximum productive capacity every day.

BEV deployments showed notably lower range utilization at 50%, indicating that operators maintain substantial buffers against charging uncertainty and terrain variation. This conservative approach is operationally rational given current infrastructure density and the consequences of mid-route battery depletion, but it imposes productivity costs — a fleet operating at 50% utilization leaves substantial daily capacity unused. 

Whether range utilization will increase as infrastructure matures remains uncertain — operators may maintain conservative buffers indefinitely given the operational risks of stranded vehicles or charging network expansion may enable higher utilization over time. The 28-point gap between BEV (50%) and CNG (78%) utilization quantifies this constraint without predicting its trajectory.

### 6.5.2 Energy Cost Positioning

Energy cost per mile varied across fuels because of differences in energy density, drivetrain efficiency, and regional pricing. Battery-electric fleets can achieve favorable cost per mile under off-peak depot charging, but

---

<!-- page: 244 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 244

this requires charging windows that align with shift cadence — a constraint that limits operational flexibility. Cost variability increases substantially when fleets rely on mid-shift fast charging under demand tariffs, and electricity rate structures vary significantly by utility territory. Fleets should model their specific operating conditions rather than assuming generic BEV cost advantages.

CNG/RNG fleets maintained competitive cost performance with strong price stability through contractual arrangements, though station availability outside established corridors remains a planning constraint. The Run's CNG participants benefited from years of infrastructure positioning that new CNG adopters would need to replicate or access. Diesel and renewable diesel fleets delivered 9.0 to 11.8 MPG depending on terrain — the baseline against which alternatives are measured. 

Renewable diesel offers lifecycle carbon reductions with no infrastructure or operational changes, though fuel premiums vary regionally and availability remains constrained in some markets. Hydrogen fuel cell cost positioning reflected early-stage production and distribution economics. Both hydrogen fleets achieved strong operational efficiency (7.5 to 7.8 mi/kg) but faced fuel costs that currently exceed other options by substantial margins.

Across all fuels, fleets emphasized that cost stability is as important as nominal cost. BEVs depend heavily on tariff design; CNG/RNG offers strong contractual stability; diesel is supported by deep market liquidity; hydrogen depends on maturing supply chains. Comprehensive TCO analysis — incorporating capital costs, residual values, maintenance requirements, infrastructure investment, and incentive structures — will be addressed in a separate NACFE report from Run on Less – Messy Middle.

## 6.7 Human-Factor Performance Modeling 

Fleet interviews and telematics patterns consistently showed that human factors remain central to performance across all four technologies — and that driver adaptation requirements vary significantly by powertrain. Drivers highlighted the value of route familiarity, energy-window planning, charging or fueling procedures, and predictable dispatch cadence. The Run provided insight into how driver behavior, training requirements, and operational practices differ across technologies.

### 6.7.1 Technology-Specific Driver Skills

BEV drivers emphasized grade anticipation and regeneration opportunities — techniques that require training and experience to execute consistently. Effective BEV operation involves anticipating descents to lift throttle early, maximizing regenerative braking rather than friction braking, and managing speed to optimize the efficiency-versus-progress tradeoff. The learning curve for energy-optimal BEV driving differs substantially from a diesel operation, and fleets reported that driver buy-in affected efficiency outcomes. Drivers who embraced

---

<!-- page: 247 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 247

the technology and understood the energy dynamics achieved meaningfully better efficiency than those who drove BEVs like diesel trucks.

Drivers of hydrogen fuel cell trucks emphasized fueling-procedure consistency and coordination with station availability windows. Unlike diesel refueling — where any station works and the process is universally familiar — hydrogen fueling requires specific procedures, nozzle handling techniques, and awareness of station-specific protocols. Drivers also needed to coordinate arrival times with station availability, particularly given the limited number of heavy-duty hydrogen stations. This logistical layer adds complexity compared to diesel refueling but becomes routine with experience.

Diesel and CNG/RNG drivers emphasized speed control, idle management, and aerodynamic compliance — familiar techniques that transfer across conventional powertrains. The CNG drivers noted that fuel gauge interpretation differs from diesel (measuring pressure rather than volume), but driving techniques remain largely unchanged. These transferable skills mean that fleets transitioning to CNG face lower driver training requirements than BEV or hydrogen transitions.

### 6.7.2 Range Anxiety & Confidence Building

BEV and hydrogen drivers reported that range confidence developed over time as they learned their vehicles' actual capabilities versus displayed estimates. Early in deployments, drivers tended toward conservative operation — maintaining higher state-of-charge buffers, avoiding aggressive acceleration, and selecting routes with known charging or fueling access. As experience accumulated, drivers developed intuition for how terrain, speed, payload, and weather affected range, enabling more confident operation closer to vehicle limits.

This confidence-building process has fleet implications. New BEV or hydrogen fuel cell deployments may show lower productivity initially as drivers build experience, with performance improving over subsequent months. Fleets reported that pairing new alternative-fuel drivers with experienced mentors accelerated the learning curve. The Run's September timeframe captured fleets at various experience levels, which likely contributed to performance variation within technology categories.

### 6.7.3 Operational Rhythm & Consistency

Normalized data showed that day-to-day variability narrowed when operational rhythms were stable. Fleets with consistent lanes, predictable schedules, and routine maintenance demonstrated tighter clusters around their mean energy intensity. UPS's 0.8% daily mileage variation exemplifies how disciplined operations translate to predictable performance. However, achieving this consistency often required operational constraints — fixed routes, scheduled refueling or charging windows, access to appropriate fueling infrastructure — that reduce the flexibility conventional diesel operations take for granted.

Dispatch practices emerged as a critical human factor. Fleets that integrated vehicle capabilities into dispatch decisions — assigning BEVs to routes within comfortable range, scheduling hydrogen fuel cell trucks around station availability — achieved better outcomes than those treating alternative-fueled vehicles as direct diesel substitutes. This dispatch intelligence represents institutional knowledge that develops over time and may be difficult to transfer or replicate.

### 6.7.4 Temperature & Climate Considerations

The Run's 14 deployments operated across diverse climate zones during September 2025, providing preliminary insight into temperature effects on powertrain performance. Operating temperatures ranged from 40°F lows in

---

<!-- page: 248 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 248

Canadian prairie operations (Kleysen CNG) to 95°F highs in Southern California's Inland Empire (4Gen BEV). This 55°F temperature span affected different technologies in distinct ways.

Battery electric vehicles showed sensitivity to temperature extremes. Three BEV deployments — 4Gen, Nevoya, and JoyRide — operated in regions where September temperatures regularly exceeded 90°F, requiring active battery thermal management that consumed energy and potentially limited charging rates. Saia 1's Bay Area operation experienced milder conditions (55°F to 75°F), closer to optimal battery operating temperatures. While the Run's data does not isolate temperature effects from other variables, the pattern suggests that BEV deployments in hot climates face additional energy overhead that mild-climate deployments avoid.

CNG fleets operated predominantly in cooler continental climates (40°F to 70°F range), where temperature effects on CNG storage and combustion are minimal. The Canadian operations (Kleysen) experienced the coolest conditions in the Run, though September temperatures remained well above the extreme cold that can affect CNG system performance in winter months. Diesel operations spanned the full temperature range with no observable temperature-related constraints — consistent with diesel's well-established tolerance for diverse operating conditions.

Hydrogen fuel cell vehicles operated in California's variable climate, with Pilot encountering temperatures from 55°F to 95°F across statewide routes. Fuel cell systems generate waste heat that requires cooling, and high ambient temperatures can stress thermal management systems. However, the Run's hydrogen fuel cell sample was too small to draw conclusions about temperature sensitivity. Future demonstrations spanning winter and summer conditions would provide more definitive temperature-performance relationships across all technologies.

Technology determines the boundary of what is possible. Human behavior determines how much of that potential is realized; but operational context — including climate, terrain, and infrastructure — determines whether that potential is practically achievable in specific applications.

## 6.8 Future Analytical Priorities 

The Run revealed several opportunities for improving analytical consistency as fleets deploy multiple powertrains across their operations.

Standardized telematics across OEMs would reduce normalization challenges. The current analysis required substantial data reconciliation across different telematics platforms, GPS systems, and energy-accounting methodologies. Industry-wide standards for freight efficiency reporting would enable more direct cross-fleet comparison and accelerate learning across the sector.

Predictive route-energy tools would help fleets schedule charging and fueling windows more effectively, especially in BEV and hydrogen fuel cell operations where infrastructure access timing significantly affects productivity. Integration of real-time terrain data, traffic patterns, and weather forecasts with vehicle energy models could enable dynamic range prediction that improves operational confidence.

Seasonal performance characterization emerged as a gap in this analysis. The September demonstration captured mild fall conditions across most regions, but BEV battery performance, hydrogen fuel cell thermal management, and CNG cold weather operation all vary with temperature extremes. Future demonstrations spanning winter and summer months would provide more complete performance envelopes for fleet planning.

---

Benchmarking tools could help fleets compare their performance to anonymized peers across technologies, though meaningful comparison requires controlling for duty cycle, terrain, and operational context — factors that often dominate technology effects. Coordination across utilities, station developers, and OEMs will be necessary as BEV and hydrogen fuel cell deployments scale, though the pace and extent of infrastructure development remain uncertain and will be shaped by policy, investment returns, and technology evolution. 

The Run's findings highlight that for many applications, vehicle capability currently exceeds infrastructure availability — but infrastructure development at the scale needed to close this gap represents a multi-decade undertaking with uncertain outcomes. See Figure 156.

These priorities reflect a central message from the Messy Middle — the next stage of freight efficiency progress relies as much on data clarity, planning, and coordination as on the vehicles themselves.

## 6.9 Key Cross-Fleet Findings — All Powertrains 

The cross-fleet analysis across 14 vehicles and four powertrain technologies yielded several consistent findings with direct implications for fleet planning and technology deployment decisions.

1. **Cumulative elevation varies 17x across deployments (312 to 5,313 ft. climb/100mi) and correlates with BEV productivity differences.** High-intensity operations like 4Gen's port drayage achieved 189 mi/day while low-intensity operations like JoyRide achieved 409 mi/day — despite comparable platforms. Route topology analysis should precede BEV deployment decisions.
2. **Overnight operations provided efficiency and productivity advantages across all technologies.** Five of 14 deployments operated more than 50% of the time overnight, avoiding peak traffic and optimizing driver hours. Alternative-fuel operations can exploit off-peak advantages when routes and infrastructure align.
3. **Only two fleets — UPS CNG and Penske hydrogen fuel cell — achieved fixed-lane consistency (CV less than 10%).** Four of five BEV deployments showed variable-dispatch patterns (CV 48% to 62%), but Saia 1's regional LTL achieved semi-fixed status (35% CV), demonstrating that BEV consistency depends on duty cycle matching rather than inherent technology limitations.
4. **CNG achieved 78% range utilization — the highest of any technology — reflecting infrastructure maturity built over decades.** BEV's 50% utilization reflects operational buffers that operators maintain against charging uncertainty. Whether this gap will narrow depends on charging network expansion and operator risk tolerance — not assured outcomes.
5. **Infrastructure density constrains alternative-powertrain productivity more than vehicle capability in many applications.** JoyRide's 875-mile BEV maximum shows substantial single-day capability. The 239-mile BEV fleet average reflects the gap between what vehicles can do under optimal conditions and what operations achieve routinely. Closing this gap requires infrastructure investment, operational learning, and acceptance of constraints that may persist.
6. **Cross-technology comparisons showed that duty cycle often influenced productivity as much as technology selection.** Saia's two Tesla Semi deployments — 79 mi/day (LTL) versus 361 mi/day (linehaul) — illustrate 4.6x productivity variation with identical technology. Direct technology comparisons are complicated by operational differences, but the data suggests that matching powertrain to duty cycle matters at least as much as powertrain selection itself.
7. **Technology determines where a fleet can operate; operations determine how well it performs there.** The physics governing freight movement do not change with the propulsion system — only the way each energy system manages those forces.

## 6.10 Cross-Fleet Analytics Summary 

The cross-fleet analytics presented in this section provide quantitative foundations for technology deployment decisions. Cumulative elevation analysis reveals that gravitational work intensity — ranging from 312 to 5,313 ft. of climb per 100 miles — has outsized impact on BEV productivity, making route topology assessment essential for deployment planning. Time-of-day analysis confirms that overnight operations provide efficiency advantages across all technologies, with five of 14 deployments concentrating more than half their miles in off-peak hours.

---

<!-- page: 251 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 251

Route consistency analysis demonstrates that operational discipline matters as much as technology selection. The only fixed-lane operations (CV less than 10%) were UPS CNG and Penske hydrogen; four of five BEV deployments showed variable-dispatch patterns, although Saia 1's semi-fixed LTL operation demonstrates BEV consistency is achievable with appropriate duty cycle matching. For fleets evaluating alternative powertrains, fixed-route applications with predictable infrastructure access will systematically outperform variable-dispatch deployments requiring dynamic coordination.

Infrastructure dependency modeling quantifies the productivity constraints imposed by fueling and charging access. CNG's 78% range utilization reflects decades of infrastructure development and deliberate route-station alignment. Replicating this for BEV or hydrogen fuel cell will require comparable investment and time. BEV's 50% utilization reflects buffers that operators maintain given current charging uncertainty — a rational response to infrastructure gaps rather than a temporary condition with assured resolution. The productivity differences between technologies reflect real operational constraints that fleet planners must weigh against decarbonization objectives and total cost of ownership.

Cross-technology direct comparisons reveal that duty cycle characteristics often matter as much as powertrain selection. Within California, four BEV deployments ranged from Saia 1's 79-mile LTL average to Saia 2's 361-mile linehaul average — a 4.6x productivity difference with identical Tesla Semi technology. Among high-productivity operations, CNG approached diesel performance when infrastructure aligned with routes, while no BEV deployment exceeded a 400-miles-per-day average during the Run. 

These findings reinforce the central Messy Middle thesis — no single technology addresses all applications optimally, each powertrain carries distinct constraints and capabilities, and fleet planners must match technologies to specific operational requirements using validated performance data rather than manufacturer specifications or theoretical projections. The data supports informed technology selection — not technology advocacy.

# 7 Operational Action Playbook 

Run on Less – Messy Middle documented more than 73,000 validated miles across 13 fleets operating 14 trucks demonstrating four powertrain technologies in revenue freight service. This section synthesizes those findings into guidance for fleet decision-makers evaluating technology options. The analysis that follows is grounded in operational data from working freight assets — providing context that complements manufacturer specifications and controlled test results. 

The Messy Middle describes a period where multiple powertrain technologies coexist, each serving applications where its characteristics align with operational requirements. How long this period lasts remains uncertain — it depends on infrastructure development, technology costs, regulatory evolution, and market dynamics. What is clear from this Run is that successful technology deployment requires matching each powertrain to appropriate duty cycles rather than assuming any single technology will serve all applications.

## 7.1 Key Findings 

Run on Less – Messy Middle produced validated operational data across 73,000+ miles of revenue freight service. The following key findings emerged from the demonstration. Run on Less – Messy Middle produced 11 findings relevant to fleet technology decisions. See Figure 157.

> **Figure 157 — Key findings from Run on Less – Messy Middle**
> Type: schematic
> Axes: n/a vs n/a
> Series: n/a
> Values: 
> 1. Diesel Benchmark: Up to 11.8 MPG - nearly double typical fleet average
> 2. Terrain Dominates: BEV efficiency varies 50% to 70% by terrain vs. diesel at 30%
> 3. CNG for Specific Duty Cycles: Highway-dominant routes with predictable infrastructure
> 4. BEV Beyond Regional: 350 to 500+ mile daily operations; public charging emerging
> 5. Hydrogen Faces Barriers: Quick refueling but only 60 HD stations nationwide

> 7. Daily Productivity: Suitability depends on mileage capability, not just efficiency

> Notes: RUN ON LESS MESSY MIDDLE by NACFE.

Figure 157: Key findings from Run on Less – Messy Middle

1. **Diesel remains the operational benchmark.** The diesel fleets achieved efficiency levels nearly double the typical long-haul fleet average — up to 11.8 MPG compared to the approximately 6 MPG documented in NACFE's 2024 Fleet Fuel Study while maintaining 500 to 800+ mile daily productivity across varied terrain [12]. This performance, combined with ubiquitous infrastructure, establishes context for evaluating alternative powertrains. Renewable diesel provides a near-term decarbonization option (50% to 80% carbon intensity reduction) using existing assets and infrastructure.
2. **Terrain is the most significant performance variable.** Diesel demonstrated terrain resilience with approximately 30% efficiency variation between flat corridors (11.78 MPG) and mountain passes (9.03 MPG). BEVs showed greater sensitivity, with efficiency variations of 50% to 70% on comparable terrain differences, translating directly to range variation. Route-specific terrain analysis is essential before deployment decisions.
3. **CNG/RNG serves specific duty cycles well.** The Cummins X15N natural gas engine — newly available in 2025 — demonstrated diesel-competitive pulling power across demanding applications including heavy-

---

<!-- page: 253 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 253

haul tanker and triple-trailer configurations. CNG/RNG efficiency (4.5 to 6.7 MPG diesel-gallon equivalent in this Run) favors highway-dominant duty cycles with predictable infrastructure access. RNG sourced from organic waste can achieve carbon-negative operations.
4. **BEV technology is expanding beyond regional applications.** Battery-electric trucks with 438 to 900+ kWh battery packs achieved 350 to 500+ mile daily operations, with an 875-mile validated single-day maximum on favorable terrain. Growing availability of public BEV charging also was demonstrated. However, terrain sensitivity, charging infrastructure requirements, and payload constraints (battery weight reduces freight capacity) require corridor-specific evaluation before deployment.
5. **Hydrogen fuel cells demonstrated operational characteristics but face significant barriers.** Fuel cell trucks showed quick refueling (10 to 15 minutes) and weight advantages over BEVs. However, infrastructure scarcity (approximately 60 heavy-duty stations nationwide versus 150,000+ diesel stations), high fuel costs ($15 to $25/kg translating to 3x to 5x BEV energy costs per mile), and energy pathway efficiency concerns (25% to 40% well-to-wheel versus 85% to 90% for BEV) present substantial challenges for commercial-scale deployment [7].
6. **Real-world operational data complements specifications.** Across all technologies, actual operational performance varied based on terrain, duty cycle, payload, and driver behavior. Specification sheets provide important baseline information; validated operational data — like that in this report — helps fleet operators understand performance in specific applications and set realistic expectations.
7. **Daily productivity matters as much as efficiency.** Technology suitability depends not only on energy efficiency but on daily mileage capability — the ability to complete freight assignments within HOS windows. MVT's diesel fleet averaged 770 miles per day; JoyRide's BEV achieved 409-mile daily averages with an 875-mile maximum. Both metrics inform technology suitability for specific applications.
8. **Infrastructure availability shapes deployment options.** Charging and hydrogen refueling networks are developing unevenly across regions. Successful deployments require alignment between vehicle needs and infrastructure positioning along freight corridors. California operations benefit from more developed alternative-fuel infrastructure; other regions may require different approaches. Bio-based and renewable diesel also face infrastructure challenges — production and distribution capacity remains limited and will require significant development as fleet demand grows.
9. **Human factors influence outcomes across all technologies.** Efficiency outcomes are shaped by driver training, performance incentives, maintenance discipline, and dispatch strategy. The highest-performing fleets in this Run demonstrated comprehensive operational optimization alongside technology selection.
10. **Fleet strategy benefits from portfolio thinking.** Many fleets will operate multiple powertrain technologies in the near- to mid-term, matching each to appropriate applications based on duty cycle, infrastructure access, and operational requirements. This approach provides flexibility as technologies mature and infrastructure develops.
11. **Organizational culture influences technology success.** Fleets that approach technology transitions with commitment and adaptability — from leadership to drivers and technicians — demonstrated stronger outcomes in Run on Less – Messy Middle. 4Gen Logistics, Nevoya, Wegmans, Kleysen, Penske, and UPS

exemplified how a positive culture for change enables successful technology integration, regardless of the powertrain selected.

## 7.2 Technology Decision Framework 

The following framework — informed by NACFE's 2023 Framework for Decision Making — summarizes where each powertrain technology demonstrated strengths and limitations in this Run [1]. No single technology optimizes across all applications; the goal is matching technology characteristics to operational requirements.

### 7.2.1 Diesel, Renewable Diesel & Biodiesel

During the Run diesel-powered trucks achieved 9 to 11.8 MPG and operated between 500 and 1,175 miles daily. Infrastructure for diesel-powered trucks is readily available across the US and Canada.

Favorable applications for diesel-powered vehicles include the following:
* Long-haul operations requiring extended range and routing flexibility
* Mountain freight corridors with sustained grades
* Variable-demand operations requiring dispatch flexibility
* Heavy-payload applications

Factors that need to be considered when choosing diesel-powered vehicles include the following.
* Emissions sensitivity — burning diesel brings with it NOx, particulate and carbon emissions
* Renewable diesel provides 50% to 80% carbon intensity reduction with no operational changes
* Fuel price volatility affects operating economics
* Regulatory requirements vary by state and are evolving
* Operational optimization (aerodynamics, driver training, speed management) influences efficiency

### 7.2.2 CNG/RNG

During the Run trucks powered by CNG/RNG achieved 4.5 to 6.8 MPGe and traveled between 400 and 700 miles daily. These vehicles can be refueled rapidly in 10 to 20 minutes.

Favorable applications for CNG/RNG vehicles include the following.
* Low vehicle NOx, particulate or carbon emissions and net zero capability when using renewable sources
* Highway-dominant duty cycles (great than 80% highway miles)
* Regional operations with CNG infrastructure access
* Flat-to-moderate terrain corridors
* Operations with private fueling or reliable public station access

Factors that need to be considered when choosing CNG/RNG vehicles include the following.
* Urban operation showed significant efficiency degradation in this Run
* Infrastructure assessment needed before expanding to new corridors
* RNG provides additional carbon intensity reduction; can achieve carbon-negative operations
* Cummins X15N engine now provides diesel-competitive power for demanding applications

### 7.2.3 BEVs

During the Run BEVs achieved 0.44 to 0.71 mi/kWh and operated 170 to 875 miles daily depending on the platform, terrain and duty cycle. 

---

<!-- page: 255 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 255

Favorable applications for BEVs include the following.
* Zero vehicle NOx, particulate or carbon emissions
* Regional return-to-base operations with depot charging
* Flat-to-moderate terrain corridors (efficiency decreases significantly on grades greater than 3%)
* Terminal-to-terminal linehaul with predictable daily mileage
* Operations in emissions-regulated zones
* Corridors with established high-power charging infrastructure

Factors that need to be considered when choosing diesel-powered vehicles include the following.
* Terrain sensitivity requires corridor-specific range validation
* Charging infrastructure positioning is critical to operational success
* Battery weight (6,000 to 10,000 lbs.) reduces available freight capacity
* Cold weather affects range and efficiency (not captured in this September Run)
* Vendor ecosystem considerations vary by platform

### 7.2.4 Hydrogen Fuel Cell

During the Run hydrogen fuel cell trucks achieved 7.5 to 7.8 mi/kg and operated between 120 and 430 miles daily. Quick refueling time.

Observed Characteristics:
* Zero vehicle NOx, particulate or carbon emissions
* Quick refueling time compared to BEV charging
* Weight advantage over BEVs enables greater payload capacity
* Zero direct emissions at point of use
* Current platform (XCIENT) speed governed at 56 mph by operator choice

Factors that need to be considered when choosing hydrogen fuel cell vehicles include the following.
* Infrastructure scarcity (approximately 60 heavy-duty stations nationwide) severely limits deployment options
* High fuel costs ($15 to $25/kg) result in operating costs 3x to 5x higher than BEV per mile
* Energy pathway efficiency (25% to 40% well-to-wheel) compares unfavorably to BEV (85% to 90%)
* Hydrogen production pathway affects lifecycle emissions (gray hydrogen from natural gas has significant upstream emissions)
* Commercial-scale viability depends on infrastructure development and cost reductions not yet achieved
* Availability of long distance, on-highway vehicle specifications is still needed.

## 7.3 Deployment Readiness Assessment 

Before committing to alternative powertrain deployment, fleet operators should evaluate readiness across multiple dimensions. Figure 158 provides structured assessment approaches.

TCO analysis comparing powertrain technologies across various duty cycles will be addressed in a separate NACFE report.

### 7.3.1 Route & Corridor Assessment

Route characteristics fundamentally determine powertrain suitability. The Run on Less – Messy Middle data demonstrated that terrain, daily mileage patterns, and route structure create operational envelopes where specific technologies excel — and boundaries where they struggle to match conventional performance. Battery-electric efficiency varied by 50% to 70% across terrain profiles, while hydrogen operations required precise alignment between route patterns and station access. 

Before evaluating infrastructure investments or economic models, fleets must assess whether their actual routes create favorable conditions for alternative powertrains.

The following framework identifies the critical route characteristics that determine technology viability.
* Terrain profile: What percentage of route miles involve grades greater than 3%? Sustained grades significantly affect BEV range and efficiency.
* Daily mileage requirements: What is average and maximum daily mileage? How do requirements compare to technology range capabilities?

---

<!-- page: 257 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 257

* Route predictability: Are routes fixed or variable? Fixed routes enable infrastructure optimization; variable routes benefit from diesel flexibility.
* Speed requirements: What sustained speeds are needed? Platform capabilities vary.
* Infrastructure positioning: Are fueling/charging stations positioned appropriately along routes?

### 7.3.2 Infrastructure Assessment

Infrastructure availability determines operational viability as fundamentally as vehicle capability. The Run demonstrated that successful alternative powertrain deployments required precise alignment between route patterns and infrastructure positioning — Penske's hydrogen operation coordinated mobile refueling around fixed corridors, while BEV fleets like Saia and JoyRide structured operations around depot charging capabilities and en-route fast-charging access. Infrastructure gaps don't just create inconvenience; they impose hard constraints on route selection, daily mileage, and operational flexibility that fundamentally reshape how fleets can deploy vehicles.

Effective infrastructure assessment evaluates both current availability and realistic development timelines.
* Depot capability: What electrical capacity exists at facilities? Can it support planned fleet size?
* En-route access: Are public charging/fueling stations available along routes? What is station reliability?
* Backup options: What happens if primary fueling/charging location is unavailable?
* Infrastructure timeline: If infrastructure buildout is required, what is realistic timeline and cost?

### 7.3.3 Operational Readiness Assessment

Vehicle acquisition is only the beginning; sustained performance depends on the operational systems and workforce capabilities that support new technology in daily service. The following questions help fleets evaluate whether the people, processes, and support structures are in place to operate alternative powertrain vehicles reliably and efficiently from day one.
* Driver training: Are training programs in place for new technology? Include efficiency optimization and emergency protocols.
* Maintenance capability: Does the maintenance team have training and tools? Are service agreements in place?
* Dispatch adaptation: Can dispatch systems account for range constraints and charging/fueling requirements?
* Performance monitoring: What systems will track performance and inform optimization?
* Contingency planning: What happens when vehicles cannot complete assignments?

### 7.3.4 Economic Assessment

Economic viability extends beyond comparing vehicle purchase prices or fuel costs per gallon equivalent. The Run revealed that operational economics depend on the interplay among capital requirements, infrastructure investments, energy price volatility, utilization rates, and available incentives — factors that vary significantly across duty cycles and deployment scenarios. 

A BEV that achieves favorable economics in high-utilization, return-to-base service with depot charging may show entirely different results in lower-mileage applications requiring public charging infrastructure. Understanding these economic drivers before deployment prevents assumptions that look sound on spreadsheets but fail under operational reality.

---

<!-- page: 258 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 258

The following framework identifies the critical economic factors that determine deployment viability.
* Capital requirements: What are vehicle acquisition costs? Infrastructure investment requirements?
* Incentive availability: What federal, state, and utility incentives apply? What are eligibility requirements?
* Energy cost assumptions: What fuel/electricity prices are assumed? How sensitive is analysis to price variation?
* Utilization impact: Are utilization assumptions realistic given operational constraints?

Comprehensive TCO analysis across powertrain technologies and duty cycles will be addressed in a separate NACFE report.

### 7.3.5 Sustainability & Decarbonization Assessment

Sustainability objectives increasingly drive technology selection alongside operational and economic factors. The Run documented that fleets pursue alternative powertrains for varied reasons — organizational decarbonization commitments, customer sustainability requirements, regulatory compliance, and competitive positioning in emissions-regulated markets. 

The CNG/RNG fleets demonstrated that renewable natural gas could achieve carbon-negative lifecycle intensity when sourced from organic waste, illustrating that sustainability outcomes depend on fuel pathways as much as powertrain choice. Understanding which sustainability drivers apply to specific operations—and their timelines—helps fleets prioritize technology investments that align business objectives with decarbonization goals.

An effective sustainability assessment evaluates both voluntary commitments and regulatory requirements.
* Organizational sustainability goals: What emissions reduction targets exist? What timeline applies?
* Customer requirements: Do customers have sustainability requirements or preferences that affect carrier selection?
* Regulatory environment: What emissions regulations apply now? What regulations are anticipated? (e.g., California ACT rule, EPA Phase 3)
* Carbon intensity by pathway: What is lifecycle carbon intensity of each option? Consider fuel/electricity source.
* Near-term options: Can renewable diesel or RNG provide immediate carbon reduction while evaluating longer-term options?
* Reporting requirements: What emissions data collection and reporting is needed for sustainability programs?
* Local air quality: Are there specific requirements for operations in non-attainment areas or port zones?

Note: Detailed emissions analysis for the Run on Less – Messy Middle fleets will be addressed in a separate NACFE emissions report.

## 7.5 Conclusions 

Run on Less – Messy Middle documents how 13 fleets operating 14 trucks demonstrating four powertrain technologies in revenue freight service across multiple North American regions perform. The data confirm that freight decarbonization involves matching technologies to appropriate applications rather than finding a single solution for all duty cycles.

Diesel continues to serve as the operational benchmark for flexibility, daily productivity, and infrastructure independence. Fleets achieved up to 11.8 MPG — nearly double typical fleet averages — while covering 500 to 800+ miles daily across varied terrain. Renewable diesel provides an available pathway for carbon intensity reduction using existing equipment and infrastructure.

CNG and RNG serve specific applications well, particularly highway-dominant duty cycles with predictable infrastructure access. The Cummins X15N engine demonstrated diesel-competitive capability in demanding

---

<!-- page: 260 -->
Terrain, Technology, and Telematics: The Messy Middle Operations Report

March 15, 2026

Page 260

applications. RNG offers additional carbon reduction potential, including carbon-negative operations when sourced from organic waste.

Battery-electric technology demonstrated viability beyond regional applications in Run on Less – Messy Middle, with validated performance of 350 to 500+ mile daily operations and an 875-mile single-day maximum. Public BEV truck charging is evolving on key corridors. Terrain sensitivity and charging infrastructure requirements mean corridor-specific evaluation is essential. When charged with renewable electricity, BEVs can achieve near-zero lifecycle emissions.

Hydrogen fuel cells face significant challenges that constrain commercial-scale deployment. While the technology demonstrated certain operational characteristics (quick refueling, payload advantages), infrastructure scarcity, high fuel costs, and energy pathway efficiency concerns present substantial barriers. Green hydrogen production could enable near-zero emissions, but the timeline for addressing infrastructure and cost challenges remains uncertain.

Fleets navigating technology transitions benefit from:
1. Matching technology to duty cycle based on route characteristics, infrastructure access, and operational requirements
2. Conducting route-specific analysis before deployment, particularly for technologies sensitive to terrain
3. Evaluating infrastructure realistically including current availability, development timelines, and backup options
4. Investing in operational factors like driver training, maintenance capability, and dispatch optimization that affect performance across all technologies
5. Maintaining flexibility as technologies evolve, infrastructure develops, and costs change
6. Using operational data not just specifications to inform deployment decisions

The freight industry's path forward will likely involve different technologies serving different applications based on operational requirements, infrastructure availability, and sustainability goals. Run on Less – Messy Middle provides operational data to help inform those decisions.

# APPENDIX B – INFRASTRUCTURE REFERENCE

This appendix summarizes fueling and charging infrastructure relevant to the Run on Less – Messy Middle fleets.

**Diesel, Renewable Diesel and Biodiesel Infrastructure**
Diesel infrastructure is effectively ubiquitous across North America. Renewable diesel (R99/R100) uses existing diesel infrastructure without modification. Tens of thousands of truck stops, travel centers, and fueling stations provide nationwide coverage. Fleets operating diesel face no infrastructure constraints for route planning.

**CNG/RNG Station Network**
More than 700 public CNG stations operate in the US, with more than 1,200 total stations including private facilities. Station concentration is highest in California, Texas, and along major freight corridors.

The fleets in this study accessed infrastructure as follows:
* **Wegmans:** Commercial CNG fast-fill station (Beyond6) near Rochester distribution center
* **UPS Salt Lake:** Private CNG facility at Salt Lake City hub
* **Kleysen:** Public fast-fill CNG stations along Alberta corridors

**BEV Charging Infrastructure**
Battery-electric fleets in the Run relied on a combination of depot-based charging and public DC fast charging (DCFC) networks.

Charging infrastructure varied by fleet:
* **4Gen:** Depot DC fast charging plus corridor DCFC at port facilities and inland distribution centers
* **Nevoya:** Depot-based overnight charging with supplemental public DCFC
* **Saia:** Tesla proprietary charging at terminal facilities plus Megawatt charger network access
* **JoyRide:** Strategic corridor DCFC placement integrated with driver rest periods

**Hydrogen Refueling Infrastructure**
Hydrogen infrastructure for Class 8 trucks remains extremely limited, with deployments concentrated in California.

Both hydrogen fleets in this demonstration operated with dedicated fueling arrangements:
* **Penske:** Mobile 700-bar heavy-duty dispenser providing 10- to 12-minute refueling
* **Pilot:** Fixed 700-bar hydrogen station in California corridor network

# APPENDIX C – METHODOLOGY TECHNICAL DETAILS

This appendix provides extended technical detail on data collection, validation, and analytical methodologies used in Run on Less – Messy Middle.

**Telematics Data Collection**
Each participating tractor was equipped with a primary telematics system providing continuous duty-cycle data at 10-second recording intervals. This high-resolution capture produced approximately 155,520 data points per vehicle over the 18 days of the Run. Data fields captured included: timestamp, GPS coordinates, speed, distance, elevation, fuel/energy consumption, battery state-of-charge (BEV), hydrogen mass (FCEV), weight, and truck activity status.

**GPS Elevation Integration**
Terrain analysis required integration of GPS elevation data with Digital Elevation Model (DEM) sources. The methodology cross-referenced onboard barometric altitude with USGS DEM data to produce validated elevation profiles. Grade calculations used differential elevation over distance with smoothing algorithms to reduce GPS noise while preserving meaningful terrain features.

**Hydrogen Consumption Methodology**
Hydrogen fuel cell vehicles reported cumulative energy totals rather than incremental consumption. The analytical methodology applied differential calculations with reset detection to convert cumulative values to trip-level consumption. Usable hydrogen mass was verified at 68.6 kg based on actual tank capacity measurements rather than nominal specifications.

**BEV Energy Component Separation**
Battery-electric vehicles presented challenges in distinguishing driving energy, charging energy, regenerative recovery, and idle consumption. The methodology tracks each energy component separately: `driving_energy_kwh` (traction consumption), `energy_from_charge_kwh` (grid input), `energy_regen_kwh` (regenerative recovery), and `idle_energy_kwh` (stationary consumption). This enables analysis of both net efficiency (grid-to-wheel) and driving efficiency (battery-to-wheel).

**Validation Procedures**
Each dataset underwent multi-stage validation.
1. Automated screening for missing values, anomalies, timestamp gaps, and statistical outliers
2. Cross-validation between telematics calculations and fleet-provided dashboard data
3. GPS verification of distance against odometer readings
4. Energy reconciliation comparing calculated consumption against charging/fueling records
5. Payload verification through onboard sensors or dispatch documentation. The validation process achieved alignment within 1% to 5% between calculated metrics and fleet dashboard baselines.

# APPENDIX D – PREVIOUS RUN ON LESS COMPARISONS

This appendix provides historical context by comparing Run on Less – Messy Middle results with previous NACFE demonstrations.

> **Figure D — Run on Less Demonstration History**
> Type: photograph
> Axes: none vs none
> Series: Run on Less Demonstration History
> Values: none
> Notes: Table of historical Run on Less events, years, truck counts, total miles, technologies, and focus areas.

| Event | Year | Trucks | Total Miles | Technologies | Focus |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| RUN ON LESS 2017 | 2017 | 7 | 50,000+ | Diesel | Fuel efficiency best practices |
| RUN ON LESS REGIONAL | 2019 | 10 | 40,000+ | Diesel, CNG | Regional operations |
| RUN ON LESS ELECTRIC | 2021 | 13 | 100,000+ | BEV | Electric demonstration |
| RUN ON LESS ELECTRIC DEPOT | 2023 | 16 | 85,000+ | BEV (depot) | Depot charging operations |
| RUN ON LESS MESSY MIDDLE | 2025 | 14 | 73,000+ | Diesel, CNG, BEV, H₂FCEV | Multi-powertrain comparison |

**Diesel Efficiency Evolution**
Diesel fuel economy has improved significantly across Run on Less demonstrations. The 2017 demonstration established a 10.1 MPG fleet average for optimized long-haul operations [143]. By 2025, leading fleets like Albert Transport achieved 11.8 MPG, representing continued improvement through aerodynamics, powertrain optimization, and operational discipline. Industry-average fuel economy has improved from 5.85 MPG (2013) to 6.91 MPG (2022) according to FHWA data, indicating that demonstration fleets continue to operate at roughly 1.7x industry average efficiency [144].

**BEV Technology Progression**
Battery electric vehicle technology has progressed substantially between Run on Less – Electric in 2021 and Run on Less – Messy Middle. Key advances include battery capacity growth from 264 to 396 kWh (2021) to 438 to 900+ kWh (2025); daily range extension from 100 to 200 miles typical to 200 to 500+ miles achievable; charging infrastructure expansion from depot-only to corridor DC fast charging networks; and demonstrated long-haul capability including an 875-mile validated maximum single-day distance.

**Technology Readiness Assessment**
Based on all Run on Less demonstrations from 2017 to 2025, technology readiness varies by application. Diesel remains the most operationally mature technology with no range or infrastructure constraints. CNG/RNG has proven viable for regional and corridor operations where infrastructure exists. BEV technology has advanced from demonstration-only (2021) to commercial viability for drayage, regional, and emerging long-haul applications (2025). Hydrogen FCEV remains in early commercialization with infrastructure availability as the primary constraint rather than vehicle capability.
